\subsection{Relation to Part II and to the rest of the collection}
\label{hset:zr:sub:relation}

\begin{remark}[The weak-base form of Theorem~\ref{hset:thm:eternity-replacement}]
\label{hset:zr:rem:eternity}
Source 10 did not know this report. Its spine is the same principle as
\cref{hset:thm:eternity-replacement} (source 09's): \emph{Replacement is exactly
the bounding of birthdays of definable families of surreals}. The two
statements live on different bases, and neither contains the other.
\begin{itemize}
\item \Cref{hset:thm:eternity-replacement} is a theorem of ZFC about two set
structures at an uncountable cardinal $\kappa$: the eternity scheme $\Et$
(\cref{hset:sec:growth}), a scheme of the birthday-field language evaluated in
$\B_\kappa$ for families indexed by embedded ordinals, holds iff the
hereditary-size universe $H_\kappa$ satisfies Replacement. Its proof uses the
bi-interpretation of \cref{hset:part:spine} and, in the converse direction,
ambient Replacement and Choice.
\item Source 10's \cref{hset:zr:thm:equivalence,hset:zr:thm:ranked} are
internal scheme equivalences over the weak theory $\Zf$ in the ordinary
membership language, with sign sequences as sets and only their order used.
Over $\Zf$ alone birthday bounding is ordinal bounding $\OB$; it becomes full
Replacement (and Collection) only over $\Zf+\Hier$, and no Choice is used.
\Cref{hset:zr:cor:individual} is the per-family form, the counterpart of a
single instance of $\Et$. New beside $\Et$ is $\NIB$: a strict bound can
always be sought inside the one interval $(-1,0)$.
\item The bases differ: $H_\kappa$ need not satisfy Power Set
(\cref{hset:lem:H-background} does not list it), which $\Zf$ contains, and
$\Hier$ can fail in $H_\kappa$. Where $H_\kappa$ does satisfy $\Zf+\Hier$, for
example at an uncountable $\kappa$ with $\kappa=\beth_\kappa$, where
$H_\kappa=V_\kappa$, \cref{hset:zr:thm:ranked} applies inside $H_\kappa$, and
together with \cref{hset:thm:eternity-replacement} it gives: $\B_\kappa\models\Et$
iff $H_\kappa$ satisfies any one of the six schemes of
\cref{hset:zr:thm:equivalence}, read in $H_\kappa$. This combination is an
observation of this merge, not a statement of either source.
\end{itemize}
Source 10's $V_{\omega+\omega}$ countermodel (\cref{hset:zr:prop:rankmodel})
has the same shape as the failures recorded in \cref{hset:sec:growth}: every
member of a definable family exists, but the family cannot be collected; compare
the $\beth_\omega$ instance $\RepB$ (\cref{hset:thm:beth}) and the cofinal
power-tower sequence at $\varepsilon_0$ that defeats $\Et$. Source 10's
Replacement-free set-cut theorem (\cref{hset:zr:thm:setcut}) is a different
statement from the Replacement-free arithmetic certificates of
\cref{hset:sec:certificates}: the latter compute field operations inside
$H_\kappa$, the former constructs the simplest separator of a set cut from the
order alone.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The same Collection spectrum in two collections]
\label{hset:zr:rem:nee}
Batch-89 manuscript 05, placed as the research-report collection's new report
\repo{SetTheory/Cardinals/docs/reports/ordinals-and-order-types/naming-elementary-embeddings}
(\emph{Naming Elementary Embeddings: Atom Components, Replacement, and
Reflection}, pinned to the same commit; AI-assisted, unrefereed, not
formalized), works with the same kernel models, written $M_\kappa(A)$ there
after Yao's ideal construction. Its lemma ``No new atoms in a unique
base-language output'' (Lemma~2.3, \lbl{nee:lem:nonew}) and its ``Known kernel-ideal
theorem, specialized'' (Theorem~2.4, \lbl{nee:thm:base}) are \cref{hset:zr:lem:confinement}
and \cref{hset:zr:thm:supportbase}. Its lemmas ``Automatic small-domain
Collection'' (Lemma~7.1, \lbl{nee:lem:smallcoll}) and ``The limit-cutoff obstruction is
already bounded'' (Lemma~7.2, \lbl{nee:lem:limitcoll}), with its corollary ``Exact local
spectrum at limit cutoffs'' (Corollary~7.3, \lbl{nee:cor:limitspectrum}), prove
\cref{hset:zr:lem:smallcollection,hset:zr:thm:limitspectrum} for the same models
and for their expansions by named atom injections, with the added information
that the failing instance at a limit cardinal has a $\Delta_0$ matrix. Its
``Full Collection criterion'' (Theorem~7.5, \lbl{nee:thm:collection}) proves full Collection
at successor cardinals, as in \cref{hset:zr:thm:successorspectrum}, by a
reservoir argument for named tuples. The two manuscripts were written
independently on the same pin; neither is claimed first, and both credit the
mechanism to Hamkins--Yao, L\'evy, Glazer--Yao and Yao. This part prints source
10's proofs. That report's dated note after its Corollary~7.6 compares the two:
its failing instance at a limit cardinal collects injection graphs with a
$\Delta_0$ matrix, where source 10 collects sets of atoms of prescribed
cardinalities, and its successor-case reservoir consists of whole components,
so that the permutations commute with the named maps. Source 10's enumeration predicate $E$
(\cref{hset:zr:sec:expansion}) and that report's named lifts of atom injections
are different expansions of the same models, and their theorems are different.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Full-class rank models]
\label{hset:zr:rem:swo}
Batch-89 manuscript 01, placed as Part~XV of the report
\repo{foundations-and-computation/surreal-well-orders} (its source 31), computes
for the two-sorted full-class rank models $(V_\lambda,\Pow(V_\lambda))$ at every
limit $\lambda>\omega$ the domains on which class Replacement and Collection
hold: exactly those of size below $\cf\lambda$. At $\lambda=\omega+\omega$ this
contains the failure of \cref{hset:zr:prop:rankmodel} for the one
\emph{definable} family $n\mapsto\omega+n$. The failure of $\NIB$ there, and the
weak-base equivalences, are source 10's own.
\end{remark}

\section{The base theories and the meaning of a family}
\label{hset:zr:sec:base}

\subsection{Zermelo set theory without hidden Replacement}
\begin{definition}
$\Zf$ consists of Extensionality, Empty Set, Pairing, Union, Power Set, Infinity, the full Separation scheme, and Foundation, in classical first-order logic. It does not contain Replacement or Choice.
\end{definition}
We use ordinary set encodings of ordered pairs and functions. The usual finite constructions---Cartesian products, restrictions, domains and ranges of \emph{set relations}, and finite tagged unions---are available in this theory. For example, $X\times Y$ is obtained by Separation from a sufficiently iterated power set of $X\cup Y$. No image of an arbitrary definable class function is presupposed.

All appearances of a class in the weak-base half of source 10 abbreviate a first-order formula with finitely many set parameters. An assertion quantified over formulas is an external scheme: there is one first-order assertion for each formula. There is no set of all formulas together with an unrestricted truth predicate for the universe, and no quantification over arbitrary subclasses of a proper class.

\begin{definition}[Definable set-indexed family]
A formula $\varphi(i,s,p)$ presents a surreal family on a set $I$ if
\[
\forall i\in I\;\exists!s\,[s\in\No\land\varphi(i,s,p)].
\]
We write $s_i$ for its unique value. This notation does not assert that $\{(i,s_i):i\in I\}$ is a set.
\end{definition}
The distinction is essential. If a family is given \emph{as a set function}, its range is a set in $\Zf$. If it is only given by a formula satisfying the displayed condition, forming its range is a Replacement-type step.

\subsection{A separately named hierarchy hypothesis}
\begin{definition}[The hierarchy axiom $\Hier$]
The following two assertions constitute $\Hier$.
First, for every ordinal $\alpha$ there is a set function $h$ with domain $\alpha+1$ satisfying
\[
h(0)=\varnothing,\qquad h(\zeta+1)=\Pow(h(\zeta)),\qquad
h(\lambda)=\bigcup_{\zeta<\lambda}h(\zeta)
\]
for the relevant successor and nonzero limit indices. Second, every set $x$ belongs to $h(\alpha)$ for some such $\alpha,h$.
\end{definition}
The unions in this definition are unions of ranges of restrictions of a \emph{set function}. Uniqueness and coherence of the hierarchy segments follow by induction along an ordinal. Consequently $V_\alpha$ is unambiguously definable under $\Hier$.

ZF proves $\Hier$ by its usual recursion and rank constructions. We do not assume that $\Hier$ follows from $\Zf$. Our characterization over plain $\Zf$ is a characterization of ordinal bounding; only after adjoining $\Hier$ do we identify it with full Replacement. The model in \cref{hset:zr:sec:countermodel} satisfies $\Zf+\Hier$ but not Replacement, so $\Hier$ itself does not trivialize the result.

\subsection{Two different ambient settings}
The first half of source 10 concerns pure set theory and the sign-sequence order. The later support-model construction takes place externally in a full well-founded universe with atoms and Choice. We write $\ZFUR$ for urelement set theory formulated with Replacement, rather than building Collection into its name. This prevents an ambiguity in the literature: some authors use ``ZFU'' for a theory already containing Collection.

In the atom setting, Extensionality applies to sets, and atoms have no members. A tagged interpretation in ordinary ZFC can be used to implement the background urelement universe. No new large-cardinal assumption is needed for the constructions here. When a class model is mentioned, satisfaction is used formula by formula through relativization; a universal class truth predicate is not introduced.

\section{Sign sequences and bounded containers}
\label{hset:zr:sec:signs}

\begin{definition}
A surreal sign sequence is a function $s:\alpha\to\signs$ for an ordinal $\alpha$. Its birthday is $\bd(s)=\alpha$. The empty sequence is $0$, and the one-sign sequence $(-)$ is denoted by $-1$.
\end{definition}
We distinguish the two signs from the numerical values of these named surreals. For comparison, use the order
\[
-\;<\;\text{termination}\;<\;+.
\]
Two unequal sequences are compared at the first position where their signs differ or where precisely one terminates. Such a position exists and has a least ordinal index. This defines a linear order. Write $p\pref s$ if $p$ is an initial segment of $s$, possibly equal to $s$.

Only this ordered tree is needed. We do not invoke the recursive definitions of surreal addition, multiplication, exponentiation, or real closedness in the weak base.

\begin{lemma}[Bounded sign container]
\label{hset:zr:lem:container}
For every ordinal $\sigma$, the class
\[
T_\sigma=\{s\in\No:\bd(s)\leq\sigma\}
\]
is a set in $\Zf$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Every such function is a subset of $\sigma\times\signs$. Separate from $\Pow(\sigma\times\signs)$ the graphs which are functions whose domains are ordinals. These domains are subsets of $\sigma$, hence are at most $\sigma$. Conversely every sign sequence of length at most $\sigma$ occurs. This uses Power Set and Separation, not Replacement.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Set families have bounded birthdays]
\label{hset:zr:lem:setbounds}
If $S$ is a set of sign sequences, there is an ordinal $\gamma$ with $\bd(s)\leq\gamma$ for every $s\in S$. Moreover,
\[
\lambda=\sup\{\bd(s)+1:s\in S\}
\]
is an ordinal available in $\Zf$, and $\bd(s)<\lambda$ for all $s\in S$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The union $\bigcup S$ is a set relation, so its domain exists. This domain is the union of the ordinal domains of the members of $S$, and is therefore an ordinal $\gamma$. Every $\bd(s)$ is at most $\gamma$. To avoid assuming that the displayed successor image is a set, define directly
\[
\lambda=\{\xi\in\gamma+1:\exists s\in S\ (\xi\leq\bd(s))\}.
\]
This is a downward-closed set of ordinals, equals the indicated supremum, and has the desired strict bound. When $S$ is empty, it is $0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[All-plus ordinal codes]
\label{hset:zr:lem:ordcode}
For an ordinal $\alpha$, let $o_\alpha$ be the all-plus sequence of length $\alpha$. Then $\alpha<\zeta$ if and only if $o_\alpha<o_\zeta$. For every sign sequence $s$,
\[
s\leq o_{\bd(s)}<o_{\bd(s)+1}.
\]
In particular, $o_\alpha<s$ implies $\alpha<\bd(s)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
These comparisons follow at the first negative sign, or at the first termination position when no earlier negative sign occurs. Each code is constructed directly as a constant function on its given ordinal. There is no appeal to a set containing all ordinal codes.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[One bounded interval contains an ordinal detector]
\label{hset:zr:lem:negativecode}
For each ordinal $\alpha$, define $\nu_\alpha$ on $\alpha+2$ by
\[
\nu_\alpha(\xi)=
\begin{cases}
-,&\xi=0,\\
+,&0<\xi<\alpha+2.
\end{cases}
\]
Then
\[
-1<\nu_\alpha<0,\qquad
\alpha<\zeta\Longleftrightarrow\nu_\alpha<\nu_\zeta.
\]
For every negative sign sequence $c$ of birthday $\sigma$,
\[
c<\nu_\sigma.
\]
Consequently $\nu_\alpha<c<0$ implies $\alpha<\bd(c)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The first sign makes the sequence negative, and the plus at position $1$ makes it greater than $-1$. Adding more trailing plus signs increases its value. Right addition by the finite ordinal $2$ is strictly increasing, giving the equivalence involving $\alpha$ and $\zeta$.

A negative $c$ has a minus at position $0$. If it has another minus, then $\nu_\sigma$ is larger at the first such position. If it has only plus signs after position $0$, then it is a proper initial segment of $\nu_\sigma$ and the latter has a plus at its termination position. Thus $c<\nu_\sigma$. If $\alpha\geq\sigma$, monotonicity would give $\nu_\alpha\geq\nu_\sigma>c$, proving the last assertion.
\end{proof}

\section{Actual set cuts: a Replacement-free simplicity proof}
\label{hset:zr:sec:setcuts}

\begin{theorem}[Set-cut interpolation with a birthday bound]
\label{hset:zr:thm:setcut}
In $\Zf$, let $L,R$ be sets of sign sequences with $L<R$, meaning $l<r$ for every $l\in L$ and $r\in R$. Empty sides are allowed. There is a unique simplest separator $c$ satisfying
\[
L<c<R.
\]
Every separator extends $c$ as a sign sequence. In particular, $c$ is the unique separator of least birthday, and
\[
\bd(c)\leq\lambda,
\qquad
\lambda=\sup\{\bd(x)+1:x\in L\cup R\}.
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{hset:zr:lem:setbounds}, $\lambda$ is a set ordinal, and every endpoint has birthday below $\lambda$. The proof takes place inside the set $T_\lambda$ of \cref{hset:zr:lem:container}.

For a prefix $p$, say it has a left obstruction if some $l\in L$ satisfies $p\leq l$, and a right obstruction if some $r\in R$ satisfies $r\leq p$. The two obstructions cannot occur together: they would yield $r\leq p\leq l<r$. If neither occurs, $p$ is a separator. Otherwise prescribe the next sign to be plus for a left obstruction and minus for a right obstruction.

Call $p\in T_\lambda$ a \emph{good path} if, for every $\zeta<\bd(p)$, the restriction $p\restr\zeta$ is not yet a separator and the sign $p(\zeta)$ is the prescribed sign at that restriction. Good paths form a set $G$ by Separation. The empty path belongs to $G$.

\emph{Compatibility.} Any two good paths are compatible. At a first disagreement they would have the same earlier prefix but different prescribed signs, contrary to the rule's uniqueness. Hence one extends the other.

The union $p_* = \bigcup G$ is therefore a sign sequence of length at most $\lambda$. It is itself good: each of its sign positions already belongs to some good path, whose earlier restrictions coincide with its own. Thus $p_*\in G$.

We verify the invariant
\begin{align}
\label{hset:zr:eq:invariant}
l\in L,\ p\not\pref l&\ \Longrightarrow\ l<p,\\
\notag
r\in R,\ p\not\pref r&\ \Longrightarrow\ p<r
\end{align}
for every good path $p$. It is vacuous at the empty path. Suppose the prescribed extension is $p^+$, obtained by appending plus. Any left endpoint not extending $p^+$ either was already separated from $p$, equals $p$, or takes minus at the next position; in every case it is below $p^+$. A left obstruction ensures some $l\geq p$, and all right endpoints exceed this $l$. Therefore a right endpoint that still extends $p$ cannot equal $p$ or take minus next. Right endpoints not extending $p$ remain separated at their earlier comparison position. This proves the invariant for $p^+$. The minus case is symmetric.

At a limit path, an endpoint not extending that path already fails to extend some shorter prefix: use a position past its first disagreement or its termination. The invariant at that prefix persists in all later extensions. Induction along the set ordinal $\lambda+1$ proves \eqref{hset:zr:eq:invariant}.

If $\bd(p_*)<\lambda$ and $p_*$ were not a separator, appending its prescribed sign would yield a good path longer than the union of all good paths, a contradiction. If $\bd(p_*)=\lambda$, no endpoint can extend $p_*$, since every endpoint has smaller birthday. The invariant then says that $p_*$ is a separator. Set $c=p_*$.

Finally let $q$ be any separator. Induction along a good path shows that $q$ extends that path. At a plus step, an extension of the current prefix that terminates or takes minus is at most the prefix and hence at most the obstructing left endpoint; it cannot be $q$. The minus step is symmetric. At limits, extension follows by union. Thus $q$ extends $c$. Since $c$ itself separates, it is the unique separator of least birthday.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why this is not an invocation of class recursion]
The proof does not first build a class-length recursion and then take its image. It separates \emph{all good approximations from one set container} and takes their union. Every induction is along a given set ordinal. The set-existence steps are precisely those available in $\Zf$.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Sharpness]
\label{hset:zr:prop:sharp}
The birthday bound in \cref{hset:zr:thm:setcut} cannot be uniformly lowered. For a nonzero successor $\lambda=\zeta+1$, the cut $(\{o_\zeta\},\varnothing)$ has simplest separator $o_\lambda$. For a nonzero limit $\lambda$, the cut
\[
L=\{o_\zeta:\zeta<\lambda\},\qquad R=\varnothing
\]
has simplest separator $o_\lambda$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
In the successor case, a separator of birthday at most $\zeta$ is excluded by \cref{hset:zr:lem:ordcode}, and $o_{\zeta+1}$ works. In the limit case, $L$ is a set by Separation in $T_\lambda$; it is not being formed by an unbounded image operation. Any separator $c$ must have birthday at least $\lambda$, because $o_\zeta<c$ gives $\zeta<\bd(c)$ for every $\zeta<\lambda$. Again $o_\lambda$ works. The empty cut has simplest separator $0$ and bound $0$.
\end{proof}

\section{The equivalence theorem for definable surreal families}
\label{hset:zr:sec:equivalence}

\subsection{Six schemes}
All formulas below may have arbitrary set parameters in the language under consideration.

\begin{definition}
The schemes are as follows.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item $\OB$: every definable function $i\mapsto\alpha_i$ from a set into the ordinals has an ordinal $\gamma$ such that $\alpha_i<\gamma$ for all indices.
\item $\Gr$: every definable set-indexed surreal family has a set graph.
\item $\UB$: every definable set-indexed surreal family has a strict surreal upper bound.
\item $\SC$: if $I$ is a set and $\forall i\in I\,\exists s\in\No\,\varphi(i,s,p)$, there is a set $S\subseteq\No$ with $\forall i\in I\,\exists s\in S\,\varphi(i,s,p)$.
\item $\DCI$: if two definable families $(l_i)_{i\in I}$ and $(r_j)_{j\in J}$ have set index domains and satisfy $l_i<r_j$ for all $i,j$, there is a surreal $c$ with $l_i<c<r_j$ for all $i,j$.
\item $\NIB$: every definable set-indexed family of elements of $(-1,0)$ has a strict upper bound that also lies in $(-1,0)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}
The right bound $0$ is not an acceptable witness to $\NIB$. This is why the last scheme is stronger than the tautological assertion that a family in $(-1,0)$ is bounded in $\No$.

\begin{theorem}[Exact weak-base equivalence]
\label{hset:zr:thm:equivalence}
Over $\Zf$,
\[
\boxed{\quad
\OB\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \Gr\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \UB
\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \SC\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \DCI
\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \NIB.\quad}
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\emph{$\OB\Rightarrow\Gr$.} Given a definable family $s_i$, its birthdays are uniquely defined ordinals. Choose $\gamma$ bounding them. Every value lies in the set $T_\gamma$. The graph is now a subset of $I\times T_\gamma$, defined by the original formula, and exists by Separation.

\emph{$\Gr\Rightarrow\UB$.} The range of the set graph is a set $S$. By \cref{hset:zr:lem:setbounds}, all birthdays are at most an ordinal $\gamma$. The all-plus sequence $o_{\gamma+1}$ strictly bounds every member of $S$.

\emph{$\UB\Rightarrow\OB$.} Apply $\UB$ to the definable family $o_{\alpha_i}$. If $c$ strictly bounds it, \cref{hset:zr:lem:ordcode} gives $\alpha_i<\bd(c)$ for every $i$.

\emph{$\OB\Rightarrow\SC$.} For each $i$, let $\alpha_i$ be the least birthday of a surreal witness to $\varphi(i,s,p)$. The least ordinal exists without a choice of witnesses for the whole family: for each fixed $i$, one witness supplies an ordinal interval in which Separation and well-ordering locate a minimum. The definition of $\alpha_i$ is functional, so $\OB$ gives $\alpha_i<\gamma$ uniformly. The set $T_\gamma$ is then a collecting set of possible witnesses. If desired, separate from it the witnesses relevant to at least one index. No choice function selecting a particular witness is needed.

\emph{$\SC\Rightarrow\Gr$.} Apply $\SC$ to a functional surreal family. Its unique values all lie in a set $S$. Separate the graph inside $I\times S$.

\emph{$\Gr\Rightarrow\DCI$.} Obtain set graphs and set ranges for the two families. Apply \cref{hset:zr:thm:setcut} to these actual sets.

\emph{$\DCI\Rightarrow\NIB$.} For a nonempty family in $(-1,0)$, use it as the left family and use the constant right family $\{0\}$. Any separator is automatically greater than $-1$. For an empty family, $\nu_0$ works. Equivalently one can adjoin $-1$ to the left family using a finite tagged union of index sets.

\emph{$\NIB\Rightarrow\OB$.} Given ordinal values $\alpha_i$, apply $\NIB$ to $\nu_{\alpha_i}$. Its negative upper bound $c$ yields $\alpha_i<\bd(c)$ by \cref{hset:zr:lem:negativecode}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The obstruction for an individual family]
\label{hset:zr:cor:individual}
For a given definable set-indexed surreal family, the following are equivalent in $\Zf$: its graph is a set; its range is contained in a set of sign sequences; and its birthdays admit an ordinal bound. Mere numerical boundedness of its values is not equivalent to these conditions.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A set graph has a set range and hence bounded birthdays. A birthday bound provides the container needed to separate the graph. The counterexample to the last possible equivalence is given in \cref{hset:zr:sec:countermodel}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Restricted domains and restricted formulas]
The proof preserves the index domain in most implications. The passage from a negative interval to a cut uses only a singleton right side. Thus the ordinal-detector implications already hold for a fixed domain such as $\omega$. One must not conclude that the $\omega$-restricted scheme implies Replacement on \emph{all} domains. Similarly, the transformations of formulas are explicit, but this article does not assert optimal preservation of their position in the L\'evy hierarchy.
\end{remark}

\section{When the test becomes full Replacement}
\label{hset:zr:sec:ranked}

\begin{theorem}[Ranked-base characterization]
\label{hset:zr:thm:ranked}
Over $\Zf+\Hier$, each of the six schemes in \cref{hset:zr:thm:equivalence} is equivalent to full Replacement and to full Collection. No Choice is required.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Replacement forms the set of ordinal outputs of a definable function and therefore gives $\OB$.

Conversely assume $\OB$ and consider a total relation
\[
\forall i\in I\;\exists y\,\varphi(i,y,p).
\]
By $\Hier$, for each $i$ there is some ordinal $\alpha$ such that a suitable witness lies in $V_\alpha$. Define
\[
\alpha_i=\min\{\alpha:\exists y\in V_\alpha\,\varphi(i,y,p)\}.
\]
This is a uniquely defined ordinal, even when the witness is not unique. As before, a least element of the nonempty ordinal class is obtained by bounding the minimization with one witness and using Separation. By $\OB$, some $\gamma$ bounds all $\alpha_i$. Monotonicity of the cumulative hierarchy shows that $V_\gamma$ collects witnesses for every $i$. This proves Collection.

Finally, for a functional relation, Collection puts the unique outputs inside a set, and Separation removes extraneous members and forms the graph. Thus Collection implies Replacement. Combining with \cref{hset:zr:thm:equivalence} proves the assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{resultbox}
\textbf{The precise conclusion.} Actual set-cut interpolation is a theorem of $\Zf$. Definable set-indexed cut interpolation is equivalent to ordinal bounding over $\Zf$. It is equivalent to full Replacement over the \emph{explicitly stronger base} $\Zf+\Hier$.
\end{resultbox}

This is an exact diagnosis of a common informal shortcut. The expression $\{s_i:i\in I\}$ cannot be inserted into the ordinary cut construction until its existence as a set has been justified. In a rank-complete pure universe, justifying that step for all formulas has the full strength of Replacement.

\section{An explicit omitted cut in a rank-initial universe}
\label{hset:zr:sec:countermodel}

We now work externally in ZFC. Let
\[
\theta=\omega+\omega,\qquad\text{and consider the structure }(V_\theta,\in).
\]
All assertions about $V_\theta$ below refer to its internal membership structure. In particular, ``a set'' in $V_\theta$ means an element of $V_\theta$.

\begin{proposition}
\label{hset:zr:prop:rankmodel}
$V_\theta$ satisfies $\Zf+\Hier$ and Choice, but fails $\OB$, Replacement, and $\NIB$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The ordinal $\theta$ is a limit and is closed below itself under adding any fixed finite ordinal. Pairing, Union, and Power Set increase the ranks of the finitely many input objects by only a finite amount, so their required outputs remain below $\theta$. Every subset of an element of $V_\theta$ is again in $V_\theta$, giving Separation for formulas interpreted in $V_\theta$. Infinity and Foundation hold, and Extensionality is inherited from transitivity.

For every $\alpha<\theta$, the hierarchy segment $\seq{V_\zeta:\zeta\leq\alpha}$ has rank at most $\alpha$ plus a fixed finite coding overhead, and therefore belongs to $V_\theta$. Every member of $V_\theta$ belongs to a sufficiently high stage below $\theta$. This gives $\Hier$. An ambient well-ordering of any $x\in V_\theta$ is a relation on $x$ of rank bounded by the rank of $x$ plus a finite amount, so it also belongs to $V_\theta$. Hence $V_\theta$ satisfies Choice.

The formula $n\mapsto\omega+n$, for $n\in\omega$, defines a total ordinal-valued family in $V_\theta$. Every value exists there, but these values are cofinal in $\theta=\Ord^{V_\theta}$. They have no internal ordinal bound, so $\OB$ fails. Replacement fails as well.

Finally define $s_n=\nu_{\omega+n}$. Each individual sign sequence has birthday $\omega+n+2$ and, with ordinary pair coding, rank below $\theta$. Thus it belongs to $V_\theta$, and $V_\theta$ recognizes
\[
\forall n\in\omega\quad -1<s_n<0.
\]
Given any negative $c\in\No^{V_\theta}$, its birthday $\sigma$ is below $\theta$. Choose $n$ with $\omega+n\geq\sigma$. By \cref{hset:zr:lem:negativecode},
\[
c<\nu_\sigma\leq\nu_{\omega+n}=s_n.
\]
There is therefore no negative strict upper bound for this definable family. This refutes $\NIB$ and presents an omitted definable cut with right side $\{0\}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What is and is not missing]
The family is externally countable. Internally, however, it is only a formula assigning one existing object to each natural number; its graph is not an element of $V_\theta$. Every \emph{actual set cut} in $V_\theta$ still has an interpolant by \cref{hset:zr:thm:setcut}. Also, $0$ is an upper bound for the displayed family. What fails is an upper bound \emph{strictly below $0$}, not ordinary numerical boundedness.
\end{remark}

This countermodel shows that the distinction survives Choice, the existence of all internal hierarchy stages, and the existence of each individual member of the family. The omitted resource is the ability to collect the entire definable family into one set.

\noindent[Added 4 October 2026, batch~89: the class form of this failure at
every limit height, recorded in \cref{hset:zr:rem:swo}. Part~XV of
\repo{foundations-and-computation/surreal-well-orders} (its source~31, batch-89
manuscript~01) uses the same witness $n\mapsto\omega+n$ at
$\lambda=\omega+\omega$ (its Section~XV.11, \lbl{swo:gcz:sec:examples}). In the
two-sorted full-class rank model $(V_\lambda,\Pow(V_\lambda))$, for every limit
$\lambda>\omega$, class Replacement and class Collection on an internal set $a$
hold exactly when $\card{a}<\cf\lambda$ (its Lemma~XV.9.2,
\lbl{swo:gcz:lem:domain}), and full class Replacement holds exactly when
$\lambda$ is strongly inaccessible (its Theorem~XV.9.3,
\lbl{swo:gcz:thm:replacement}). The failure of Replacement above is the
definable case at a height of cofinality $\omega$; the failure of $\NIB$ is
source~10's own. Neither manuscript cites the other.]

\section{Urelements: rank bounds versus atom-base bounds}
\label{hset:zr:sec:tworesources}

For an object $x$ in a full well-founded urelement universe, its kernel $\kerat(x)$ is the set of atoms occurring in the transitive closure of $\{x\}$. An object is pure if its kernel is empty. For a set $B$ of atoms define the hierarchy
\[
V_0(B)=B,\qquad V_{\alpha+1}(B)=\Pow(V_\alpha(B))\cup B,
\]
with unions at nonzero limit stages, and put $V(B)=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\Ord}V_\alpha(B)$ as a class abbreviation. An object lies in $V(B)$ exactly when its kernel is contained in $B$.

There are now two independent resources: the height $\alpha$ and the atom base $B$. In the pure case the base is always empty. With atoms, an ordinal bound on heights need not control the supports of witnesses.

\begin{proposition}[A two-resource witness criterion]
\label{hset:zr:prop:tworesources}
Suppose the required set hierarchies over atom bases and kernels exist. For a total relation on a set $I$, the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item there is a set collecting witnesses;
\item there are a set $B$ of atoms and an ordinal $\gamma$ such that every index has a witness in $V_\gamma(B)$.
\end{enumerate}
If ordinal bounding is available, condition (ii) is equivalent to the existence of a single atom base $B$ such that every index has some witness in $V(B)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
A collecting set $W$ has a kernel $B=\kerat(W)$ and belongs to some $V_\eta(B)$. Its members then all lie in a sufficiently high common stage over $B$, giving (ii). Conversely $V_\gamma(B)$ itself is a collecting set.

For the last assertion, fix a common base $B$ and assign to each index the least ordinal stage over $B$ containing a witness. This assignment is functional. Ordinal bounding bounds all its values, producing a common $\gamma$. The reverse implication is immediate.
\end{proof}

In $\ZFUR$, Replacement supplies ordinal bounding. The resulting common-base characterization is precisely the mechanism highlighted by Glazer--Yao, Proposition 1 \cite{GlazerYao}. Our use of it is to separate what a pure surreal detector can see from what it cannot: birthdays measure ordinal height, whereas pure sign sequences carry no atom kernel at all.

\section{Cardinal-support models and their basic axioms}
\label{hset:zr:sec:supportmodels}

Fix a full urelement universe $\Uk$ with Choice, containing a \emph{set} $A$ of all its atoms. Let $\kappa$ be an infinite cardinal with $|A|\geq\kappa$, with cardinalities computed in $\Uk$. Define the class
\[
\Mker_\kappa=\{x\in\Uk:|\kerat(x)|<\kappa\}.
\]
It contains every atom individually and every pure set, but does not contain the set of all atoms. Thus its atoms form a proper class \emph{internally}, even though $A$ is a set in the ambient universe.

The restriction is on kernel cardinality, not on the cardinality or rank of the whole object. For a permitted base $B$, every stage $V_\alpha(B)$, at every ordinal height, belongs to $\Mker_\kappa$.

\begin{lemma}[Kernel confinement of a unique output]
\label{hset:zr:lem:confinement}
Suppose $w,p\in \Mker_\kappa$, $x\in w$, and a formula has a unique output $y\in \Mker_\kappa$ for $x,p$, when interpreted in $\Mker_\kappa$. Then
\[
\kerat(y)\subseteq K,
\qquad K=\kerat(w)\cup\kerat(p).
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Every permutation of $A$ extends by rank recursion to an automorphism of $\Uk$ and preserves $\Mker_\kappa$, because it preserves kernel cardinalities. Suppose $a\in\kerat(y)\setminus K$. As $\kappa$ is infinite, the union $K\cup\kerat(y)$ has size below $\kappa$. Choose an atom $b$ outside this union. The transposition swapping $a$ and $b$ fixes $K$ pointwise, hence fixes $x$ and $p$. It moves $\kerat(y)$ and therefore moves $y$.

The image of $y$ is another output satisfying the same formula in $\Mker_\kappa$, by invariance under this automorphism. This contradicts uniqueness.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The base support model]
\label{hset:zr:thm:supportbase}
For every such infinite $\kappa$, $\Mker_\kappa$ is a model of $\ZFUR+\AC$. It is transitive, has every ambient subset of each of its sets, and has exactly the same pure sets as $\Uk$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Membership can only decrease kernels, so the class is transitive. Subsets do not increase kernels; hence every ambient subset of an internal set is internal, and the ambient power set of an internal set is itself internal. Pairing and finite unions use only finite unions of kernels of size below an infinite $\kappa$, again of size below $\kappa$. Union similarly adds no atoms beyond those already in the input's kernel. Extensionality, Foundation, and Infinity follow immediately. Separation follows by relativizing a fixed formula to the definable class $\Mker_\kappa$ and taking a subset of the given set.

For Replacement, suppose a formula assigns a unique internal output to every member of a set $w$, with parameter tuple coded by $p$. Ambient Replacement forms its range $W$; relativization to $\Mker_\kappa$ is a legitimate fixed ambient formula. By \cref{hset:zr:lem:confinement}, every output has kernel contained in $K=\kerat(w)\cup\kerat(p)$. Therefore $\kerat(W)\subseteq K$ and $W\in \Mker_\kappa$. This proves every internal Replacement instance.

For Choice, an ambient well-ordering of a set $w\in \Mker_\kappa$ has kernel contained in $\kerat(w)$, so that well-ordering belongs to $\Mker_\kappa$. Finally every pure set has empty kernel and is included; no impure object becomes pure by passing to this transitive model.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Agreement about cardinalities of internal sets]
\label{hset:zr:lem:cardagreement}
For every $w\in \Mker_\kappa$, its cardinality as computed in $\Mker_\kappa$ equals its ambient cardinality. Cofinalities of pure ordinals also agree.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
An ambient bijection between $w$ and its initial ordinal has kernel contained in $\kerat(w)$, so it is internal. Conversely an internal bijection is an ambient bijection. For cofinality, every function between pure ordinals is pure, so the two universes have exactly the same candidate cofinal maps.
\end{proof}

\section{The exact Collection spectrum}
\label{hset:zr:sec:spectrum}

Write $\CollS_{<\tau}$ for the scheme restricting index sets to cardinality below $\tau$. Saying that Collection fails at $\tau$ means that one explicit formula has a total witness relation on a domain of cardinality $\tau$ with no collecting set. It is not a quantification over a set of all schemes.

\begin{lemma}[The small-domain positive bound]
\label{hset:zr:lem:smallcollection}
For every infinite $\kappa$, $\Mker_\kappa$ satisfies $\CollS_{<\cf\kappa}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $w\in \Mker_\kappa$ have size $\lambda<\cf\kappa$, and suppose every $x\in w$ has an internal witness to a fixed formula. In the ambient universe, Collection followed by Choice gives a family of internal witnesses $y_x$. Each has a kernel of size below $\kappa$. A union of fewer than $\cf\kappa$ sets, each of size below $\kappa$, has size below $\kappa$; this is the usual cofinality calculation in the ambient universe with Choice. Consequently
\[
\left|\kerat\bigl(\{y_x:x\in w\}\bigr)\right|
\leq\left|\bigcup_{x\in w}\kerat(y_x)\right|<\kappa.
\]
The selected range is therefore an internal collecting set. By \cref{hset:zr:lem:cardagreement}, the domain-size hypothesis is the same internally and externally.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Limit-cardinal failure at the exact threshold]
\label{hset:zr:thm:limitspectrum}
Let $\kappa$ be a limit cardinal, with $\omega$ included in this terminology, and set $\tau=\cf\kappa$. Then
\[
\Mker_\kappa\models\CollS_{<\tau},
\qquad
\Mker_\kappa\not\models\CollS_{\tau}.
\]
For every cardinal $\lambda\geq\tau$, Collection also fails on a domain of size $\lambda$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The positive assertion is \cref{hset:zr:lem:smallcollection}. Choose a pure sequence of cardinals $(\mu_i)_{i<\tau}$ below $\kappa$, cofinal in $\kappa$. For $\kappa=\omega$, use finite cardinals. The sequence and its domain belong to $\Mker_\kappa$ because they are pure.

Consider the relation
\[
\varphi(i,B):\quad B\text{ is a set of atoms and }|B|=\mu_i.
\]
Every $i<\tau$ has a witness: select an ambient set $B\subseteq A$ of size $\mu_i$. Both $B$ and a bijection witnessing its cardinality have kernels of size below $\kappa$, so they are internal.

If $W\in \Mker_\kappa$ collected such witnesses, put $\rho=|\kerat(W)|<\kappa$. Some $\mu_i$ exceeds $\rho$. But every atom set $B\in W$ satisfies $B\subseteq\kerat(W)$ and cannot have size $\mu_i$. This is a contradiction.

For a larger domain, extend the same relation by giving a harmless fixed witness, such as the empty set, to the additional indices. A collecting set for the enlarged relation would collect witnesses for the original one.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The successor-cardinal case]
\label{hset:zr:thm:successorspectrum}
If $\kappa=\mu^+$ for an infinite cardinal $\mu$, then $\Mker_\kappa$ satisfies full Collection, with no restriction on the size of its index set.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let a formula with parameter $p$ have a witness for every $x\in w$. Set $K=\kerat(w)\cup\kerat(p)$; its size is at most $\mu$. Choose a set $B\subseteq A\setminus K$ of size $\mu$. This is possible because $|A|\geq\mu^+$.

Given one internal witness $y$, its kernel outside $K$ has size at most $\mu$. There is a permutation of $A$ fixing $K$ pointwise and sending these remaining atoms into $B$. To justify extension to a permutation, choose an injection onto a subset of $B$ and biject the two complementary parts of $A\setminus K$; both complements have cardinality $|A|$. The resulting automorphism fixes $x,p$ and preserves $\Mker_\kappa$. Thus an appropriate witness exists in $V(K\cup B)$ for every $x$.

For each $x$ take the least stage of this fixed-base hierarchy containing a witness. Replacement in $\Mker_\kappa$, already proved in \cref{hset:zr:thm:supportbase}, bounds these ordinal stages. A sufficiently high set $V_\gamma(K\cup B)$ belongs to $\Mker_\kappa$ and collects all the required witnesses.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Complete capacity classification]
\label{hset:zr:cor:classification}
For an infinite cardinal $\kappa$, $\Mker_\kappa$ satisfies full Collection if and only if $\kappa$ is a successor cardinal. If it is not, the least domain cardinality at which Collection fails is $\cf\kappa$.
\end{corollary}

The distinction is not simply regular versus singular. For example, $\omega$ is regular but the finite-support model fails countable Collection. A regular uncountable limit cardinal has failure first at that cardinal. A successor cardinal, by contrast, has a largest permitted infinite support size, and the common-reservoir argument works for domains of arbitrary cardinality.

\subsection{Prescribing the first failure}
\begin{corollary}
\label{hset:zr:cor:arbitrarythreshold}
For every infinite regular cardinal $\tau$, there is a cardinal-support model whose least Collection-failure domain has size $\tau$, while its pure universe is unchanged from the ambient one.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Choose $\kappa=\aleph_\tau$ and an ambient set of at least $\kappa$ atoms. Since $\tau$ is an infinite regular ordinal, $\kappa$ is a limit cardinal with $\cf\kappa=\tau$. The latter equality follows from continuity and strict increase of the aleph sequence: an index-cofinal sequence gives the upper bound, while a shorter cofinal sequence of ordinals below $\aleph_\tau$ would yield a shorter cofinal sequence of indices below $\tau$. Apply \cref{hset:zr:thm:limitspectrum}.
\end{proof}

\section{Naming an enumeration: exact failure of Replacement}
\label{hset:zr:sec:expansion}

Fix $A_0\subseteq A$ of size $\kappa$, and an ambient bijection
\[
e:\kappa\longrightarrow A_0.
\]
Its graph has kernel $A_0$, so it is not a set of $\Mker_\kappa$. Nevertheless it can be named as an external binary predicate $E$ on the domain of that model. We analyze the expansion $(\Mker_\kappa,E)$ with \emph{Separation and other schemes understood in the expanded language}.

This is a controlled instance of language sensitivity. It is not a reproof of Glazer's global-choice theorem, which concerns a different pure-set situation \cite{GlazerChoice}. The shared lesson is that adjoining a predicate can add functional definitions to which a scheme must apply.

\begin{proposition}[Elementary axioms survive the expansion]
\label{hset:zr:prop:expandedseparation}
The expansion $(\Mker_\kappa,E)$ satisfies the urelement versions of $\Zf$ and Choice, including full Separation for formulas using $E$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The structural axioms and Choice are unchanged. For an expanded-language formula, replace $E$ by its ambient set graph and relativize quantifiers to $\Mker_\kappa$. Ambient Separation gives a subset of the specified internal set. Every such subset is internal by \cref{hset:zr:thm:supportbase}, so expanded Separation holds.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The exact expanded-language threshold]
\label{hset:zr:thm:expandedthreshold}
For every infinite $\kappa$, let $\tau=\cf\kappa$. In $(\Mker_\kappa,E)$, both Collection and Replacement hold for all domains of cardinality below $\tau$, and both fail on a domain of cardinality exactly $\tau$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The positive argument in \cref{hset:zr:lem:smallcollection} does not use invariance under atom permutations. It uses only ambient Collection and Choice to gather fewer than $\cf\kappa$ witnesses, followed by a kernel-size calculation. It therefore works unchanged for formulas involving the ambient predicate $E$. This gives Collection, and hence Replacement, on all smaller domains.

Choose a pure strictly increasing cofinal sequence $(\alpha_i)_{i<\tau}$ in $\kappa$. The sequence is an internal set and may be used as a parameter. The expanded language defines, for each $i<\tau$, the unique set
\[
Y_i=e\,``\,\alpha_i.
\]
Each individual $Y_i$ belongs to $\Mker_\kappa$ because $|\alpha_i|<\kappa$. Its characterization uses $E$ and the cofinal sequence, not an internally existing graph of $e$.

If the range $\{Y_i:i<\tau\}$ were an internal set, its union would be the set $A_0$, since the $\alpha_i$ are cofinal in $\kappa$. But $A_0$ has kernel cardinality $\kappa$ and is not internal. Replacement therefore fails for this functional family. A collecting set for these unique witnesses would have kernel containing their union $A_0$, so Collection fails for the same family.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Countable supports]
Let $\kappa=\aleph_1$. The reduct $\Mker_{\aleph_1}$ satisfies full Collection. After naming an enumeration of $\aleph_1$ atoms, all countable-domain Collection and Replacement instances still hold, but both schemes fail at domain size $\aleph_1$. No pure set, sign sequence, or ordinary set function between pure objects has changed.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Singular support capacity]
Let $\kappa=\aleph_\omega$. In the reduct, Replacement holds on all domains but countable Collection fails. In the expansion, the sets $e\,``\,\aleph_n$ form a uniquely specified countable family with no internal range set. Thus countable Replacement now fails as well.
\end{example}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}l>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
Capacity & Replacement in $\Mker_\kappa$ & First Collection failure in $\Mker_\kappa$ & First failure of both in $(\Mker_\kappa,E)$ \\
\midrule
$\omega$ & All domains & $\omega$ & $\omega$ \\
$\aleph_1$ & All domains & None & $\aleph_1$ \\
$\aleph_\omega$ & All domains & $\omega$ & $\omega$ \\
$\aleph_{\omega_1}$ & All domains & $\omega_1$ & $\omega_1$ \\
General limit $\kappa$ & All domains & $\cf\kappa$ & $\cf\kappa$ \\
General successor $\kappa$ & All domains & None & $\kappa$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
Here ``first failure'' refers to the cardinality of the index domain. The table is an exact consequence of the preceding proofs, not a numerical experiment.

\section{Why pure surreal structures cannot see the support obstruction}
\label{hset:zr:sec:blindness}

\begin{proposition}[Identity of pure structures]
\label{hset:zr:prop:pureidentity}
The pure universe of $\Mker_\kappa$ is the pure universe of $\Uk$. Therefore their sign-sequence classes, birthdays, prefix relations, ordinal codes, and all set-coded relations between pure objects are identical. Any specified operation defined solely within the pure universe has the same interpretation in both.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Every pure object has empty kernel and is internal, and membership on these objects is unchanged. Every set made entirely from pure objects is itself pure. Thus the two pure membership structures are identical. Induction on a formula whose quantifiers are restricted to pure objects proves agreement on all such definitions. Apply this to sign sequences and the stated relations.
\end{proof}

This assertion is deliberately restricted to pure definitions. A formula with unrestricted quantification over impure objects can have different truth values in the two universes, even when its free variables happen to be pure.

\begin{theorem}[Pure-valued Collection survives definable expansions]
\label{hset:zr:thm:purecollection}
In $\Mker_\kappa$, expanded by any fixed finite list of ambient-definable predicates, Collection holds for every total relation whose witnesses are required to be pure. Index sets and parameters may be impure. In particular, this holds in $(\Mker_\kappa,E)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $w$ be an internal set, and suppose that for each $x\in w$ some pure $y$ satisfies a fixed formula in the expansion. Relativize that formula and the added predicates to the ambient universe. Ambient Collection produces a set of suitable witnesses. Separate away any extraneous nonpure members, obtaining a set $S$ of pure witnesses covering every index. A set of pure objects is pure, so $S\in \Mker_\kappa$. This same $S$ witnesses the original internal Collection instance.
\end{proof}

\noindent[Added 4 October 2026, batch~90: source 12 (\cref{hset:ns:part})
proves, independently, the case $\kappa=\omega$ of this theorem for the graph
$\Act$ of a named action of a pure set-sized group on the atoms
(\cref{hset:ns:thm:purecollection}), by a second route: it bounds the least pure
ranks of witnesses by ambient Replacement and separates them from one pure
stage. With it, the identity of pure structures
(\cref{hset:zr:prop:pureidentity}), the surreal schemes and the no-go
corollary below extend to such actions and to the graded levels
$\mathsf R_k$ of Replacement for outputs with at most $k$ atoms
(\cref{hset:ns:prop:surrealinvariance,hset:ns:cor:surreal,hset:ns:cor:invisible};
\cref{hset:ns:rem:companion}).]

\begin{corollary}[All six surreal schemes survive]
\label{hset:zr:cor:surrealsurvive}
Every scheme $\OB,\Gr,\UB,\SC,\DCI,\NIB$ holds in $(\Mker_\kappa,E)$, including its instances using $E$ and arbitrary internal parameters, despite the failure of full Replacement in \cref{hset:zr:thm:expandedthreshold}.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Surreal sign sequences and ordinals are pure. Hence \cref{hset:zr:thm:purecollection} gives $\SC$ and collects ordinal witnesses. Set graphs can then be separated using expanded Separation; their kernels are contained in the kernel of the index domain. The pure range sets have pure ordinal birthday bounds and pure interpolants, all of which are internal. The arguments of \cref{hset:zr:thm:equivalence} apply directly to these sign sequences.
\end{proof}

There is no contradiction with \cref{hset:zr:thm:ranked}. The hierarchy axiom used there is a cofinal \emph{pure} hierarchy covering every object of an atom-free universe. No pure $V_\alpha$ contains an atom. In the urelement setting, the varying atom base is an additional resource not controlled by ordinal bounding.

\begin{corollary}[No pure-surreal characterization of ambient Collection]
\label{hset:zr:cor:nogo}
Across urelement models of the kind constructed here, no statement or scheme depending only on the pure surreal structure can characterize full ambient Collection. Even the enlarged package of all definable surreal-valued Collection and interpolation instances does not characterize it. Across the displayed predicate expansions, that package does not characterize full ambient Replacement either.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Within an ambient universe with enough atoms, compare a successor-capacity model and a limit-capacity model. Their pure universes, and hence their pure surreal structures, are identical, while full Collection holds in the former and fails in the latter. Both have all the surreal schemes by Replacement or \cref{hset:zr:thm:purecollection}.

For Replacement, compare the same $\Mker_\kappa$ with an empty interpretation of the extra predicate and with the enumeration interpretation $E$. The first expansion is definitionally equivalent to the reduct and satisfies full Replacement. The second fails it by \cref{hset:zr:thm:expandedthreshold}, while satisfying all the surreal schemes by \cref{hset:zr:cor:surrealsurvive}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Reflection and the limit of the conclusion}
Full first-order reflection to transitive sets implies Collection: reflect simultaneously the totality assertion for a witness relation and the relation itself, with the index set and parameters included. The reflecting set then supplies genuine witnesses for all indices. This standard implication is also explicit in Glazer--Yao's analysis \cite{GlazerYao}. Thus our limit-capacity models fail this full reflection principle.

We do not obtain Glazer--Yao's finer separations between partial reflection, supertransitive partial reflection, and full reflection. Nor do we exclude a useful \emph{mixed} surreal--atom theory. Naming atom kernels or permitting atom-labelled objects can expose exactly the data that the pure structure forgets. The obstruction concerns the proposed pure detector, not the possibility of any formalization of urelement set theory.

\section{A formalization route for ProveIt}
\label{hset:zr:sec:formalization}

\subsection{Repository alignment and inspection boundary}

\end{center}

Location & Role in a future development \\

This was a targeted README and directory inspection, not a full source audit or a successful build of the repository. The repository's existing kernel-audit claims do not transfer to the present manuscript. No purportedly checked declaration name is invented here.

above is the commit ``Catalogue batches 81 to 86''. Source 10 did not inspect

\subsection{Separate object-level strength from host-level resources}
A theorem about ordinary Lean types can use host-level function types, images, ordinal recursion, and classical choice without recording whether these correspond to Replacement or Choice inside a weak object theory. Consequently proving a cut theorem for a universe-polymorphic surreal type is not by itself a formal proof that $\Zf$ proves that theorem.

The proposed formalization should have two layers. The first is a conventional order-theoretic library proving the sign comparisons and the finite-container construction. The second interprets the same constructions in arbitrary models of the explicitly listed weak axioms. A function type in the host must not silently substitute for an internal set graph.

In particular, ``$\varphi$ defines a functional family'' and ``there is an internal graph for $\varphi$'' should be different predicates. Their equivalence is one of the conclusions under investigation, not an implementation convenience.

\subsection{Suggested modules and proof order}
The following are proposed module names, not existing files.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \texttt{WeakSignSequences}: internal function graphs on ordinals, lexicographic comparison, prefix, bounded containers, and all-plus and negative ordinal codes.
\item \texttt{SetCutSimplicity}: the set of good paths, compatibility, its union, the separation invariant, and the unique simplest interpolant with its birthday bound.
\item \texttt{DefinableFamilySchemes}: syntactic transformations implementing the six implications, with explicit parameters and index domains.
\item \texttt{RankedReplacement}: the hierarchy axiom, least-stage witnesses, and the equivalence with Collection and Replacement.
\item \texttt{AtomKernelModels}: tagged urelement semantics, kernel operations, permutation action, and the proof of kernel confinement.
\item \texttt{CapacitySpectra}: the small-domain union bound, cofinal support-size obstruction, and the successor reservoir argument.
\item \texttt{PredicateExpansion}: interpretation of an external enumeration, expanded Separation, exact failure families, and pure-valued Collection.
\end{enumerate}
The set-cut lemma should be completed before importing any full-ZF surreal construction into the weak-base argument. Its proof has a natural bounded interface: a fixed ordinal $\lambda$ and the set $T_\lambda$.

\subsection{Trust boundaries and quantification}
A satisfactory formal artifact would explicitly audit at least four boundaries. First, it would specify the object theory rather than merely a list of host axioms. Second, it would distinguish each standard formula instance from a single internal assertion quantifying over formula codes. Third, it would not represent all subsets of a proper class by a host powerset. Fourth, it would distinguish the original language from its expansion by $E$ when stating Separation and Replacement.

For the support models, one can formalize an external set-sized model and its interpreted submodel, or a definable class interpretation in a stronger metatheory. The theorem is relative: it constructs a model from an ambient model. It is not an assertion that a consistent first-order theory proves its own consistency.

\section{Executable finite checks and reproducibility}
\label{hset:zr:sec:checks}
The accompanying program, shipped as \path{code/10-cuts-replacement-verify_finite.py} (delivered as \path{code/verify_finite.py}), uses only the Python standard library. It represents a finite sign sequence by a tuple of $-1$ and $+1$, with termination compared as $0$. It implements the obstruction rule from \cref{hset:zr:thm:setcut}, then independently enumerates candidates to check separation, unique minimum birthday, and the prefix property.

The recorded run, shipped as \path{data/10-cuts-replacement-finite_checks.json} (delivered as \path{data/finite_checks.json}), completed with the following results:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lr@{}}
\toprule
Check & Number \\
\midrule
Sign words of birthday at most $6$ & $127$ \\
Endpoint intervals, including empty sides & $8\,256$ \\
Assignments to left/right/neither at depth $2$ & $2\,187$ \\
Separated assignments among these & $576$ \\
Negative-code comparisons and birthday implications & $1\,050$ \\
Atom-transposition checks & $4\,032$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Every test passed. Endpoint intervals suffice for all finite sets of bounds from the depth-six endpoint universe, since a nonempty finite left set has a maximum and a nonempty finite right set has a minimum. The depth-two assignment check separately exercises the implementation on nonsingleton sets.

The atom test verifies the local combinatorial fact used by the confinement proof: a swap outside a fixed parameter support fixes that support while moving a witness support containing exactly one of the swapped atoms. It does not simulate a model of set theory or a singular cardinal.

In the delivered layout the run was
\begin{verbatim}
python3 code/verify_finite.py --output data/finite_checks.json
\end{verbatim}
followed by two \texttt{pdflatex} passes of the delivered manuscript. With the
shipped names, run the program on a copy and pass an explicit output path
outside this directory (the report's \path{README.md} gives the commands). For
this merge it was rerun with Python 3.14.4 on a copy: it passed and reproduced
the recorded file exactly, apart from line endings.
The tests cannot establish a transfinite induction, an axiom-scheme equivalence, an internal cardinal calculation, or a relative-consistency claim. Those depend on the mathematical proofs above. The delivered archive also contained a README, a proof-status note and a build script; the proof-status note is shipped as \path{10-cuts-replacement-PROOF_STATUS.md} and the build script as \path{code/10-cuts-replacement-build.sh} (it builds the delivered manuscript in the delivered layout, not this report), while the delivery README, the manuscript and its PDF are not shipped (\cref{hset:zr:sec:provenance}).

\section{Further research questions and proposed projects}
\label{hset:zr:sec:questions}
The questions below are a research agenda arising from the proved results. Their status is ``not resolved in this manuscript''; no claim is made that every formulation is globally new or currently open in the literature.

\begin{question}[Remove or characterize the hierarchy hypothesis]\label{hset:zr:q:hierarchy}
Over plain $\Zf$, determine the exact relationship between $\OB$ and full Replacement without assuming $\Hier$. Identify the weakest rank-coverage or tier-coverage principle that turns the equivalence in \cref{hset:zr:thm:equivalence} into full Replacement, and give explicit models distinguishing the relevant choices of coverage axiom.
\end{question}
The point is not to omit $\Hier$ typographically. A useful answer must analyze the existence of ranks and hierarchy segments inside the weak model.

\begin{question}[Formula-complexity accounting]\label{hset:zr:q:complexity}
Determine the optimal correspondence between restricted $\Sigma_n$ or $\Pi_n$ instances of ordinal bounding, surreal graph existence, and interval bounding. In particular, quantify the complexity increase caused by ``least birthday of a witness'' and by the good-path definition.
\end{question}
A precise answer would turn the full-scheme result into a hierarchy of proof-theoretic characterizations.

\begin{question}[Domain spectra in weak pure models]\label{hset:zr:q:domainspectra}
For models of $\Zf+\Hier$, classify the index-domain sizes on which the six equivalent principles hold. Which initial segments of cardinalities can occur, and how are they constrained by definable cofinal maps into the model's ordinals?
\end{question}
The $V_{\omega+\omega}$ example gives failure already on $\omega$, but the general domain spectrum deserves a separate analysis. Internal and external cardinalities must be kept distinct.

\noindent[Added 3 October 2026, batch~89: for the two-sorted \emph{full-class}
rank models $(V_\lambda,\Pow(V_\lambda))$ the domain spectrum of class
Replacement and Collection is computed in Part~XV of
\repo{foundations-and-computation/surreal-well-orders}: exactly the domains of
size below $\cf\lambda$ (\cref{hset:zr:rem:swo}). The question above, for the
\emph{definable} schemes in arbitrary models of $\Zf+\Hier$, is not answered
there and stays open.]

\begin{question}[Beyond cardinality ideals]\label{hset:zr:q:ideals}
Replace $|\kerat(x)|<\kappa$ by membership of the kernel in a permutation-invariant ideal $\mathscr I$ on the atoms. Give necessary and sufficient conditions on $\mathscr I$ for Replacement, for Collection on a specified class of domains, and for a common-reservoir argument.
\end{question}
The cardinal-capacity calculation suggests that the relevant invariants combine union closure, available support sizes, and the action of permutations fixing parameter kernels. An arbitrary ideal need not have the fresh-atom property used in \cref{hset:zr:lem:confinement}.

\begin{question}[Mixed surreal--atom witnesses]\label{hset:zr:q:mixed}
Design a natural two-sorted extension of the sign-sequence structure whose witnesses encode both a birthday and an atom base. Can a local interpolation or extension principle in this richer structure characterize full Collection without merely restating the two-resource criterion by definition?
\end{question}
A successful construction should remain mathematically recognizable and should explain how automorphisms act on the added data.

\begin{question}[Admissible language expansions]\label{hset:zr:q:expansions}
Classify predicates $P$ for which $(\Mker_\kappa,P)$ retains Replacement or full Collection. Can the first failure be characterized in terms of the support growth of uniquely $P$-definable outputs? How much of the permutation group must remain available to force kernel confinement?
\end{question}
The enumeration predicate supplies a sharp negative case. Predicates invariant under every atom permutation supply an immediate positive mechanism, but intermediate stabilizer groups are more interesting.

\noindent[Added 3 October 2026, batch~89: for one further family of predicates,
the graphs of canonical lifts of finitely many atom injections, the report
\repo{SetTheory/Cardinals/docs/reports/ordinals-and-order-types/naming-elementary-embeddings}
(\cref{hset:zr:rem:nee}) decides exactly when the expansion keeps Replacement
and full Collection, by a criterion on the components of the named injections
(Theorem~5.1, \lbl{nee:thm:profile}; Theorem~7.5, \lbl{nee:thm:collection}).
This answers the question for that family only; the general classification
stays open.]

\noindent[Added 4 October 2026, batch~90: answered for a second family at
$\kappa=\omega$, the graphs $\Act$ of uniformly named actions of pure set-sized
groups, by source 12 (\cref{hset:ns:part}; \cref{hset:ns:rem:expansions}).
Replacement holds iff every orbit is finite and only finitely many atoms lie in
finite orbit types that occur finitely often (\cref{hset:ns:thm:full}); the
support growth of uniquely definable outputs is measured by the levels
$\mathsf R_k$, which hold, for $k\ge1$, iff every orbit is finite and only
finitely many atoms have an orbit of size at most $k$ under the centralizer
$\mathcal Z$ of the named action (\cref{hset:ns:thm:graded}); so the group that
must remain is $\mathcal Z$. Full Collection fails at $\kappa=\omega$ for every
such expansion. For finitely many named permutations this criterion is the
case $\kappa=\omega$ of the one cited in the preceding note. Arbitrary
predicates, and group actions at uncountable $\kappa$, stay open.]

\begin{question}[Reflection spectra rather than Collection spectra]\label{hset:zr:q:reflection}
In controlled kernel models, determine which additional features separate partial reflection from full reflection while the pure surreal structure remains fixed. Seek a precise comparison with the more elaborate models of Glazer--Yao, rather than assuming the cardinal-capacity invariant alone controls reflection.
\end{question}
The present models are intentionally homogeneous and do not reproduce those finer separations.

\noindent[Added 3 October 2026, batch~89: the same report shows, for the kernel
models expanded by named lifts, that full transitive reflection is equivalent
to Collection there (Theorem~7.7, \lbl{nee:thm:reflection}), and that partial transitive
reflection of assertions $\forall x\in w\,\exists y\,\delta$ with $\Delta_0$
matrix is $\Delta_0$ Collection, which fails at limit cutoffs, although each
single true $\exists y\,\delta$ reflects (Proposition~8.1, \lbl{nee:prop:partial}). It bears on
this question but does not reproduce Glazer--Yao's separations; the question
stays open.]

\begin{question}[Weaker power-set assumptions]\label{hset:zr:q:powerset}
The good-path proof uses the set $\Pow(\lambda\times\signs)$. Determine whether a restricted binary-sequence existence axiom suffices in place of full Power Set, and whether interpolation over such a base becomes equivalent to additional Collection or recursion principles.
\end{question}
This asks for a genuinely different lower-strength construction, not a proof that secretly retains the same function-space container.

\begin{question}[Constructive variants]\label{hset:zr:q:constructive}
Analyze which parts of the six-scheme equivalence survive intuitionistic logic. The current comparison rule uses a first disagreement and decidable alternatives between obstructions, while least-witness birthday selection also has classical content. A constructive version should specify which order and cut notions replace these choices.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[A proof-assistant benchmark with semantic safeguards]\label{hset:zr:q:benchmark}
Implement the weak-base and expanded-language results in ProveIt, using the same internal formula language for the positive and negative directions. Include regression tests that intentionally try to confuse a definable family with a set graph, or to use a reduct's Replacement instance after adjoining $E$.
\end{question}
Such a benchmark would test more than symbolic algebra: it would test whether an automated proof pipeline preserves the intended theory and language across an argument.

\section{Conclusion of source 10, ledger and provenance}
\label{hset:zr:sec:provenance}

\subsection{Conclusion}
The phrase ``a set-indexed family of surreal numbers'' conceals two different assertions. A family already represented by a set graph supports the ordinary simplicity construction in Zermelo set theory without Replacement. A merely definable functional family can fail to have that graph. Uniformly repairing this failure is equivalent to ordinal bounding, and on an explicit pure cumulative-hierarchy base it is equivalent to full Replacement and Collection. The obstruction is visible inside the single interval $(-1,0)$.

Urelements expose a second direction of failure. Pure sign sequences retain ordinal height but forget atom bases. Cardinal-support models keep the entire pure universe unchanged while varying the exact domain threshold for Collection. An external atom enumeration can additionally destroy Replacement, with an exact cofinality threshold, even though every pure-valued witness relation still satisfies Collection.

These results provide a concrete intersection between weak-set-theoretic research and surreal formalization. Their most useful lesson is operational: record the language, the index domain, the birthday bound, and the atom support separately. Each is mathematically consequential, and none should be supplied silently by the notation or by the proof assistant's host theory.

\part{Surreal universality without choice}\label{hset:kw:part}

\section{Source 11: question, results, notation and relation to the report}
\label{hset:kw:sec:intro}

This part was also added in batch~89. It prints the manuscript \emph{Surreal
Universality Without Choice: Exact coding strength, set-sized universal fields,
and finite-transcendence collapse}, ``A research manuscript prepared for
Vladimir Reshetnikov'', October 3, 2026. It is batch-89 manuscript 04 and
\emph{source 11} in this report's local numbering; its shipped files carry the
prefix \texttt{11-choiceless-universality-}. It names the repository commit
\texttt{2b7b388ba} (``Catalogue batches 81 to 86''; source 11 calls it a ``tree
identifier'', but it is a commit), arrived in commit \texttt{7c0f2d9f9} and was
placed in commit \texttt{23adb85f9}. Its provenance and the mechanical changes
made in printing it are in \cref{hset:kw:sec:provenance}. Inside this part,
``this manuscript'', ``here'', ``our'' and ``we'' refer to source 11.

\begin{resultbox}
\textbf{Research intersection (source 11).} Choiceless set theory and permutation models, as in the work of Elliot Glazer and Bokai Yao, meet the sign-sequence, Hahn-series, and omnific-integer foundations developed in the ProveIt repository.

\medskip
\textbf{Status.} The manuscript supplies complete mathematical arguments for the stated deductions from explicitly identified classical background. It is an AI-generated, unrefereed research draft, not a Lean- or Rocq-checked development. Historical novelty is not certified. It does not claim to solve the reflection problems studied by Glazer and Yao, nor to transfer an atom-model counterexample automatically to pure set theory.

\medskip
\textbf{Main message.} Freely naming independent surreal coordinates costs exactly one powerset-of-an-ordinal code. Preserving a given order is a separate requirement. In symmetric atom models, finite support places a sharp finite bound on how many independent coordinates any one representation can retain.
\end{resultbox}

\paragraph{Source 11's summary.} We calibrate several universality assertions for the surreal numbers over set theory without the axiom of choice. For every set $X$, an injection $X\to\No$ exists if and only if $X$ injects into the powerset of an ordinal. An explicit double-monomial construction upgrades any such injection to algebraically independent positive infinite omnific integers. Consequently, the existence of embeddings of all free integer polynomial rings into $\Oz$, or all free real rational-function fields into $\No$, is exactly the first Kinna--Wagner principle.

For every infinite initial ordinal $\kappa$, we construct a set-sized real-closed subfield $U_\kappa\subseteq\No$, equinumerous with $\Pow(\kappa)$ without choice, which realizes precisely the free real rational-function fields whose variable sets inject into $\Pow(\kappa)$. The cardinal upper bound uses canonical fraction and root codes rather than an unjustified choice of quotient representatives.

An orbit-factorization theorem then yields sharp bounds in permutation models. A polynomial representation into the pure surreals supported by $s$ atoms has image-field transcendence degree at most $s+1$ in the basic Fraenkel model, and at most $2s+1$ in the ordered Mostowski model. Both bounds are attained. The latter model contains an ordered rational-function field with no embedding into its surreals, although every finitely generated-over-$\R$ coordinate subfield embeds in order. Compatible local embeddings would produce a global one, so the missing ingredient is coherence, not a defect of the finite algebra.

We separate coding universality from prescribed-order universality, give an ordinal convex-resolution criterion for order embeddings, identify the precise foundations required by the proofs, and propose a formalization and research program.

\subsection{The research question}
The slogan that the surreal numbers are a universal ordered field suppresses several different quantifiers. Does one wish to assign distinct surreal coordinates to the elements of a set? To assign algebraically independent coordinates? To embed a field as a field, or as a field with its already specified order? Must the embedding belong to the universe in which the field was constructed? These questions coincide much less often when choice is unavailable.

This distinction provides a concrete meeting point between two research programs. Glazer and Yao's \emph{Reflection Principles in ZFU} studies how assertions that agree under choice can separate when urelements and permutation symmetries are present \cite{GlazerYao}. ProveIt's surreal-number project develops sign-sequence foundations, normal forms, the real-closed surreal field, and the omnific-integer subring; it also explicitly distinguishes research claims from checked declarations \cite{ProveItSurreal}. Our subject is the choice cost of \emph{representing labelled algebra inside those fields}.

The relevant weak-choice principle is classical. With the indexing convention of Karagila and Schilhan \cite{KaragilaSchilhan},
\begin{equation}\label{hset:kw:eq:kwp}
\KWP_1:\qquad
\forall X\ \exists\alpha\in\Ord\quad X\hookrightarrow\Pow(\alpha).
\end{equation}
Its coding formulation is not a new principle introduced here. Our contribution is an explicit algebraic calibration, a set-sized optimal target at each code level, and a quantitative analysis of how symmetry defeats the same constructions internally.

\subsection{What is proved, and what is not claimed}
The principal questions answered in this manuscript are as follows.
\begin{description}[style=nextline,leftmargin=0pt,labelsep=0pt]
\item[How much choice is required to realize arbitrary independent variables in $\No$ or $\Oz$?]
Exactly $\KWP_1$, for the free-algebra assertions specified in \cref{hset:kw:thm:equivalences}. The result is an equivalence over $\ZF$, not merely an implication from choice.
\item[Can one use an optimal set-sized real-closed target at a fixed ordinal code level?]
Yes. The field $U_\kappa$ of \cref{hset:kw:thm:universal} has the same cardinality, in the sense of an actual bijection, as $\Pow(\kappa)$.
\item[What does finite support force a polynomial representation to forget?]
It identifies variables in the same stabilizer orbit. In two standard atom models this gives the sharp bounds $s+1$ and $2s+1$ of \cref{hset:kw:thm:fraenkel,hset:kw:thm:mostowski}.
\item[Does finite-stage embeddability ensure a global surreal embedding?]
No, in the ordered permutation model. It does so precisely when the finite-stage maps can be assembled coherently, as formalized in \cref{hset:kw:thm:coherence}.
\end{description}
These are natural questions formulated and answered here; they are not presented as named, previously published open problems. The elementary coding observation, finite normal-form identities, polynomial universal property, and standard permutation-model construction are established machinery. The possible novelty lies in the exact combined statements and the quantitative refinements. A targeted literature review did not establish historical priority for those formulations.

\subsection{Reading paths}
For the main positive results, read \cref{hset:kw:sec:background,hset:kw:sec:coding,hset:kw:sec:independence,hset:kw:sec:universal}. For the negative results, read \cref{hset:kw:sec:orbits,hset:kw:sec:fm,hset:kw:sec:counterexample}. The prescribed-order issue is isolated in \cref{hset:kw:sec:order,hset:kw:sec:resolutions}. The final sections discuss proof boundaries, formalization, and research questions. All displayed embeddings into a proper class have set-sized domains and set graphs.

\Cref{hset:q:axiomatics} lists, as source 06's open item, ``a universal

all of whose finitely generated coordinate subfields embed has no embedding
(\cref{hset:kw:thm:orderedcounter}). It leaves open universality for prescribed
orders and for general ordered fields without choice (its
\cref{hset:kw:q:prescribed,hset:kw:q:kwporder,hset:kw:q:fields}), and any ZF
transfer of the ZFA counterexample. Source 11 did not know this report.

\begin{remark}[Comparison with the choice-free Section~\ref{hset:sec:choice}]
\label{hset:kw:rem:choice}
\Cref{hset:thm:choice} (source 06) identifies, over ZF, the range of the pointed
ordinal-graph \emph{interpretation} of sets: exactly $\HWO$, the sets whose
transitive closure is well-orderable. \Cref{hset:kw:thm:coding} concerns
\emph{injections} of a set into $\No$: $X\hookrightarrow\No$ iff
$X\hookrightarrow\Pow(\alpha)$ for some $\alpha$. The second class is the wider
one. If $x\in\HWO$ then $x\subseteq\TC(\{x\})$ is well-orderable and so injects
into an ordinal; but $\Pow(\omega)$ always injects into $\No$ (take $\alpha=\omega$),
while in a model of ZF where $\Pow(\omega)$ is not well-orderable it is not in
$\HWO$ (\cref{hset:prop:internal-choice} uses the same hypothesis). Under Choice
both classes are everything. A set coded by a surreal in the sense of
\cref{hset:thm:choice} is recovered as a set; an injection into $\No$ only names
its elements. This comparison is an observation of this merge.
\end{remark}
