\section{Foundations, notation, and the surreal interface}
\label{hset:kw:sec:background}

\subsection{Set theory and classes}
Unless otherwise specified, the positive results are theorems over $\ZF$. We use classes only as definable predicates and definable operations on sets. Equivalently, one may work in a conservative class-theoretic presentation without adding set choice or global choice. The permutation-model sections work in $\ZFA$: set theory with a \emph{set} of atoms. This is a special setting within urelement theories, not the unrestricted proper-class-of-atoms setting of all results in \cite{GlazerYao}.

A surreal number is represented by a function
\[
s:\zeta\longrightarrow\{-,+\},\qquad \zeta\in\Ord.
\]
Its birthday is $\bd(s)=\zeta$. The totality $\No$ of such functions is a proper class. We write
\[
\No_{\le\alpha}=\bigcup_{\zeta\le\alpha}\{-,+\}^{\zeta}.
\]
This is a set. An expression $f:X\to\No$ means that $f$ is a set of ordered pairs, its domain is the set $X$, and every value satisfies the defining predicate for $\No$. Replacement therefore applies to the values and to their birthdays. We never take a powerset of $\No$.

In $\ZFA$, the signs, ordinals, and sign sequences are pure sets: their transitive closures contain no atoms. Atom permutations fix each surreal number pointwise. A function whose arguments involve atoms need not be pure, even if all its values are surreals. This difference drives the counterexamples.

\subsection{The standard surreal facts used}
We use the following classical interface, with no choice principle added to the ambient set theory. Conway's foundational construction is the underlying reference \cite{Conway}; Costin and Ehrlich explicitly develop their positive surreal theory in a class framework without set or class choice \cite{CostinEhrlich}.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(S\arabic*)]
\item $\No$ is an ordered real-closed field containing the canonically embedded ordered field $\R$.
\item The Conway monomial map $x\mapsto\omega^x$ is strictly increasing, has positive values, and satisfies
\[
\omega^{x+y}=\omega^x\omega^y,\qquad\omega^0=1.
\]
Here $\omega^x$ denotes Conway's monomial, not the separate Gonshor exponential function.
\item If $x_1>\cdots>x_n$ and $r_i\in\R\setminus\{0\}$, the finite normal form
\[
\sum_{i=1}^n r_i\omega^{x_i}
\]
is nonzero and has the sign of $r_1$. In particular, distinct monomials are linearly independent over $\R$.
\item A surreal is omnific when its normal-form exponents are nonnegative and its constant coefficient is an ordinary integer. These numbers form the subring $\Oz$. In particular, a monomial $\omega^e$ with $e>0$ is a positive infinite omnific integer.
\item For set-sized $A,B\subseteq\No$ with $A<B$, there is a unique simplest surreal strictly between them. This is the usual set-cut construction.
\end{enumerate}
Only finite normal forms are needed for the independence and symmetry theorems. The set-sized real-closed-target theorem additionally uses real closedness. No theorem about arbitrary rearrangements of infinite Hahn sums, surreal integration, or sharp product birthdays is used.

\begin{remark}[Three meanings of support]
A polynomial has a finite \emph{variable support}; a Hahn or Conway series has a \emph{normal-form support}; an object in a permutation model has an \emph{atom support}. They are different notions. In particular, a polynomial homomorphism with finite atom support can have a domain with infinitely many variables.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Algebra on arbitrary sets of variables}
For any set $X$, let $\N^{(X)}$ be the set of finitely supported functions $X\to\N$. The ring $k[T_X]$ consists of finitely supported coefficient functions on $\N^{(X)}$. Addition is pointwise and multiplication is finite convolution. This definition requires no ordering or enumeration of $X$. If $k$ is a field, the resulting ring is a domain, and its fraction field is denoted $k(T_X)$.

An embedding of $\R$-algebras fixes the specified copy of $\R$. A unital field homomorphism is automatically injective. A field embedding, unless explicitly called ordered, need not preserve a prescribed order on the source. Finite transcendence degrees below are ordinary nonnegative integers; no existence of an arbitrary infinite transcendence basis is assumed.

\section{The exact coding strength of a surreal coordinate set}
\label{hset:kw:sec:coding}

\begin{theorem}[Ordinal-code equivalence]\label{hset:kw:thm:coding}
For every set $X$, the following are equivalent over $\ZF$:
\[
X\hookrightarrow\No
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\exists\alpha\in\Ord\quad X\hookrightarrow\Pow(\alpha).
\]
The same equivalence holds in $\ZFA$ with pure sign-sequence surreals.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Suppose first that $j:X\to\No$ is injective. By Replacement, the set of birthdays of its values exists. Let
\[
\alpha=\sup\{\bd(j(x)):x\in X\},
\]
with $\alpha=0$ if $X=\varnothing$. Every value belongs to $\No_{\le\alpha}$.

For a sign sequence $s$ of length $\zeta\le\alpha$, put
\begin{equation}\label{hset:kw:eq:signcode}
\operatorname{enc}_\alpha(s)=
\{2\cdot\xi+1:\xi<\zeta\}
\ \cup\
\{2\cdot\xi:\xi<\zeta\text{ and }s(\xi)=+\}.
\end{equation}
This is a subset of the ordinal $2\cdot\alpha$. Multiplication here is ordinal multiplication on the indicated side: $2\cdot\alpha$ is the sum of $\alpha$ consecutive two-point blocks. The odd position of each block records whether the sign is defined; the even position records whether it is positive. Thus the code uniquely determines both the domain and every sign, so $\operatorname{enc}_\alpha$ is injective. Composing with $j$ proves the forward implication.

Conversely, given an injection $c:X\to\Pow(\alpha)$, let $s_x$ be the sign sequence of exact length $\alpha$ defined by
\[
s_x(\xi)=+\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \xi\in c(x).
\]
Different codes give different surreals. This definition uses only Separation and Replacement and works unchanged when the members of $X$ are atoms or sets involving atoms.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why definedness must be encoded]
Coding a sign sequence solely by its positive positions loses information: the empty sequence and the one-symbol sequence $-$ would both have empty code. The first half of \eqref{hset:kw:eq:signcode} prevents that error. Padding by a third symbol, as in \cref{hset:kw:sec:resolutions}, is an alternative.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Exact birthday capacity]\label{hset:kw:prop:capacity}
For every infinite initial ordinal $\kappa$, there is a bijection
\[
\No_{\le\kappa}\ \simeq\ \Pow(\kappa)
\]
in $\ZF$. For a finite ordinal $n$,
\[
|\No_{\le n}|=2^{n+1}-1.
\]
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Subsets of $\kappa$ inject into the sign sequences of exact length $\kappa$. In the other direction, \eqref{hset:kw:eq:signcode} maps $\No_{\le\kappa}$ into $\Pow(2\cdot\kappa)$. The ordinal $2\cdot\kappa$ is equipotent with $\kappa$: finite products of an infinite well-orderable cardinal do not require choice. Transporting subsets along such a bijection and applying Cantor--Bernstein gives the result. At finite $n$, sum the $2^i$ sign sequences of each length $0\le i\le n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Coincidence of least code and birthday budgets]\label{hset:kw:cor:budgets}
Let $X$ be an infinite set admitting the embeddings in \cref{hset:kw:thm:coding}. Define
\[
\operatorname{cl}(X)=\min\{\alpha:X\hookrightarrow\Pow(\alpha)\},\qquad
\operatorname{bl}(X)=\min\{\alpha:X\hookrightarrow\No_{\le\alpha}\}.
\]
Then $\operatorname{cl}(X)=\operatorname{bl}(X)$, and their common value is an infinite initial ordinal.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Each minimum exists by taking the least ordinal below an existing witness. Neither is finite, since a set injecting into a finite set is finite. If an infinite witness ordinal $\alpha$ is not initial, replace it by its initial ordinal $\kappa<\alpha$. Subsets of $\alpha$ and of $\kappa$ are equipotent. Moreover, the proof of \cref{hset:kw:prop:capacity} shows $\No_{\le\alpha}\hookrightarrow\Pow(\kappa)\hookrightarrow\No_{\le\kappa}$. Thus a least witness is initial. At infinite initial ordinals the two target sets have equal capacity by \cref{hset:kw:prop:capacity}.
\end{proof}
The finite restriction matters. A three-element set fits into $\No_{\le1}$, whereas it does not fit into $\Pow(1)$.

\section{Double monomials and free-algebra universality}
\label{hset:kw:sec:independence}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform algebraic independence]\label{hset:kw:thm:double}
Let $j:X\to\No$ be an injection. Define
\begin{equation}\label{hset:kw:eq:double}
e_x=\omega^{j(x)},\qquad t_x=\omega^{e_x}.
\end{equation}
Then $(t_x)_{x\in X}$ is algebraically independent over $\R$. Every $t_x$ is a positive infinite omnific integer. Evaluation gives embeddings
\[
\Z[T_X]\hookrightarrow\Oz,
\qquad
\R[T_X]\hookrightarrow\No,
\qquad
\R(T_X)\hookrightarrow\No.
\]
All these maps are definable from $j$ and have set graphs.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The numbers $e_x$ are distinct Conway monomials. By finite normal-form uniqueness they are linearly independent over $\R$, and in particular over $\Z$.

For $m\in\N^{(X)}$, define the finite sum
\[
E_m=\sum_{x\in X}m(x)e_x.
\]
If $m\ne n$, the finite linear combination $\sum_x(m(x)-n(x))e_x$ is nonzero. Hence $E_m\ne E_n$. The multiplication law for Conway monomials gives
\[
\prod_x t_x^{m(x)}=\omega^{E_m}.
\]
Now take a nonzero polynomial, written after collecting like monomials as
\[
p=\sum_{m\in F}a_mT^m,
\]
where $F$ is finite and every $a_m\ne0$. Evaluation produces
\begin{equation}\label{hset:kw:eq:normal-evaluation}
p(t)=\sum_{m\in F}a_m\omega^{E_m}.
\end{equation}
The exponents are distinct. They can be sorted because they form a finite subset of a linear order. The resulting finite normal form is nonzero, proving algebraic independence.

Each $e_x>0$, regardless of the sign or magnitude of $j(x)$. Thus $t_x=\omega^{e_x}$ has a single positive exponent and is a positive infinite element of $\Oz$. In \eqref{hset:kw:eq:normal-evaluation}, every nonconstant monomial has exponent $E_m>0$. If the coefficients are integers, the only possible constant coefficient is an integer, so the value is omnific. Evaluation is therefore injective on both polynomial rings in the stated targets. Since nonzero denominators do not vanish, the real polynomial embedding extends uniquely to its fraction field. Replacement on the set of polynomials and fractions gives the graphs of the maps.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Why the second monomial layer is needed]
An arbitrary injective assignment $j$ does not make $\omega^{j(x)}$ algebraically independent. For labels $j(x_0)=0$, $j(x_1)=1$, and $j(x_2)=2$, the assigned values are $1,\omega,\omega^2$, and two immediate polynomial relations hold. The second layer in \eqref{hset:kw:eq:double} makes the \emph{exponents} linearly independent before using them to build algebraically independent monomials.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[A concrete leading-term calculation]
With two labels $j(a)=0$ and $j(b)=1$, the double-monomial values are
$t_a=\omega$ and $t_b=\omega^\omega$. For instance,
\[
T_b^2-3T_aT_b+T_a^5
\quad\longmapsto\quad
\omega^{2\omega}-3\omega^{\omega+1}+\omega^5>0.
\]
The three displayed exponents are distinct and decreasing. This illustrates the proof mechanism: a formal monomial becomes a Conway monomial, and the greatest surviving exponent certifies nonvanishing. The products and sums in these exponents are surreal field operations, not the ordinal multiplication used in the coding formula.
\end{example}

\begin{corollary}[Independent infinitesimal coordinates]\label{hset:kw:cor:infinitesimal}
Under the hypotheses of \cref{hset:kw:thm:double}, the numbers
\[
u_x=t_x^{-1}=\omega^{-e_x}
\]
are algebraically independent positive infinitesimals. They are not omnific integers.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
They are infinitesimal because $-e_x<0$. A polynomial relation among finitely many inverses, multiplied by a sufficiently large product of the corresponding $t_x$, would give a nonzero polynomial relation among the $t_x$. Their negative normal-form exponents exclude them from $\Oz$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Equivalent forms of coordinate universality]\label{hset:kw:thm:equivalences}
For a set $X$, the following conditions are equivalent over $\ZF$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $X\hookrightarrow\Pow(\alpha)$ for some ordinal $\alpha$;
\item $X\hookrightarrow\No$;
\item $X\hookrightarrow\Oz$;
\item there is a unital ring embedding $\Z[T_X]\hookrightarrow\Oz$;
\item there is an $\R$-algebra embedding $\R[T_X]\hookrightarrow\No$;
\item there is an $\R$-field embedding $\R(T_X)\hookrightarrow\No$;
\item there is an $X$-indexed algebraically independent family over $\R$ consisting of positive infinite omnific integers.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently, the universal closure over all sets $X$ of any of these assertions is exactly $\KWP_1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The equivalence of (i) and (ii) is \cref{hset:kw:thm:coding}. From (ii), \cref{hset:kw:thm:double} gives (iv)--(vii) and also (iii). Conversely, (iii) implies (ii) by inclusion. An embedding in (iv), (v), or (vi) assigns distinct values to the distinct indeterminates: otherwise it kills the nonzero polynomial $T_x-T_y$. Thus each yields (ii). Algebraic independence likewise gives distinct values, so (vii) yields (ii). Universal closure and \eqref{hset:kw:eq:kwp} prove the last statement.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Not an equivalence with full choice]
In pure $\ZF$, $\KWP_1$ is weaker than $\AC$; Cohen's first model is a standard example satisfying the former and not the latter, as reviewed in \cite{KaragilaSchilhan}. Thus these \emph{free-algebra} universality assertions do not recover the well-ordering theorem. They also do not yet assert anything about preserving an independently prescribed order on $X$ or on a field.
\end{remark}

\section{An optimal set-sized real-closed target}
\label{hset:kw:sec:universal}

The preceding theorem places all realizations in the proper class $\No$. It is useful, both mathematically and for formalization, to obtain a target that is an ordinary set and has exactly the required coding capacity.

Fix an infinite initial ordinal $\kappa$. Let
\[
\mathbb S_\kappa=\{-,+\}^{\kappa},\qquad
 t_s=\omega^{\omega^s}\quad(s\in \mathbb S_\kappa),
\]
where each $s$ is identified with its surreal value. Set
\begin{equation}\label{hset:kw:eq:Uk}
\mathcal R_\kappa=\R(t_s:s\in \mathbb S_\kappa),\qquad
U_\kappa=\{u\in\No:u\text{ is algebraic over }\mathcal R_\kappa\}.
\end{equation}
The notation on the right initially defines a class; the next proof shows that it is a set.

\subsection{A choice-free coding lemma}
\begin{lemma}[Finite syntax at a powerset level]\label{hset:kw:lem:syntax}
Let $\kappa$ be an infinite initial ordinal. Suppose that $D$ and $B$ inject into $\Pow(\kappa)$, that $D$ is linearly ordered, and that $B$ is a field with a fixed linear order on its underlying set. Then each of the following admits an injection into $\Pow(\kappa)$:
\[
B[T_D],\qquad B(T_D),\qquad
\text{any ordered algebraic extension of }B(T_D).
\]
For the last assertion the extension is a set, or is a definable class in which each polynomial has only finitely many roots and ordered root indexing applies.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Finite lists of subsets of $\kappa$ can be encoded by a subset of $\kappa$: first tag the list length and each entry in $\omega\times\kappa$, then use a fixed bijection $\omega\times\kappa\simeq\kappa$. These are well-orderable sets, so this bijection needs no choice. Naturals and finite auxiliary flags can be accommodated by additional tags.

Every finite subset of $D$ can be uniquely listed in increasing order. A monomial is therefore coded by an increasing finite variable list together with its positive integer exponents. Monomials are linearly ordered by comparing exponent vectors at the greatest variable where they differ. Every finite polynomial has a unique list of its nonzero terms in this order. Encoding these lists and their coefficients proves the assertion for polynomials.

A fraction requires more care. It would be invalid, without further argument, to infer that an arbitrary quotient of a $\Pow(\kappa)$-codable set is again $\Pow(\kappa)$-codable. Instead use the unique reduced representative
\begin{equation}\label{hset:kw:eq:reduced}
\frac{p}{q},\qquad \gcd(p,q)=1,\qquad\lc(q)=1,
\end{equation}
with $0$ represented as $0/1$.

Here leading coefficients are taken with respect to the preceding largest-variable monomial order. They exist because a polynomial has finitely many terms. The polynomial ring in an arbitrary set of variables over a field is a unique factorization domain in the finite sense needed here: put $p$ and $q$ in the ring generated by their finite union of variables and use finite-variable factorization. A factor cannot introduce a new variable, since degree in that variable is additive in a polynomial domain. Gcds computed in the finite subring therefore remain gcds in the full ring. Dividing out a gcd and scaling by the leading coefficient of the denominator produces \eqref{hset:kw:eq:reduced}. Uniqueness follows from coprimality and cancellation in a UFD. These are existence-and-uniqueness arguments, not selections from an unstructured family of equivalence classes. Thus a fraction is injected into a pair of polynomial codes.

Finally, an algebraic element $a$ of an ordered extension has a unique monic minimal polynomial over $B(T_D)$. Attach to it the position of $a$ among the finitely many roots of that polynomial \emph{in the extension}, in increasing order. Polynomial plus finite index uniquely determines $a$. This is an injection into finite lists of field elements with a natural-number tag, and hence into $\Pow(\kappa)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The order on $D$ is not required to be a well-order, and the monomial order is not claimed to well-order all monomials. Only the maximum of each finite support and compatibility with addition of exponent vectors are used. Finite-variable gcd theory can use a separate finite enumeration when proving existence; the normalization in \eqref{hset:kw:eq:reduced} makes the answer independent of that temporary enumeration.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Optimal code-level universal field]\label{hset:kw:thm:universal}
Over $\ZF$, for every infinite initial ordinal $\kappa$, the class $U_\kappa$ in \eqref{hset:kw:eq:Uk} is a set-sized real-closed subfield of $\No$ and
\begin{equation}\label{hset:kw:eq:Uk-card}
U_\kappa\simeq\Pow(\kappa).
\end{equation}
For every set $X$,
\begin{align}
X\hookrightarrow\Pow(\kappa)
&\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\R(T_X)\hookrightarrow_{\R}U_\kappa,\label{hset:kw:eq:univ-real}\\
X\hookrightarrow\Pow(\kappa)
&\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\Z[T_X]\hookrightarrow U_\kappa\cap\Oz.
\label{hset:kw:eq:univ-omnific}
\end{align}
The embeddings in \eqref{hset:kw:eq:univ-real} do not have to preserve a separately prescribed order on the source.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{hset:kw:thm:double}, the $t_s$ are algebraically independent over $\R$. The set of finite rational expressions in them is a set, and their evaluation is definable. Replacement gives the set $\mathcal R_\kappa$, together with an isomorphism from $\R(T_{\mathbb S_\kappa})$.

For each nonzero polynomial $p\in \mathcal R_\kappa[Y]$, there are at most $\deg p$ distinct roots in the field $\No$. Its definable class of roots is therefore a finite set. More explicitly, the assertion that a definable class has at most $n$ members allows its members to be collected using finite induction; this is not a choice principle. The roots form a linearly ordered finite set. The function that assigns to $(p,i)$ its $i$-th root, when present, and $0$ otherwise is a definable function on a set. Replacement shows that the union of all these root sets is a set. It is exactly $U_\kappa$.

Algebraic elements are closed under field operations, so $U_\kappa$ is an ordered field algebraic over $\mathcal R_\kappa$. If $a>0$ belongs to it, its positive square root in $\No$ is algebraic over $\mathcal R_\kappa$ and thus lies in $U_\kappa$. If an odd-degree polynomial has coefficients in $U_\kappa$, take one of its roots in the real-closed field $\No$. Its finitely many coefficients are algebraic over $\mathcal R_\kappa$, and algebraicity is transitive, so the root belongs to $U_\kappa$. The usual square-root/odd-degree criterion proves that $U_\kappa$ is real closed. This is a real closure of $\mathcal R_\kappa$ inside the chosen ordered field $\No$.

For the cardinal upper bound, $\mathbb S_\kappa\simeq\Pow(\kappa)$ by its definition and is linearly ordered by surreal order. The reals inject into $\Pow(\omega)$ by their rational Dedekind cuts, and thus into $\Pow(\kappa)$. Apply \cref{hset:kw:lem:syntax}, using canonical reduced fractions and minimal-polynomial/root-index codes. It gives an injection $U_\kappa\hookrightarrow\Pow(\kappa)$. Conversely, $s\mapsto t_s$ injects $\mathbb S_\kappa$ into $U_\kappa$. Cantor--Bernstein proves \eqref{hset:kw:eq:Uk-card}.

Suppose now $c:X\hookrightarrow\Pow(\kappa)$. Convert each code to its length-$\kappa$ sign sequence $s_x$, and send $T_x$ to $t_{s_x}$. Algebraic independence gives the embedding into $\mathcal R_\kappa\subseteq U_\kappa$, and integer polynomial values are omnific. This proves the forward implications in \eqref{hset:kw:eq:univ-real} and \eqref{hset:kw:eq:univ-omnific}. Either embedding sends distinct variables to distinct elements of $U_\kappa$; compose with its established code to obtain $X\hookrightarrow\Pow(\kappa)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{What optimality means here}
The theorem is sharp in a precise coding sense. Any set-sized target that contains an injective realization of $\mathbb S_\kappa$ many independent variables must admit an injection from $\Pow(\kappa)$. Our field attains that lower bound and is real closed. We are \emph{not} claiming that every ordered field whose underlying set injects into $\Pow(\kappa)$ order-embeds in $U_\kappa$.

Nor do we claim a sharp explicit birthday bound for $U_\kappa$. Such a bound exists by Replacement because $U_\kappa$ is a set. This qualitative fact must not be confused with an optimal formula for birthdays under multiplication, inversion, or taking algebraic roots.

\section{Prescribed order is a different universality problem}
\label{hset:kw:sec:order}

Let $L$ be a linearly ordered set. The free abelian group $\Z^{(L)}$ has a largest-variable order: for distinct $m,n$, let $\ell$ be the greatest member of the finite set on which they differ, and declare
\begin{equation}\label{hset:kw:eq:revlex}
m<n\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad m(\ell)<n(\ell).
\end{equation}
This is a translation-invariant linear order. Define an order on $\R[T_L]$ by saying that a nonzero polynomial is positive when the coefficient of its greatest monomial is positive. Extend it to the fraction field, denoted $F(L)$.

\begin{lemma}[Largest-term ordering]\label{hset:kw:lem:orderedfield}
The preceding construction makes $F(L)=\R(T_L)$ an ordered field over $\R$, without requiring a well-order of $L$. Its variables are positive and satisfy
\[
\ell<\ell'\quad\Longrightarrow\quad T_\ell<T_{\ell'}.
\]
Every order automorphism of $L$ induces an order automorphism of $F(L)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Translation invariance in \eqref{hset:kw:eq:revlex} is immediate because adding the same vector leaves the greatest differing coordinate and the sign of the difference unchanged. A polynomial has a finite nonempty set of monomials, hence a greatest one. If $m_0,n_0$ are the greatest monomials of nonzero $p,q$, then $m_0+n_0$ is the unique greatest exponent vector arising in their product: any strict decrease in either summand strictly decreases the sum. Consequently,
\[
\lm(pq)=\lm(p)+\lm(q),\qquad \lc(pq)=\lc(p)\lc(q).
\]
This proves closure of positive elements under multiplication. For addition, unequal leading monomials retain the greater positive term, while equal leading monomials have positive coefficient sum. Exactly one of $p,-p$ is positive for nonzero $p$. These observations give an ordered domain, whose order extends uniquely to its fraction field. The largest variable in $T_{\ell'}-T_\ell$ is $\ell'$, with coefficient $1$. All definitions are preserved by an order automorphism of $L$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact reduction of order universality]\label{hset:kw:thm:order}
For every linearly ordered set $L$, over $\ZF$,
\[
L\hookord\No
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
F(L)\hookord\No.
\]
In the forward direction the field embedding fixes $\R$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $j:L\hookord\No$. Use $t_\ell=\omega^{\omega^{j(\ell)}}$ as in \cref{hset:kw:thm:double}. For distinct exponent vectors $m,n$, the sign of
\[
E_m-E_n=\sum_\ell(m(\ell)-n(\ell))\omega^{j(\ell)}
\]
is determined by the greatest $\ell$ where the coefficient is nonzero, because $j$ preserves order and finite normal-form signs are determined by the greatest exponent. Thus $m<n$ in \eqref{hset:kw:eq:revlex} exactly when $E_m<E_n$ in $\No$. The sign of an evaluated polynomial is its largest-term coefficient, so the resulting polynomial and fraction-field embeddings preserve order.

Conversely, restricting any ordered embedding $F(L)\to\No$ to the strictly increasing family of variables gives an ordered injection of $L$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Well-orderable domains]\label{hset:kw:prop:wellordered}
Every linear order whose underlying set is well-orderable order-embeds into $\No$ in $\ZF$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Fix one well-ordering and its enumeration $(x_\xi)_{\xi<\theta}$. Recursively assign to $x_\xi$ the simplest surreal between the images of earlier elements below it and the images of earlier elements above it. Those two collections are sets, are separated by the induction hypothesis, and the simplest choice is unique. Transfinite recursion and Replacement construct a set graph of length $\theta$. No new choice is made at any stage. The result preserves all comparisons.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[A gap that must not be silently bridged]
An injection of the underlying set of $L$ into $\Pow(\alpha)$ gives it \emph{some} surreal-induced order. It does not say that an already specified order on $L$ is respected. Thus \cref{hset:kw:thm:equivalences} is not, by itself, a proof that $\KWP_1$ implies ordered universality for all linear orders or all ordered fields. We leave that implication as a separately stated research question rather than conflating it with the proved equivalence.
\end{remark}

\section{A convex-resolution criterion for ordered embeddings}
\label{hset:kw:sec:resolutions}

There is a choice-free combinatorial certificate for the missing prescribed-order requirement.

\begin{definition}
A \emph{ternary convex resolution of length $\theta$} of a linear order $L$ is a sequence $(E_\xi)_{\xi\le\theta}$ of equivalence relations on $L$ such that:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item $E_0=L\times L$ and $E_\theta$ is equality;
\item the relations refine with increasing $\xi$, and every equivalence class is convex in $L$;
\item at a limit $\lambda\le\theta$, $E_\lambda=\bigcap_{\xi<\lambda}E_\xi$;
\item every $E_\xi$-class is partitioned into at most three $E_{\xi+1}$-classes.
\end{enumerate}
A binary resolution is defined by replacing three with two.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Order-coding certificate]\label{hset:kw:thm:resolution}
For a set-sized linear order $L$, the following are equivalent over $\ZF$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $L$ order-embeds into $\No$;
\item $L$ has a ternary convex resolution of some ordinal length;
\item $L$ has a binary convex resolution of some ordinal length.
\end{enumerate}
A ternary resolution of length $\theta$ produces an order embedding into sign sequences of exact length $2\cdot\theta$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $j:L\hookord\No$. Bound the birthdays of its range by an ordinal $\theta$. Pad each sign sequence to length $\theta$ with the symbol $0$ after it ends, using $-<0<+$. Surreal comparison is precisely lexicographic comparison of these padded strings. Let $xE_\xi y$ mean that their first $\xi$ symbols agree. Prefix classes in a lexicographic order are convex; each splits into at most three next-symbol classes, limit relations are intersections, and equality of the whole padded strings gives $x=y$. Thus (i) implies (ii).

Now suppose a ternary resolution is given. Inside each $E_\xi$-class, its finitely many $E_{\xi+1}$-classes inherit a linear order. Label them by $0,1,2$ according to their number of predecessors; unused labels do not matter. This requires no selection of class representatives. For $x\in L$, let $d_x(\xi)$ be the label of the child containing $x$.

If $x\ne y$, there is a least stage at which they cease to be equivalent. It is a successor stage: at a limit, the intersection condition would give an earlier separating stage. Thus their label strings first differ at the corresponding coordinate. Convexity says that $x<y$ precisely when the first differing label of $x$ is smaller. Therefore $x\mapsto d_x$ order-embeds $L$ into $\{0,1,2\}^{\theta}$ in lexicographic order.

Replace each digit by a two-sign block
\[
0\mapsto --,\qquad 1\mapsto -+,\qquad 2\mapsto ++.
\]
Concatenating $\theta$ blocks gives a sign sequence of length $2\cdot\theta$. The replacement preserves the first differing coordinate comparison, hence gives (i). It also gives a binary convex resolution by equality of prefixes of these fixed-length sign sequences. Thus (ii) implies (iii), and (iii) implies (ii) trivially.
\end{proof}

The theorem does not identify an optimal resolution length. Its value is to isolate a checkable \emph{order-sensitive} certificate, distinct from the unconstrained powerset coding of the underlying set.

\section{Orbit factorization of polynomial representations}
\label{hset:kw:sec:orbits}

The negative theory begins with a general algebraic lemma that does not mention surreals, atoms, or choice principles.

Let a group $G_0$ act on a set $X$. It acts on $k[T_X]$ by permuting variables and fixing the coefficient field $k$. Let $X/G_0$ denote the set of its orbits. Put
\begin{equation}\label{hset:kw:eq:orbitideal}
I_{G_0}=(T_x-T_y:x,y\text{ belong to the same }G_0\text{-orbit}).
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Orbit quotient and factorization]\label{hset:kw:thm:orbit}
There is a canonical $k$-algebra isomorphism
\[
k[T_X]/I_{G_0}\ \cong\ k[T_{X/G_0}].
\]
If $D$ is a $k$-algebra on which $G_0$ acts trivially and a $k$-algebra homomorphism $\phi:k[T_X]\to D$ satisfies
\[
\phi(h\cdot p)=\phi(p)\qquad(h\in G_0),
\]
then it factors uniquely through this quotient. If $X/G_0$ has $q<\omega$ members and $D$ is a field, then
\begin{equation}\label{hset:kw:eq:orbitbound}
\trdeg_k\Frac(\im\phi)\le q.
\end{equation}
Equality holds precisely when the $q$ orbit values are algebraically independent over $k$; in that case $\ker\phi=I_{G_0}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The orbit map $x\mapsto[x]$ induces a surjective homomorphism
\[
\pi:k[T_X]\longrightarrow k[T_{X/G_0}].
\]
It kills $I_{G_0}$. Conversely, in $k[T_X]/I_{G_0}$ all variables from one orbit have the same residue. Assign to an orbit this uniquely determined residue; existence follows from the nonemptiness of the orbit, and uniqueness follows from the defining relations. This is a function defined by a unique-value condition, not a choice of representatives. The polynomial universal property gives an inverse to the induced map from the quotient. The isomorphism follows.

Invariance gives $\phi(T_x)=\phi(T_{hx})$, so $I_{G_0}\subseteq\ker\phi$ and the unique factorization follows. With $q$ orbits the image field is generated over $k$ by their $q$ values. Its transcendence degree is at most $q$. By finite field theory it equals $q$ exactly when those values are algebraically independent. Equivalently, the factored map from $k[T_{X/G_0}]$ is injective, which is exactly the assertion that the original kernel is $I_{G_0}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Obstruction for fraction fields]\label{hset:kw:cor:fieldcollapse}
If a $G_0$-orbit in $X$ has two members, there is no invariant unital field homomorphism $k(T_X)\to D$ to a field with trivial $G_0$-action.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Such a map would kill the nonzero element $T_x-T_y$ for two variables in that orbit. A unital homomorphism from a field has zero kernel.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Coinvariants, not invariant polynomials]
The quotient in \cref{hset:kw:thm:orbit} identifies variables. It is not the invariant subring $k[T_X]^{G_0}$, whose structure can be quite different. Confusing these two constructions would invalidate the transcendence bound.
\end{remark}

\section{Sharp support bounds in two permutation models}
\label{hset:kw:sec:fm}

\subsection{The model framework}
Start with a ground universe of $\ZFA+\AC$ with a set $A$ of atoms. A permutation group $G$ on $A$ acts recursively on sets, fixing pure sets. Let the normal filter of subgroups be generated by the pointwise stabilizers
\[
\Fix_G(S)=\{g\in G:g(a)=a\text{ for every }a\in S\}
\]
of finite subsets $S\subseteq A$. The hereditarily symmetric sets form the usual full Fraenkel--Mostowski model $W$. The permutation-model construction and its set-theoretic setting are standard; \cite[Section 3.1]{GlazerYao} provides a recent primary-source presentation of this machinery.

Every set of $W$ has some finite atom support $S$: it is fixed by every member of $\Fix_G(S)$. Every pure ground set belongs to $W$, and the pure part is unchanged. In particular, the reals, ordinals, and surreals of $W$ are pure. Each atom permutation fixes each of them individually.

A map $\phi$ in $W$ with pure values and support $S$ therefore satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{hset:kw:eq:symmetry}
\phi(g\cdot x)=g\cdot\phi(x)=\phi(x)
\qquad(g\in\Fix_G(S)).
\end{equation}
For polynomial domains the induced action permutes indeterminates. This turns the algebraic factorization theorem into a precise internal obstruction.

\subsection{The basic Fraenkel model}
Take $A$ infinite and $G=\Sym(A)$, with finite supports.

\begin{theorem}[The $s+1$ bound]\label{hset:kw:thm:fraenkel}
In the basic Fraenkel model, let
\[
\phi:\R[T_A]\longrightarrow\No
\]
be an internal $\R$-algebra homomorphism with atom support $S$, where $|S|=s<\omega$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{hset:kw:eq:fraenkelbound}
\trdeg_\R\Frac(\im\phi)\le s+1.
\end{equation}
All variables indexed by $A\setminus S$ have the same image. For every finite $S$ the bound is attained by a homomorphism supported by $S$. No such homomorphism is injective, and there is no internal unital field homomorphism $\R(T_A)\to\No$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The stabilizer $G_0=\Fix_G(S)$ has exactly $s+1$ orbits on $A$: the $s$ singletons in $S$ and the infinite complement $A\setminus S$. Any two atoms in that complement can be swapped while all of $S$ is fixed. Equation \eqref{hset:kw:eq:symmetry} and \cref{hset:kw:thm:orbit} prove the collapse and the bound. Two distinct atoms outside $S$ give a nonzero difference in the kernel. The field assertion follows from \cref{hset:kw:cor:fieldcollapse}.

For sharpness, choose a listing $S=\{a_1,\ldots,a_s\}$ of this one finite set. Assign distinct members $z_0,\ldots,z_s$ of a pure algebraically independent family, for example
\[
z_i=\omega^{\omega^i},\qquad 0\le i\le s,
\]
by sending $T_{a_i}$ to $z_i$ and every variable outside $S$ to $z_0$. This assignment and its polynomial evaluation graph are supported by $S$ and hereditarily symmetric: each individual input polynomial involves only finitely many atoms, while every output is pure. Thus the map belongs to $W$. Its orbit values are algebraically independent, giving equality in \eqref{hset:kw:eq:fraenkelbound}. This construction uses only the finite choice of a listing of one given finite set, which is available without $\AC$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The ordered Mostowski model}
Now take an atom set $A$ with a dense linear order without endpoints externally isomorphic to $(\Q,<)$, and let
\[
G=\Aut(A,<)
\]
with the same finite-support filter. The ordered set $(A,<)$ itself belongs to the symmetric model: its order relation is invariant under the whole group.

\begin{lemma}[Stabilizer orbit count]\label{hset:kw:lem:intervalorbits}
If $S=\{a_1<\cdots<a_s\}\subseteq A$, the pointwise stabilizer of $S$ has exactly $2s+1$ orbits:
\[
\{a_1\},\ldots,\{a_s\},\quad
(-\infty,a_1),(a_1,a_2),\ldots,(a_s,\infty).
\]
For $s=0$, there is just the orbit $A$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Order preservation and fixation of $S$ prevent movement between the displayed pieces. Within any one interval, any chosen atom can be sent to any other by an order automorphism fixing all the endpoints in $S$. To justify existence, work in the ground universe with its fixed external countable presentation of the dense order. Extend the finite increasing partial map consisting of the fixed points of $S$ and the proposed movement by the standard back-and-forth construction. At each alternating stage put the next unused domain or range point into the corresponding nonempty interval. Density and absence of endpoints ensure success. The resulting bijection is order preserving and fixes $S$. Thus each interval is a single orbit. This external argument computes the chosen permutation group; it does not put a countable enumeration of $A$ into the symmetric model.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The $2s+1$ bound]\label{hset:kw:thm:mostowski}
In the ordered Mostowski model, every internal $\R$-algebra homomorphism
\[
\phi:\R[T_A]\longrightarrow\No
\]
with a support $S$ of size $s$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{hset:kw:eq:mostowskibound}
\trdeg_\R\Frac(\im\phi)\le 2s+1.
\end{equation}
For every finite $S$, equality is attained by a map supported by $S$. No such map is injective, and there is no internal unital field homomorphism $\R(T_A)\to\No$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{hset:kw:thm:orbit} to the $2s+1$ orbits of \cref{hset:kw:lem:intervalorbits}, using \eqref{hset:kw:eq:symmetry}. Every interval contains distinct atoms, so the kernel contains a nonzero variable difference; the field assertion follows as before.

The orbits themselves have a canonical finite order, alternating intervals and singletons. Assign to these ordered orbits the algebraically independent pure values $z_0,\ldots,z_{2s}$ from the proof of \cref{hset:kw:thm:fraenkel}. The resulting evaluation map is supported by $S$, belongs to the symmetric model, and has algebraically independent orbit values. This attains the bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Exact kernels at maximum dimension]\label{hset:kw:cor:sharpkernel}
For each of the sharpness examples above, the entire kernel is generated by differences between variables in the same stabilizer orbit. There are no further polynomial relations among the orbit values.
\end{corollary}
This follows from the last clause of \cref{hset:kw:thm:orbit}. The upper bounds apply to any chosen support, not necessarily a smallest support. As the support size varies, maps of arbitrarily large finite image-field transcendence degree exist, but no one internal map has infinite image-field transcendence degree.

\subsection{Why pure coordinates recover well-orderability in these models}
\begin{proposition}\label{hset:kw:prop:purewellorder}
In either full permutation model just described, for any internal set $X$ the following are equivalent: $X$ injects into a pure set; $X$ is well-orderable internally; $X$ injects into the internal $\No$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
A pure set belongs to the unchanged pure ground universe, which satisfies choice. It has a pure well-order, and that well-order also belongs to the symmetric model. An internal injection into the pure set therefore pulls back an internal well-order of $X$. Conversely, a well-order identifies $X$ with an ordinal, which is pure and embeds into $\No$.

An injection into $\No$ has a set-sized range; its range is a set of pure objects and is itself pure. Thus it gives an injection into a pure set. Alternatively, apply \cref{hset:kw:thm:coding} and use the purity of $\Pow(\alpha)$ in the full permutation model.
\end{proof}
There is no conflict with $\KWP_1$ being strictly weaker than choice in pure $\ZF$. The preceding proposition makes an additional assumption: the unchanged pure part of these particular atom models already satisfies choice. It is not a theorem about arbitrary models of $\ZFA$.

\section{An ordered field with all finite stages embeddable but no global embedding}
\label{hset:kw:sec:counterexample}

\begin{theorem}[Internal ordered-field counterexample]\label{hset:kw:thm:orderedcounter}
In the ordered Mostowski model $W$, the set-sized ordered field
\[
F(A)=\R(T_a:a\in A)
\]
with the largest-variable order of \cref{hset:kw:lem:orderedfield} has no unital field homomorphism into $\No^W$, even when order preservation is not required. Nevertheless, for every finite $J\subseteq A$, the subfield $F(J)$ has an internal ordered $\R$-embedding into $\No^W$, and
\[
F(A)=\bigcup_{J\in[A]^{<\omega}}F(J).
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The polynomial ring, fraction-field equivalence relation, and field operations are invariant under every order automorphism of $A$. Each expression is hereditarily symmetric, since it uses only finitely many atoms and pure real coefficients. The largest-variable order is invariant by \cref{hset:kw:lem:orderedfield}. Hence the ordered field is an object of $W$.

An internal field homomorphism into $\No^W$ would have a finite atom support. Restricting it to $\R[T_A]$ gives a map whose values are fixed by the stabilizer, whether or not the homomorphism fixes every real. In particular, two variables from one nonsingleton interval orbit have the same image. Their nonzero difference cannot be killed by a unital field homomorphism. This is a contradiction.

For a finite $J=\{a_1<\cdots<a_n\}$, the order-preserving map $a_i\mapsto i$ is a set map supported by $J$. The construction of \cref{hset:kw:thm:order} gives an ordered embedding $F(J)\to\No^W$. Its graph is supported by $J$ and hereditarily symmetric, so it belongs to $W$. Every rational expression uses only finitely many variables, proving the union identity.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Scope of the counterexample]
This proves a failure of ordered-field universality in a model of $\ZFA$. It is \emph{not} asserted to be a counterexample in pure $\ZF$. Removing atoms while preserving a statement about embeddings into the entire internal surreal class requires a separate transfer argument; none is supplied here. The external countability of $A$ also causes no contradiction: an external enumeration can produce an external embedding, but such an enumeration and embedding do not belong to $W$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The exact coherence obstruction}
\begin{theorem}[Gluing compatible coordinate embeddings]\label{hset:kw:thm:coherence}
Let $L$ be a set-sized linear order in $\ZF$ or $\ZFA$. A global ordered $\R$-embedding $F(L)\to\No$ exists if and only if there is a set-indexed family
\[
(\phi_J:F(J)\hookord\No)_{J\in[L]^{<\omega}}
\]
of ordered $\R$-embeddings satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{hset:kw:eq:compat}
J\subseteq K\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\phi_K|_{F(J)}=\phi_J.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Restrict a global embedding to obtain the family; Replacement gives its set code. Conversely, take the union of the graphs of the $\phi_J$. This union is a set. If an element lies in both $F(J)$ and $F(K)$, compare both maps with $\phi_{J\cup K}$ using \eqref{hset:kw:eq:compat}; their values agree. Thus the union is a function on $F(L)$. Any finitely many arguments and their algebraic relations lie in one $F(J)$, so the union is an order-preserving field homomorphism. The same observation shows injectivity.
\end{proof}

For the field in \cref{hset:kw:thm:orderedcounter}, each finite stage has an ordered embedding but no compatible family exists internally. This is not a claim that first-order compactness fails, and it does not concern the existence of a model of a finitely satisfiable first-order theory. The target is the particular internal class $\No$, and the obstruction is the demanded compatibility and internal existence of its coordinate map.

\subsection{Finite polynomial tests can all succeed}
\begin{proposition}[Finite separation without a faithful representation]\label{hset:kw:prop:finite-separation}
In either of the two permutation models, every finite family of nonzero polynomials in $\R[T_A]$ is simultaneously nonzero under some internal $\R$-algebra homomorphism to $\No$. Nevertheless, no one internal homomorphism is injective.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let $J$ be the finite union of the variable supports of the given polynomials. Assign algebraically independent pure surreal values to the variables in $J$, and assign a fixed pure value, say $0$, to every other variable. The evaluation map is supported by $J$ and belongs to the model. Its restriction to $\R[T_J]$ is injective by algebraic independence, so it preserves the nonzeroness of every polynomial in the family. Nonexistence of an injective map follows from \cref{hset:kw:thm:fraenkel,hset:kw:thm:mostowski}.
\end{proof}

Equivalently, for each nonzero polynomial there exists a map not killing it, but no single map avoids all nonzero kernels. One may describe the intersection of all these kernels as zero, provided that phrase is read as a first-order quantification over maps, not as the formation of a set of all maps into a proper class.

\section{Proof audit and relation to the existing repository}
\label{hset:kw:sec:audit}

\subsection{Dependency boundary}
The main logical dependencies are short:
\[
\begin{gathered}
\text{sign sequences + Replacement}
\ \Longrightarrow\ \text{ordinal-code equivalence},\\
\text{finite normal forms + monomial multiplication}
\ \Longrightarrow\ \text{free-algebra equivalence},\\
\text{canonical fraction/root codes + real closedness}
\ \Longrightarrow\ U_\kappa,\\
\text{polynomial orbit quotient + finite atom support}
\ \Longrightarrow\ \text{sharp collapse bounds}.
\end{gathered}
\]
The negative construction does not assume any speculative research claim from ProveIt's other subjects. The positive algebraic construction uses only the standard surreal interface in \cref{hset:kw:sec:background}. Thus an unrelated error in an unrefereed repository report would not affect the mathematical dependency chain here.

\subsection{A ledger of theorem status}
\begin{longtable}{@{}P{0.24\textwidth}P{0.40\textwidth}P{0.29\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Result & Mathematical status in this manuscript & Novelty / checking status \\
\midrule
\endhead
Ordinal-code equivalence & Direct proof in $\ZF$ and $\ZFA$; exact birthday capacity included & Elementary consequence of sign coding; no priority claim \\
\addlinespace
Double-monomial family & Explicit finite-normal-form proof; free rings and fields calibrated & Classical ingredients; combined formulation developed here \\
\addlinespace
Optimal field $U_\kappa$ & Set existence, real closedness, and two cardinal injections proved without quotient-choice shortcut & Candidate synthesis/refinement; independent review needed \\
\addlinespace
Convex resolution & Necessary and sufficient order-coding certificate proved & Related to ordered coding; historical novelty not established \\
\addlinespace
Orbit factorization & Full algebraic proof, with finite-dimensional equality criterion & Standard universal-property mechanism \\
\addlinespace
$s+1$ and $2s+1$ bounds & Exact stabilizer counts, upper bounds, and sharp constructions & Quantitative application developed here; priority unverified \\
\addlinespace
Ordered-field failure & Internal counterexample in the stated $\ZFA$ permutation model & No automatic pure-$\ZF$ transfer asserted \\
\addlinespace
Coherent gluing & Set-graph union proof; finite separation distinguished & Standard gluing mechanism, applied to fixed target \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

No theorem in this manuscript is presented as already kernel-checked. The supplied finite tests detect mistakes in finite encodings and algebraic identities but do not prove a theorem about all ordinals, arbitrary models, or proper classes.

\subsection{The choice issue in a Lean audit}
ProveIt's surreal README reports a standard-axiom audit allowing \texttt{propext}, \texttt{Quot.sound}, and \texttt{Classical.choice}; it maps checked statements separately from research claims \cite{ProveItSurreal}. That is valuable evidence about the declared Lean foundation. It is not a certificate that a theorem is provable over \emph{object-theoretic} $\ZF$ without choice.

There are two defensible routes for the present results. One can formalize enough constructive set and ordinal coding to avoid the choice axiom in the relevant dependency chains. Alternatively, one can work in Lean's ordinary classical metatheory while explicitly formalizing a model of $\ZF$ or $\ZFA$ and proving that the relevant internal statements follow from the designated object-theoretic axioms. Metatheoretic choice then does not silently become internal choice; the semantic or syntactic theorem must enforce that distinction.

A theorem about every type in a universe is also not literally a theorem about the proper class of all sets. The birthday-bounded targets $\No_{\le\alpha}$ and the fields $U_\kappa$ offer useful set-sized interfaces, but a formalization must still record which universe contains each type and which internal sets it represents.

\subsection{A proposed module decomposition}
The following are suggested module responsibilities, not names of files already implemented in the repository.
\begin{description}[style=nextline,leftmargin=0pt,labelsep=0pt]
\item[OrdinalSignCoding]
Define variable-length sign functions, the definedness code \eqref{hset:kw:eq:signcode}, its decoder, and fixed-length subset encoding. Prove the birthday-capacity theorem with explicit ordinal pairings.
\item[DoubleMonomialIndependence]
Abstract the finite-normal-form leading-term property. Prove that distinct monomials are linearly independent and that the double-monomial substitution is an injective multivariate-polynomial homomorphism. Derive the omnific range condition separately from the real-coefficient assertion.
\item[CanonicalFieldCodes]
Normalize multivariate fractions by gcd and leading coefficient, code algebraic roots by minimal polynomial and root index, and construct the set-sized relative real closure.
\item[OrbitPolynomialQuotient]
Formalize the group action on variables, the orbit-indexed polynomial algebra, the quotient isomorphism, and finite transcendence bounds. This module can be independent of surreal theory.
\item[InternalSymmetryBridge]
Formalize finite-support evaluation into a pure target inside a specified permutation model. The semantic statement that an internal map has a finite support is indispensable; a theorem only about an externally chosen group action is not yet the model-theoretic conclusion.
\item[OrderedFreeFieldAndCoherence]
Construct the largest-variable order using finite supports, prove the ordered embedding reduction, and formalize gluing of compatible finite-stage maps by a set union.
\end{description}

\section{Further research questions and concrete next targets}
\label{hset:kw:sec:questions}

The following are proposed research directions. Except where a cited paper explicitly supplies a status, they should not be read as claims that the literature has left each formulation open. A literature check and, where appropriate, contact with specialists should precede any priority claim.

\begin{question}[Pure-set prescribed-order universality]\label{hset:kw:q:prescribed}
What is the exact strength over $\ZF$ of the assertion that every set-sized linear order embeds in $\No$? In particular, can one construct a pure-$\ZF$ analogue of the ordered-field counterexample in \cref{hset:kw:thm:orderedcounter}?
\end{question}
The atom proof isolates the desired obstruction but does not transfer it. A useful first target is a pure symmetric extension containing a linearly ordered set with no ordinal-length convex resolution. One must analyze its \emph{internal} sign sequences and maps rather than merely preserve a low-rank atom surrogate.

\begin{question}[Does $\KWP_1$ supply an order-sensitive code?]\label{hset:kw:q:kwporder}
Does $\KWP_1$ imply that every linear order admits a binary convex resolution of some ordinal length?
\end{question}
By \cref{hset:kw:thm:resolution}, this is exactly the missing implication from coordinate universality to prescribed-order universality. A possible approach is to study a given order on a subset of $\Pow(\alpha)$ and ask whether membership coordinates can be converted into a continuous sequence of convex refinements. Arbitrary coordinate cuts need not be convex, which is the concrete obstruction to the naive argument.

\begin{question}[Optimal convex-resolution height]\label{hset:kw:q:resolutionheight}
For an order that embeds in $\No$, how does its least binary resolution length compare with the least birthday bound of an ordered embedding?
\end{question}
The proof gives conversions with a factor of two at the ternary-to-binary step, but no optimality statement. Fixed-length and variable-length sign representations should be distinguished. Finite chains, ordinal orders, reversed ordinal orders, and lexicographic products provide initial test cases.

\begin{question}[Explicit birthday bounds for $U_\kappa$]\label{hset:kw:q:birthdaybounds}
Find useful explicit ordinal bounds for the birthdays of all members of the field $U_\kappa$ defined in \eqref{hset:kw:eq:Uk}.
\end{question}
Existence is immediate from its set-sizedness, but an effective bound requires controlling the two monomial operations and algebraic root formation. A conservative closure ordinal for a specified finite-expression grammar is a reasonable first goal; a sharp product-birthday formula should not be assumed.

\begin{question}[More general fields at a fixed code level]\label{hset:kw:q:fields}
Which additional hypotheses on a prescribed ordered field $K$ with $K\hookrightarrow\Pow(\kappa)$ guarantee an ordered embedding into $U_\kappa$ or into a canonically enlarged target of the same coding capacity?
\end{question}
Free rational-function fields with induced orders are handled here. Algebraic ordered extensions, valuations with order-embeddable value groups, and fields given by a coherent tower of simple ordered extensions are natural successive cases. The existence of a chosen transcendence basis must not be silently assumed.

\begin{question}[Higher Kinna--Wagner levels]\label{hset:kw:q:higherkw}
Is there a natural algebraic representation problem, not merely a relabelling of the definition, whose exact strength is $\KWP_n$ for $n>1$?
\end{question}
Ordinary surreal elements have one-level ordinal subset codes. Higher levels might arise from unordered families of coordinates, quotient presentations, or structured moduli. The challenge is to exhibit algebraically meaningful invariants whose coding genuinely requires the extra powerset level.

\begin{question}[Orbit profiles beyond the two examples]\label{hset:kw:q:orbitprofiles}
For a permutation group $G$ on atoms, how precisely does the function
\[
S\longmapsto |A/\Fix_G(S)|
\]
control the transcendence spectra of pure-target polynomial representations?
\end{question}
The finite-orbit case is settled by \cref{hset:kw:thm:orbit}. Infinite orbit sets introduce a new coding issue: a sharp realization requires enough independent pure coordinates indexed by the orbit set. Applying \cref{hset:kw:thm:equivalences} to that quotient suggests an exact two-part criterion, combining orbit count and ordinal-code availability.

\begin{question}[Minimal supports and essential dimension]\label{hset:kw:q:minimalsupports}
Can one recover a lower bound on the least atom-support size of a map from the transcendence degree of its image, and when is the bound exact for every map?
\end{question}
Our inequalities immediately give $s\ge d-1$ in the basic model and $s\ge\lceil(d-1)/2\rceil$ in the ordered model. They are sharp as existential bounds. A finer classification would distinguish maps with redundant support from those whose support is essential and relate this to the structure of their kernels.

\begin{question}[Real closures under symmetry]\label{hset:kw:q:realclosures}
What is the strongest ordered-algebraic enlargement of the source in \cref{hset:kw:thm:orderedcounter} that can be constructed internally while retaining an explicit pure-target obstruction?
\end{question}
An externally selected real closure is not automatically an internal object with the desired action. A canonical construction through real-algebraic root codes, if it can be made equivariant over the ordered field, is a promising route. Any such extension would inherit nonembeddability by restriction to the original rational-function field.

\begin{question}[Choice strength of compatible local embeddings]\label{hset:kw:q:compatible}
For which families of subfields does the existence of an individual embedding for every member imply a compatible family of embeddings into a fixed real-closed target?
\end{question}
The union theorem proves what compatibility would accomplish, not that it can be obtained. Separate chains with specified extension maps, directed systems indexed by finite subsets, and unrestricted systems. A useful target is an exact weak-choice principle for one sharply specified family rather than an undifferentiated appeal to compactness.

\begin{question}[Residual separation and inverse limits of representations]\label{hset:kw:q:inverselimits}
Can the finite-separation phenomenon of \cref{hset:kw:prop:finite-separation} be organized as a failure of a natural inverse-limit existence principle, with a precise choice calibration?
\end{question}
For each finite variable set, consider its space of faithful coordinate assignments and restriction maps. The naive inverse system need not have surjective transition maps with a fixed birthday bound. Understanding that issue is essential before invoking any inverse-limit or compactness theorem.

\begin{question}[Object-theoretic formal verification]\label{hset:kw:q:formal}
Can the $\ZF$ equivalence and the $\ZFA$ counterexample be checked in one repository development with an explicit audit of internal versus metatheoretic choice?
\end{question}
The shortest first milestone is the orbit polynomial quotient plus the finite-normal-form independence lemma. The hard foundational milestone is a bridge from hereditarily symmetric internal maps to stabilizer invariance, together with set-coded surreal targets. Such a development would test the semantics of the statement, not just the algebra of the displayed proof.

\section{Reproducibility and limits of the computational evidence}
\label{hset:kw:sec:reproduce}

The accompanying Python program, shipped as \path{code/11-choiceless-universality-verify_finite.py} (delivered as \path{verify_finite.py}), uses only the standard library and a fixed random seed. It checks the finite definedness encoding, fixed-length ternary-to-binary order encoding, distinct exponent-vector substitution, translation invariance of largest-variable order, leading terms of polynomial products, and finite permutation-orbit quotient identities. It also checks simultaneous evaluation as a ring homomorphism by exact integer arithmetic. The supplied deterministic run passed 20,595 assertions; this count is evidence only about the finite checks just described.

These checks are deliberately finite. A finite linearly ordered set has only the identity order automorphism, so its automorphism group is \emph{not} a valid finite simulation of the ordered Mostowski model. The program does not pretend otherwise. Infinite interval transitivity is proved by the ground-model back-and-forth argument in \cref{hset:kw:lem:intervalorbits}; ordinal recursion, class size, and model existence are mathematical arguments, not outputs of the script.

In the delivered layout the local checks were reproduced by
\begin{verbatim}
python3 verify_finite.py
\end{verbatim}
followed by two \texttt{pdflatex} passes of the delivered manuscript. Without
\texttt{--output} the program writes \path{verification_results.json} beside
itself; with the shipped names, run it on a copy or pass an explicit output
path outside this directory (the report's \path{README.md} gives the
commands). The recorded run is shipped as
\path{data/11-choiceless-universality-verification_results.json}. For this merge
the program was rerun with Python 3.14.4 on a copy: it passed and reproduced the
recorded file exactly, apart from line endings. The delivered archive contained
the source, compiled PDF, verification script and its result file, a README, a
source-and-status note and a checksum file; of these only the script, the
result file and the source-and-status note
(\path{11-choiceless-universality-SOURCES_AND_STATUS.md}) are shipped
(\cref{hset:kw:sec:provenance}). No font files are distributed.

\section{Conclusion of source 11, checklist and provenance}
\label{hset:kw:sec:provenance}

\subsection{Conclusion}
Surreal universality is not one assertion. For unconstrained independent variables, the exact obstruction is ordinal subset coding, and double Conway monomials convert that code into omnific algebra. This conversion can be carried out inside an optimally sized real-closed field at each infinite ordinal code level. When an existing order must be preserved, a convex resolution, rather than a mere injection of underlying sets, supplies the relevant certificate.

In permutation models the obstruction becomes quantitative. A pure-valued map supported by finitely many atoms cannot distinguish variables that its stabilizer moves into one another. The number of stabilizer orbits is therefore a sharp upper bound on the independent transcendence retained by the image. In the ordered model this produces an actual ordered field with no internal surreal embedding, while every finite coordinate stage embeds and every finite polynomial test can succeed.

The resulting program is concrete: distinguish coding from order, distinguish external from internal maps, and distinguish finite satisfiability from coherent realization. These distinctions are both mathematically substantive and well suited to a proof-assistant audit of choiceless surreal algebra.

\subsection{A compact checklist for reviewing the main proofs}
\label{hset:kw:app:checklist}
A referee or formalizer can focus first on the following points.
\begin{enumerate}
\item In \cref{hset:kw:thm:coding}, check both definedness and signs in the code, the side of ordinal multiplication, and the Replacement bound for the image birthdays.
\item In \cref{hset:kw:thm:double}, check that the inner monomials are linearly independent, not merely distinct; this is what separates evaluated exponent vectors. Check the positive-exponent condition separately for the omnific conclusion.
\item In \cref{hset:kw:lem:syntax}, check uniqueness of normalized fractions in the full arbitrary-variable polynomial ring. A surjection from a coded syntax set is not enough for the cardinal upper bound without a canonical code of each value.
\item In \cref{hset:kw:thm:universal}, verify collection of the algebraic roots as a set, and use the root index in the actual ordered extension. No choice of one root from each polynomial is made.
\item In \cref{hset:kw:thm:order}, compare the two leading-term orders rather than assuming an arbitrary algebraic embedding preserves order.
\item In \cref{hset:kw:thm:resolution}, use continuity at limits to justify that a first separation occurs at a successor stage.
\item In \cref{hset:kw:thm:orbit}, distinguish the orbit quotient from the invariant subring, and avoid selecting one representative from every orbit.
\item In \cref{hset:kw:thm:fraenkel,hset:kw:thm:mostowski}, apply atom support to the \emph{graph of the map}; pure values alone do not constrain an external map.
\item In \cref{hset:kw:thm:orderedcounter}, verify that the source ordered field is an internal object and that the forbidden map is internal. Do not transfer the conclusion from $\ZFA$ to $\ZF$ without proof.
\item In \cref{hset:kw:thm:coherence}, require a single set-coded compatible family; separate existence of each finite-stage map is weaker.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Non-claims of source 11}
\label{hset:kw:sub:nonclaims}
Every limitation stated by source 11, in its manuscript or in its
source-and-status note, is kept; most appear at their point of use. Collected:
the manuscript is AI-generated and unrefereed and is not a Lean- or
Rocq-checked development; the novelty of its exact formulations is not
established, and its twelve questions are proposed targets, not catalogued
open problems; it claims no solution of Glazer and Yao's reflection questions
or of another named problem; the atom-model counterexample is a result in ZFA,
and no ZF transfer is supplied or asserted; universality for prescribed orders
does not follow from $\KWP_1$ in what is proved; no sharp birthday bound for
$U_\kappa$ is claimed; $\KWP_1$ and its coding form are classical
(Karagila--Schilhan), as are sign coding, normal forms, the polynomial and UFD
facts, real-closed-field facts and the permutation-model construction; the
finite script checks encodings and algebraic identities only, does not
construct symmetric models, prove independence or check real closedness, and
does not use finite chains as a model of $\Aut(\Q,<)$; the proposed modules are
a roadmap; the repository was not modified, built or verified.

\part{Named symmetries and Replacement}\label{hset:ns:part}

\section{Source 12: question, results, notation and relation to the report}
\label{hset:ns:sec:intro}\label{hset:ns:sec:motivation}

This part was added in batch~90. It prints one further manuscript, written
independently of the seven above: \emph{Named Symmetries and Replacement: An
exact orbit criterion, a strict kernel hierarchy, and invariance of the pure
surreal universe}, ``Prepared for Vladimir Reshetnikov with ChatGPT'', 3 October
2026. It is batch-90 manuscript 05 and \emph{source 12} in this report's local
numbering; its shipped files carry the prefix \texttt{12-named-symmetries-}. It
was pinned to repository commit \texttt{8dc2592e9}, arrived in commit
\texttt{a162e4386} and was placed in commit \texttt{12076b2e8}. Its provenance,
the mechanical changes made in printing it, and its non-claims are in
\cref{hset:ns:sec:provenance}. Inside this part, ``this manuscript'', ``this
article'', ``this draft'', ``here'', ``our'' and ``we'' refer to source 12.

Source 12 continues \cref{hset:zr:part}: the ``earlier companion working note''
it names is source 10, which it read outside the repository and from which it
assumes no theorem. Its pin predates both \cref{hset:zr:part} and the
research-report collection's report
\repo{SetTheory/Cardinals/docs/reports/ordinals-and-order-types/naming-elementary-embeddings},
whose principal theorem already contains source 12's full criterion
(\cref{hset:ns:thm:full}) for finitely many named permutations. Source 12's
introduction, written without that report, must not be read as claiming that
case new; \cref{hset:ns:rem:nee} states what each proves.

\begin{resultbox}
\textbf{Research status (source 12).} The finite-kernel construction and its classical symmetry argument are established mathematics. The exact classifications and hierarchy are proved in this manuscript as proposed refinements; their historical priority has not been established. This is an unrefereed research draft, not a claim to have resolved a named published conjecture or to have obtained independent Lean/Rocq verification. Elliot Glazer is neither a coauthor nor an endorser.
\end{resultbox}

\paragraph{Source 12's summary.} Let $\Mker_\omega$ be the universe of sets and urelements with finite actual atom kernel, inside an ambient universe with Choice and a set $A$ of infinitely many atoms. We classify which uniformly named actions of a pure set-sized group preserve Replacement. The answer has two independent parts: every atom orbit must be finite, and only finitely many atoms may belong to finite transitive orbit types occurring with finite positive multiplicity.

A sharper classification measures the atom kernel of an output. Write $\mathsf R_k$ for Replacement restricted to outputs involving at most $k$ atoms. For $k\ge1$, this holds exactly when all group orbits are finite and only finitely many atoms have centralizer orbit of size at most $k$. We construct, for every $d\ge1$, two involutions whose separate expansions satisfy full Replacement, whereas their joint expansion satisfies precisely the finite levels $\mathsf R_k$ with $k<d$. All joint atom orbits are finite. A single permutation can satisfy every fixed finite level while failing full Replacement.

Every example has the same pure sets and hence the same pure sign-sequence surreal field. More strongly, Collection for pure-valued relations survives even formulas using the added symmetries and impure parameters. We give complete proofs, explicit failure witnesses, a distinction between separate symbols and a uniform evaluator, 203 finite regression checks, and a formalization roadmap.

\subsection{Why this is an appropriate intersection}
Elliot Glazer and Bokai Yao's \emph{Reflection Principles in ZFU} studies the interaction of Collection, reflection, and urelements, including small-kernel permutation constructions \cite{GlazerYao}. Glazer's work on global versus local choice in Zermelo set theory also emphasizes sensitivity to foundational hypotheses \cite{GlazerChoice}. These publications, rather than an inference from a professional profile, supply the research-interest side of the intersection.

The ProveIt repository supplies the other side: it develops surreal numbers from sign sequences and explicitly distinguishes formalized results from research drafts. Its surreal README and formalization ledger provide the relevant entry points \cite{ProveItSurrealNS}. The question pursued here is not a new algebraic identity inside $\No$. It is a foundational limitation on what an unchanged, richly structured surreal universe can reveal about the surrounding universe of sets.

The classical finite-kernel model already separates Replacement from Collection. Hamkins and Yao explain this construction and attribute its Replacement argument to L\'evy \cite{HamkinsYao}. Our earlier companion working note (source 10, printed as \cref{hset:zr:part}) examined small-kernel models and the effect of naming an atom enumeration. Naming an enumeration is a direct way to make many atoms individually accessible. Naming an involution is much subtler: it reveals only a reversible pairing. The central question is therefore:

\begin{resultbox}
\textbf{Classification problem.} Which named atom symmetries preserve Replacement in the finite-kernel universe, and how much of Replacement can fail while all pure surreal constructions remain available?
\end{resultbox}

We resolve this problem for every uniformly named action of a pure set-sized group. We also determine a strict hierarchy that records how many atoms a definable output is permitted to involve. These are questions formulated and answered here; they are not presented as previously advertised open conjectures of Glazer or Yao.

\subsection{The main conclusions}
Write $\mathcal Z=\Aut_\Gamma(A)$ for the group of atom permutations commuting with the named action. The two kinds of orbit must not be confused: $\Gamma a$ is the orbit generated by the \emph{named} symmetries, whereas $\mathcal Z a$ is the orbit under symmetries that \emph{remain available} after the action has been named.

\begin{description}[style=nextline,leftmargin=0pt,labelwidth=0pt,labelsep=0pt,font=\normalfont\bfseries]
\item[Exact full criterion (\cref{hset:ns:thm:full}).]
Full Replacement holds precisely when every $\Gamma$-orbit is finite and the union of all finitely repeated finite orbit types is finite.
\item[Exact bounded-kernel criterion (\cref{hset:ns:thm:graded}).]
For $k\ge1$, Replacement for outputs with at most $k$ atoms holds precisely when every $\Gamma$-orbit is finite and $\{a:\abs{\mathcal Z a}\le k\}$ is finite.
\item[Sharp hierarchy using two involutions (\cref{hset:ns:thm:strict}).]
For each $d\ge1$, two individually harmless involutions jointly give exactly the levels $\mathsf R_k$ for $k<d$. No infinite joint orbit is needed.
\item[The limit gap (\cref{hset:ns:thm:single}).]
One permutation with one cycle of each finite length satisfies every fixed $\mathsf R_k$ but not full Replacement.
\item[Surreal invariance (\cref{hset:ns:thm:purecollection,hset:ns:cor:surreal}).]
All examples preserve the entire pure universe, and even expanded-language Collection for pure-valued relations. Consequently pure surreal-valued Replacement and set-indexed cut interpolation survive.
\end{description}

The logical content is stronger than an isolated bad example. The classification identifies all obstructions within the specified model family, and the hierarchy locates the first possible failure exactly.

\subsection{Scope, attribution, and limits}
The background symmetry method is classical; we do not rename it as a discovery. Our contribution within this draft is the orbit-type obstruction, the centralizer-size refinement with arbitrary parameters, and their sharp realizations. The proofs below are self-contained after the ambient set-theoretic assumptions and the standard surreal simplicity theorem have been specified. The finite computation checks only finite group-action calculations, not the infinitary metatheorems.

Glazer--Yao's 2026 separation results concern reflection and Collection in a broader range of urelement models \cite{GlazerYao}. We do not strengthen those published separation theorems here. Our models retain Choice, and their full Collection already fails in the reduct. What changes is \emph{Replacement in the expanded language}. This distinction prevents an apparent but false conflict with equivalences that assume Replacement as part of the base theory.
