\part{Surreal-only foundations: theory-level bi-interpretations, the native omnific language and the class boundary}\label{hset:sf:part}

\section{Sources 13 and 14: question, results, notation and relation to the report}
\label{hset:sf:sec:intro}

This part was added in batch~95. It prints, merged into one text, two further
manuscripts written independently of each other and of the twelve above, on
the same assignment and on the same day, 4 October 2026:
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{source 13}, \emph{Surreal and Omnific Foundations: Bi-Interpretations
with Set Theory and the Boundary at Classes}, ``Research study prepared for
Vladimir Reshetnikov'', batch-95 manuscript 01, pinned to repository commit
\texttt{722337445}; it is the base of the merge, and its local file prefix
\texttt{13-omnific-foundations-} is used for provenance only, since it ships no
code or data;
\item \emph{source 14}, \emph{Surreal-Only Foundations and the Set--Class
Boundary: Explicit axiomatizations, omnific quotient birthdays,
bi-interpretations, and the obstruction at NBG}, ``Prepared for Vladimir
Reshetnikov'', ``Research study developed with ChatGPT'', batch-95 manuscript 02,
pinned to commit \texttt{bd1de458b}; its shipped files carry the prefix
\texttt{14-surreal-only-nbg-}.
\end{itemize}
Both archives arrived in commit \texttt{ec91f8c7c} and were placed in commit
\texttt{350b9a954}. Their provenance, the mechanical changes made in printing
them, the places where the merge had to choose, and their non-claims are in
\cref{hset:sf:sec:provenance}. The running text is source 13's, in its order;
source 14's results are inserted where they belong, each marked ``(source 14)''.
A result proved by both is printed once, with both sources named, and a
genuinely different proof of the other is kept as a marked second route. Inside
this part ``this article'', ``here'', ``our'' and ``we'' refer to the source of
the passage in which they occur; statements added in this merge are marked as
such.

Both sources re-derive this report's \cref{hset:thm:chy}, the announced global
theorem of Chen, Hamkins and Yang, and both credit it and this report. What they
add is a \emph{theory-level} form of it (an explicit recursively axiomatized
theory with numerical individuals, bi-interpretable with ZFC), the same
reconstruction in the native language of the omnific integers, and an analysis
of the boundary at class theory. \Cref{hset:sf:sub:relation} states what each
adds to this report and to the collection.

\begin{resultbox}
\textbf{Principal conclusion (source 13).} There are explicit, recursively
axiomatized first-order theories in which the only individuals are surreal
numbers, or alternatively omnific integers, and which are bi-interpretable with
$\ZFC$. Arithmetic, order, and birthday precedence suffice for the constructions
given here. Full $\NBG/\GBC$ cannot be recovered uniformly when the forward
interpretation uses only set-coded individuals of the same universe. A faithful
class-theoretic version retains classes of numbers as an additional sort.

\medskip
\textbf{Research status (source 13).} The surreal-field result was already
announced by Chen, Hamkins, and Yang, and is discussed in the repository. This
article supplies an explicit omnific-integer construction, axioms specifying the
two inverse interpretations, and precise class-theoretic obstructions. Proofs are
mathematical proofs; no new theorem in this article is claimed to have been
checked by a proof assistant. Literature priority is not asserted for the derived
results. Repository baseline: \url{https://github.com/VladimirReshetnikov/ProveIt},
inspected commit \texttt{722337445aa691a90e005ff8f619fd8ce5397103}.
\end{resultbox}

\begin{resultbox}
\textbf{Main conclusion (source 14).} A recursively axiomatized first-order theory
whose individuals are surreal numbers can recover \emph{all sets}, with definable
inverse interpretations. An explicit omnific-integer version is obtained by
adjoining the quotient-birthday operation. Recovering \emph{all classes of an
NBG model} is a different requirement: it cannot be achieved by a theory
uniformly interpretable in $\ZFC$, assuming $\Con(\ZFC)$. A second class sort
provides an exact positive replacement.

\medskip
\textbf{Attribution and proof status (source 14).} The fundamental
birthday-enriched surreal/ZFC bi-interpretation is due to the announced work of
Junhong Chen, Joel David Hamkins, and Ruizhi Yang, not to this report. The report
supplies detailed reconstructions and further deductions; it makes no priority
claim for those deductions. Proofs are mathematical, not Lean-verified. Finite
executable checks are supplementary only. Repository audit pinned to
\texttt{bd1de458b7145f535cfcef0bef47d851c2e82463}. No repository files have been
modified.
\end{resultbox}

\paragraph{Source 13's abstract.} Can set theory be formulated with numbers as
its only objects, in the same strong sense in which a suitable theory of
hereditarily finite sets is bi-interpretable with Peano arithmetic? For surreal
numbers, the answer depends on the language and on whether set theory means its
set universe or also its proper classes. We separate these issues and prove
explicit positive and negative results.

Our constructive focus is the ordered ring of omnific integers equipped with the
relation of strictly earlier birthday. We recover ordinals and signs in this
language and show that an omnific integer can encode an arbitrary bounded set of
ordinals: each bit occupies a block of ordinal length $\omega$, with any
intervening gaps filled uniformly. Pointed well-founded extensional graphs then
interpret the whole set universe. The sign sequence of an original integer
supplies a definable inverse to the reconstructed integer structure. This yields
a first-order bi-interpretation at the structure level and, by an explicit
recursive axiom construction, a theory $\Tth_{\Oz}$ bi-interpretable with
$\ZFC$. The same framework gives a surreal-only theory $\Tth_{\No}$, without
assuming a final intrinsic axiom list from the announced Chen--Hamkins--Yang
programme.

The class boundary is stronger than a mere cardinality intuition about proper
classes. Established model constructions give a model of $\GBC$ with countably
many sets and uncountably many classes. Hence no uniform bi-interpretation of
full $\GBC$ can send every model to a structure whose individuals are finite
tuples of its original sets, even with arbitrary class-dependent relations and
quotient coding. We also explain the reflection obstruction to interpreting
$\GBC$ in $\ZFC$, the precise limits of the diagonal argument in predicative
class theory, and a positive two-sorted theory of numbers and their classes. The
final sections distinguish proved constructions from inherited results, describe
formalization tasks, and propose further research.

\paragraph{Source 14's abstract.} The analogy between Ackermann coding of
hereditarily finite sets and arithmetic suggests replacing finite binary strings
by ordinal-length surreal sign sequences. We examine exactly what this
accomplishes. An explicit first-order code for rooted well-founded extensional
graphs recovers the set universe from the ordered surreal field with birthday. A
round-trip axiom construction turns this structural reconstruction into a
recursively axiomatized theory bi-interpretable with $\ZFC$, without assuming an
unchecked list of intrinsic surreal axiom schemes. We transfer this construction
to the omnific integers using the binary operation $h(a,b)=\bd(a/b)$, with a
convention at zero denominator. We then prove a reflection obstruction: no
theory uniformly interpretable in $\ZFC$ can interpret the usual finitely
axiomatized NBG theory, assuming $\Con(\ZFC)$. An independent class-diagonal
argument rules out uniform numerical names for every available class with an
elementary evaluation relation. Finally, we construct a reversible
correspondence between class realizations over a set model and appropriate class
realizations over its surreal structure. This yields numerical versions of GBC
and KM with a necessary class sort. The outcome is a precise positive answer at
the set level, a qualified negative answer to the native one-sorted NBG proposal,
and a research program concerning smaller omnific languages, intrinsic axioms,
choice, class recursion, and formal verification.

\subsection{The question and the answer}
\label{hset:sf:sub:question}

The motivating analogy is sound once the finite set theory is specified
carefully. An hereditarily finite set has a natural-number code, and suitable
finite set theories and $\PA$ can recover one another together with definable
isomorphisms for both compositions. The transfinite analogue asks for
substantially more than an injection between informal collections of
mathematical objects.

There are three independent requirements:
\begin{enumerate}
\item the individuals of the proposed theory must really correspond to the
surreal numbers, or the omnific integers, of the universe under study;
\item the language must allow the representation to be read and the relevant
operations to be defined;
\item both interpretations must recover their inputs by definable isomorphisms,
including every class object when the target is class theory.
\end{enumerate}
The first two requirements can be met for $\ZFC$ in a natural numerical
language. The third prevents a direct extension to the full class sort of
$\NBG$.

\paragraph{The same analogy, as source 14 puts it.} A natural number can encode
a finite set by its binary digits; a surreal number can encode a subset of
\emph{any ordinal} by its signs. The second statement supplies codes for
arbitrary well-orderable membership graphs, not merely for finite graphs. It is
therefore capable of reaching the entire universe of sets under Choice. What it
does not supply is a set-sized sign sequence for an arbitrary proper class. Two
qualifications to the motivating premise are worth making at once.
``Finitary'' should refer to finite objects or finite descriptions, not to
arbitrary objects merely studied by finitary proofs. Moreover, the PA/finite-set
correspondence requires an appropriately axiomatized theory of hereditarily
finite sets. Removing Infinity from ZF and adding its negation is not, without
further attention to induction and transitive containment, an interchangeable
description of every such theory. The careful finite-set formulations and their
bi-interpretations are discussed in \cite{KayeWong} and \cite[\S6.3]{FriedmanVisser}.

Source 14 distinguishes four requirements throughout.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \textbf{Set reconstruction:} can an enriched numerical structure interpret
$(V,\in)$, with a definable round trip?
\item \textbf{Axiomatic equivalence:} is there an effective first-order numerical
theory bi-interpretable with $\ZFC$?
\item \textbf{Class reconstruction:} can ordinary numerical individuals also
recover the class objects of an arbitrary NBG model?
\item \textbf{Literal categoricity:} do the axioms have only the externally
intended proper-class numerical universe as a model?
\end{enumerate}
The first two have positive answers. The third encounters genuine obstructions,
made precise below. The fourth is not a property of an ordinary consistent
first-order theory with infinite models. Its set-sized models can be
nonstandard, just as models of PA can be nonstandard. Bi-interpretability
concerns these models as well as the intended class-sized structure; it is not a
categoricity claim.

\paragraph{A precise summary (source 13).} Throughout this part $\precb$ denotes
\emph{strict birthday precedence}, the relation of \cref{hset:lem:birthday-language}:
\[
 x\precb y\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \bd(x)<\bd(y).
\]
It does not denote proper-prefix simplicity, and it is not the elementary
substructure relation $\prec$ of Parts~I--V. Source 13 uses the finite language
\[
 \Lb=\{0,1,+,-,\cdot,<,\precb\}.
\]
The constants and unary minus could be replaced by definitions.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}P{0.27\textwidth}YP{0.23\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Question & Answer and scope & Status\\
\midrule
Pure ordered surreal field & Does not interpret even Robinson arithmetic; its
elementary theory is $\RCF$. & Established obstruction\\
Surreal field with birthdays & Interprets the whole set universe, with definable
inverse compositions. & CHY announcement; prior repository proof\\
Omnific ring with birthdays & The same set-universe reconstruction works using
ordinal blocks of signs. & Explicit proof in this article\\
Recursive numerical axioms & $\Tth_{\No}\bi\ZFC$ and $\Tth_{\Oz}\bi\ZFC$, with
both theories in $\Lb$. & Explicit construction here\\
Full $\NBG/\GBC$ on the original numbers & No uniform bi-interpretation with a
forward domain coded by the original sets. & Corollary of established model
constructions, proved here\\
Numbers and classes of numbers & A faithful class lift is bi-interpretable with
$\GBC$; analogous lifts handle stronger class theories. & Construction and proof
here\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

The positive axiom systems below are not asserted to be complete, categorical,
or finitely axiomatized. They have the same interpretive strength as $\ZFC$,
including its possible nonstandard models and independence phenomena. Their
quantifiers range only over numbers; sets are represented by equivalence classes
of numerical codes.

\begin{theorem}[Summary of the constructions and obstructions; source 14]
\label{hset:sf:thm:summary}
In the precise interpretation conventions of \cref{hset:sf:sec:logic}:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\arabic*)}]
\item There is a recursively axiomatized theory $\SArt$ in the finite language
$\Ls=\{0,1,+,-,\cdot,<,\bd\}$, with its canonical interpretation on surreal
numbers, that is parameter-free bi-interpretable with $\ZFC$.
\item There is such a theory $\OAqb$ on omnific integers in the language
$\Lz=\{0,1,+,-,\cdot,<,h\}$, where canonically
\[
 h(a,b)=\begin{cases}\bd(a/b)&b\ne0,\\0&b=0.\end{cases}
\]
It is parameter-free bi-interpretable with $\SArt$ and with $\ZFC$.
\item If $\Con(\ZFC)$, no theory uniformly interpretable in $\ZFC$ interprets the
usual NBG theory. In particular, neither $\SArt$ nor $\OAqb$ interprets NBG.
\item With an additional sort of classes of numerical codes, there are
bi-interpretable numerical presentations of GBC, and similarly of KM. These
presentations retain, rather than eliminate, class objects.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

Part (1) reconstructs an already announced result, using a deliberately explicit
axiomatic presentation. Parts (2)--(4) are proved in this report as extensions
and applications. None is advertised as an independently verified new theorem in
the literature. The important outcome for the research question is the separation
between (1) and (3), not a claim that all forms of set theory have secretly
become the same theory. (Source~14.) In this merge, (1) is
\cref{hset:sf:thm:theories} for $\No$ (source 14's presentation is
\cref{hset:sf:thm:surrealtheory}), (2) is \cref{hset:sf:thm:omnifictheory},
(3) is \cref{hset:sf:thm:noint,hset:sf:cor:numericalnoint}, and (4) is
\cref{hset:sf:thm:classlift,hset:sf:cor:variants}. Source 13 proves the omnific
case of (1)--(2) in the weaker language $\Lb$, without $h$
(\cref{hset:sf:thm:theories}); by \cref{hset:sf:prop:unary} the two omnific
languages are definitionally equivalent.

\paragraph{What the negative statement does not say (source 14).} The hypothesis
``uniformly interpretable in $\ZFC$'' is essential. It includes a canonically
defined numerical domain and operations whose proposed axioms are all provable in
$\ZFC$. It excludes adding an arbitrary relation of independent class-theoretic
strength and merely calling its arguments numbers. It also excludes coding the
classes of a small universe by numbers belonging to a strictly larger universe
while silently changing the reference universe. We do not prove that every
conceivable structure externally relabelled by surreal numbers is incapable of
carrying NBG. External relabelling can hide essentially arbitrary information and
is not an intrinsic numerical foundation. For natural, same-universe codes with
elementary evaluation, \cref{hset:sf:thm:diagonal} gives a separate obstruction
even in class theory. These qualifications are part of the answer, rather than
loopholes to be suppressed.

\subsection{What was already known}
\label{hset:sf:sub:known}

Chen, Hamkins, and Yang announced the bi-interpretability of
$\langle\No,+,\cdot,<,\precb\rangle$ with $\langle V,\in\rangle$, and an
associated first-order theory $\SAth$ with $\ZFC$, in presentations during
2025--2026. The available slides mark the axiom programme as work whose details
were still being checked. The relevant announcements and slides are primary
sources \cite{CHYNotreDame,CHYCUNY}. We do not present that surreal result as
new, and we do not assume that an unpublished final axiomatization has been
verified.

There are also earlier approaches connecting surreals and set-theoretic
foundations. Rangel and Mariano develop an algebraic set-theoretic programme of
surreal algebras \cite{RM}; Bertram discusses set-based representations of
Conway numbers and games \cite{Bertram}. Their purposes and chosen structures
differ from the first-order bi-interpretations considered here.

The current ProveIt corpus already discusses the CHY programme, develops
birthday-based reconstructions of hereditary set universes, and studies ambiently
definable surreal and omnific codes. In particular, the full surreal
reconstruction and the distinction between local Choice and Global Choice are
prior material. The specific additional construction developed here carries the
reconstruction out within the \emph{native omnific ring with its own birthday
relation}. The class obstruction also answers a different question from
reconstructing $\langle V,\in\rangle$. The exact overlaps are recorded in
\cref{hset:sf:sub:repo}.

\paragraph{The same background, as source 14 records it.} Chen, Hamkins, and
Yang announced a first-order surreal arithmetic bi-interpretable with $\ZFC$ in
2025--2026. The key additional structure is birthday comparison; the underlying
field alone is insufficient. Hamkins's March 2026 slides describe graph coding
and candidate axioms. They label the project as work in progress and explicitly
warn that details were still being checked \cite{CHYNotreDame,CHYCUNY}. We
therefore distinguish the structural coding theorem from any particular proposed
minimal list of intrinsic axiom schemes. The requested repository already
contains substantial work on this exact background. At the audited revision, the
directory
\path{Algebra/SurrealNumbers/docs/foundations-and-computation/birthday-cutoffs-and-hereditary-sets/}
contains a merged report whose README credits the global construction to
Chen--Hamkins--Yang and describes an explicit full-universe proof. Its cutoff
results, graph encodings, and further weak-foundation discussions are not
discoveries of the present investigation. The nearby
\repo{definable-surreals-and-omnific-integers} report also explicitly credits
that precedent \cite{ProveItSF14}. The README reports no Lean
implementation mapping for its \texttt{hset:} labels. We have not built or
independently audited that entire report, and do not import its unverified cutoff
claims as axioms. The present report instead uses the classical sign-sequence and
normal-form constructions \cite{Conway,Gonshor}, gives the coding argument needed
here, and isolates three follow-on issues: a concrete omnific language,
reflection at the NBG boundary, and reversible transport of the class
collection. Ordinary interpretation terminology follows \cite{FriedmanVisser}.
For class-theory conventions and realizations we use \cite{WilliamsDiss}.

There is also an older question about intrinsic axioms for omnific integers.
Je\v{r}\'abek's answer distinguishes a useful recursively axiomatized fragment
from the \emph{complete true theory}, which cannot be recursively axiomatized
because it contains arithmetic \cite{JerabekOz}. Our $\OAqb$ is an incomplete
foundational theory in an enriched language, not the complete theory of the
intended omnific ring. Thus it does not evade that obstruction. (Source~14.)

\subsection{What counts as a result here}

Theorems below labeled as proved here are accompanied by proofs, not by claims of
bibliographic priority. The most substantial constructions are the omnific block
interpretation, the corresponding recursive theory in the native numerical
language, and the application of models with different sizes of their set and
class sorts. These resolve precise versions of the question posed in this
article. They are not advertised as solutions to previously published open
problems without a verified priority history. (Source~13.)

\label{hset:sf:sub:notation}

\subsection{Relation to the report and to the collection}
\label{hset:sf:sub:relation}

\begin{remark}[The global theorem of Part IV, and Parts I--V]
\label{hset:sf:rem:chy}
For $\Dd=\No$, \cref{hset:sf:thm:graph,hset:sf:thm:roundtrip} (source 13) and
\cref{hset:sf:thm:setinterpret,hset:sf:prop:recovery} (source 14) are two further
written proofs of \cref{hset:thm:chy}, after source 07's printed there and source
05's bisimulation version; both sources credit Chen--Hamkins--Yang and this
report, and neither is claimed first. Their codes are those of
\cref{hset:sec:codes} with a different pairing: source 13's $\pi_\kappa$ is the
map $p_\theta$ of \cref{hset:lem:pairing}, source 14's $\varpi$ is source 06's
polynomial (\cref{hset:lem:pairing06}). Source 13's ordinal and prefix lemmas
(\cref{hset:sf:lem:ord,hset:sf:lem:prefix}) re-prove
\cref{hset:lem:birthday-language,hset:lem:prefix}, now also in $\Oz$; its
\cref{hset:sf:prop:hwo} is \cref{hset:thm:choice} with
\cref{hset:prop:internal-choice} (source 13 credits this report). The status note
after \cref{hset:thm:chy} says that the proof there ``is not an audit \dots{} of
their proposed axiomatization''. That stays true: \cref{hset:sf:thm:theories}
supplies \emph{an} explicit recursive axiomatization of the round trip, not the
intrinsic axioms announced by Chen--Hamkins--Yang, which neither source verifies
(\cref{hset:sf:q:intrinsic}). A dated note after \cref{hset:thm:chy} points
here.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Axiomatics and the minimal signature]
\label{hset:sf:rem:axiomatics}
\Cref{hset:q:axiomatics} asks for definitional equivalence, or its failure,
between the schemes $\Et$, $\Pack$ and published axiom formulations of surreal
arithmetic ``once finalized''. Nothing here is finalized: the theories of
\cref{hset:sf:sec:axioms} are explicit and recursive, but they are built from the
interpretation (\cref{hset:sf:thm:axiomprinciple}), and an intrinsic list is
\cref{hset:sf:q:intrinsic}. The question's other items (ill-founded models,
quantifier elimination, choiceless universality, formalization) meet
\cref{hset:sf:q:nonstandard,hset:sf:q:choicefree,hset:sf:q:formal} and are not
answered. The ``Minimal signature'' question of \cref{hset:sec:questions} asks
which reducts of $\{0,1,+,-,\cdot,<,\bd\}$ still \emph{interpret} the hereditary
sets; source 13's \cref{hset:sf:q:reducts} asks which reducts support a
\emph{canonical bi-interpretation}. \Cref{hset:sf:prop:reducts}, an observation
of this merge, shows that the second question is a definability question: a
reduct, or any structure on $\Dd$ with set-definable primitives, is canonically
bi-interpretable with $V$ exactly when it defines $\precb$ together with the ring
operations. The one-way question is untouched. Dated notes after the two
questions point here.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Definable operations in the collection's other reports]
\label{hset:sf:rem:dsn}
Part~III of \repo{definable-surreals-and-omnific-integers} (labels
\lbl{dsn:op:}, its source~06, pinned to \texttt{1404038df} and written
independently) interprets $(V,\in)$ parameter-free in
$\mathfrak S_\Omega=(\No;+,\cdot,<,\Oz,\Omega)$, with $\Omega$ the omega map, by the
same three validity clauses (well-foundedness, extensionality and root
accessibility tested by subset quantification) as \cref{hset:sf:sec:graphs}, but
with vertex sets read from the supports of unit-coefficient series and edges read
through a pairing of supports (its Theorem~37.1, \lbl{dsn:op:thm:V-interpretation}).
It has \emph{no} comparison map back to the original numbers; its
Question~42.1 (\lbl{dsn:op:q:biinterpretation}, ``The missing comparison map'')
names ``a sufficiently strong intrinsic birthday relation'' as a promising route
and the birthday-enriched announcement as a comparison target. By
\cref{hset:sf:cor:definability} the classes $\Oz$ and the graph of $\Omega$ are
$\Lb$-definable in $\No$, so $\mathfrak S_\Omega$ is a reduct of a definitional
expansion of $(\No,\Lb)$, and the theorems of this part are that comparison
target. They do not answer the question, which concerns the reduct; but by
\cref{hset:sf:prop:reducts} its specific form, a definable relation sending each
original surreal to the class of codes of its own sign sequence, is equivalent to
the definability of $\precb$ in $\mathfrak S_\Omega$, with the same parameters.
Same coding scheme, different theorem; source 13's native $\Oz$ codes (sign
blocks) and dsn's (Hahn supports) are different mechanisms. A dated note after
\cref{hset:thm:outer} records dsn's transfer theorem.

Source 13 also credits the ambient omnific codes and hulls of
\repo{definable-surreals-and-omnific-integers} (\lbl{dsn:thm:bounded-code},
\lbl{dsn:thm:hull}); its requirement here is that both directions be first-order
definable in the native birthday language. Its \cref{hset:sf:prop:automorphism}
is a weaker consequence of \lbl{opa:thm:parameters} and \lbl{opa:sc:thm:undef} of
\repo{surreal/omnific-preserving-automorphisms}, which it credits: there a strong
real-linear automorphism preserving $\Oz$ and fixing any given set and $\R$ sends
the ordinal $\omega^{\omega^\beta}$ to a nonmonomial, and $\mathrm{Mon}\cap\Oz$ is not
definable over any set of parameters in the pure ordered ring. The fraction-field
fact \cref{hset:sf:prop:fractions} is \lbl{odg:thm:fractions} of
\repo{surreal/omnific-diophantine-geometry}, proved in Lean
(\cref{hset:sf:sub:machine}); the pure ring $\Oz$ parameter-free interprets the
field $\No$ with $\R$ and its order (\lbl{odg:def:thm:realrecovery}), is
bi-interpretable with $(\No,\Oz)$ (\lbl{dsn:op:prop:native-omnific}, Proposition~35.1
of dsn), and defines $\Z$ and $\N$ (\lbl{odg:def:cor:arithmetic}), which confirms
source 14's remark that the pure ring ``already has arithmetic strength''
(\cref{hset:sf:rem:pureoz}). The failure of the unrestricted cut principle in
\cref{hset:sf:cor:cuts} is \lbl{found:prop:allcuts} of the foundations report,
and \cref{hset:sf:sub:conservativity} agrees with \lbl{found:rem:conservativity}.
\end{remark}

The Hilbert's-tenth programme authenticated both archives at their arrival and

\section{Logical and foundational conventions}
\label{hset:sf:sec:logic}

\subsection{Interpretation is stronger than encoding}

An interpretation of a theory $S$ in a theory $T$ specifies a definable domain
$D(\bar x)$, an equivalence relation $E(\bar x,\bar y)$, and definitions of the
relations and function graphs of $S$ on $D/E$. The tuples have some fixed finite
length. The theory $T$ must prove the conditions making these data an
interpretation and must prove the translations of every axiom of $S$. Quotient
equality is often essential \cite{FriedmanTarski}.

If $\I$ constructs an $S$-model from a $T$-model and $\J$ constructs a $T$-model
from an $S$-model, bi-interpretability additionally requires uniform definable
isomorphisms
\[
 A\cong \J(\I(A)),\qquad M\cong \I(\J(M)).
\]
Merely obtaining some set-theoretic universe from the numbers and some number
system from the sets is insufficient. An arbitrary surplus predicate on the
numbers, for example, may be lost on the first translation and prevent the second
isomorphism.

All positive interpretations here are parameter-free. When discussing negative
results we explicitly allow finite parameters if the argument allows them. No
proof chooses a global representative from every code equivalence class.

\paragraph{The same conventions, as source 14 states them.} An interpretation of
a theory $T$ in a theory $U$ consists of finitely many formulas defining a domain
$D\subseteq M^n$, an equivalence relation $\sim$ on $D$, and the relations and
operations of $T$ on the quotient. The theory $U$ proves nonemptiness,
congruence, and every translated axiom of $T$. We allow a fixed finite number of
sorts and pieces, since these can be handled by the same syntax. The main positive
constructions are parameter-free. For clarity, $\I(N)$ will denote the set
structure interpreted in a numerical structure $N$, and $\J(M)$ the canonical
numerical structure interpreted in a set structure $M$. A bi-interpretation
requires \emph{definable isomorphisms}
\[
 M\cong \I(\J(M)),\qquad N\cong \J(\I(N)),
\]
uniformly in the models, and proved in the respective theories. Merely showing
that models can be encoded in both directions does not suffice. Likewise,
equality of consistency strength and conservativity do not suffice. Finite
axiomatizability is one invariant of bi-interpretability \cite{FriedmanVisser}.

Quotients are important. An arbitrary set can have many enumerations of its
transitive closure. We will not select one enumeration globally. Instead,
different codes represent the same interpreted object when a first-order formula
recognizes the appropriate isomorphism. The interpretation never quantifies over a
proper-class quotient as a new set. In an internal class construction it uses
representatives; in a set-sized model its quotient is a set in the metatheory.
(Source~14.)

\subsection{Proper-class structures are handled formula by formula}

In a universe satisfying $\ZFC$, $\No$ is a definable proper class of sets,
represented here by sign functions
\[
 s:\alpha\longrightarrow\{-,+\},\qquad \alpha\in\Ord.
\]
Every individual sign function is a set. Its birthday is $\alpha$. Writing a
first-order formula about $\No$ means relativizing its quantifiers to this
definable class and replacing its numerical symbols by their set-theoretic
definitions. This is a syntactic translation. It does not require a set
containing all surreals or a truth predicate for the whole universe.

For a model $M\models\ZFC$, let $\No^M$ and $\Oz^M$ mean these constructions
\emph{as computed inside $M$}. If $M$ is countable externally, so are these
interpreted domains. Internally, $M$ regards them as proper classes. If $M$ is
nonstandard, its internally well-founded graphs need not be externally
well-founded. The interpretation and collapse theorems are applied internally in
$M$; external well-foundedness is never silently assumed.

Source 14 adds: a surreal number is represented by a \emph{set-sized} function
$s:\alpha\to\{-,+\}$, where $\alpha$ is an ordinal. Its birthday is $\alpha$,
identified with the all-plus surreal of length $\alpha$. Thus every individual
surreal is a set, while the total collection $\No$ is a proper class. This is
precisely the reason that a domain of all surreals is a legitimate definable
domain of interpretation in $\ZFC$ but is not a set asserted to exist by $\ZFC$.
All proofs of set constructions below are uniform theorems of $\ZFC$.
Consequently they apply internally to nonstandard models. An externally
ill-founded relation which a nonstandard model regards as well-founded is not a
counterexample: the interpreted collapse is the model's internal collapse. We
never infer external well-foundedness merely from an internal first-order
formula.

\subsection{Sets versus classes}
\label{hset:sf:sub:classes}

\begin{convention}[Class-theory names]\label{hset:sf:conv:gb}
Following \lbl{found:sub:gbconvention} of the foundations report, in this part
$\GB$ is the two-sorted G\"odel--Bernays theory with set and class variables,
class extensionality, elementary class comprehension (class parameters allowed,
quantified variables restricted to sets) and class replacement, with no choice
assumption; $\GBc$ is $\GB$ with the Axiom of Choice for sets; $\GBC$ is $\GB$
with Global Choice (and hence set Choice). $\NBG$ is the usual one-sorted
presentation of $\GBC$. $\KM$ adds comprehension for formulas with class
quantifiers. Henkin semantics is used for the class sort.

Source 13 used ``$\mathsf{GB}$'' for $\GB$, stating set Choice ``when it
matters''; source 14's macro for ``$\mathsf{GB}$'' printed ``GBc'' and meant
$\GBc$, ``Choice for sets, but without Global Choice''. Each statement below
names the theory its proof uses: the diagonal arguments need only $\GB$; the
class lifts need $\GBc$, because the ordinal-graph codec uses set Choice
(\cref{hset:sf:prop:hwo}); $\GB$ without set Choice is
\cref{hset:sf:q:gbnochoice}. Where a source stated a result under a stronger
convention than its proof uses, both are said at the point of use.
\end{convention}

We use $\GBC$ for the usual two-sorted G\"odel--Bernays theory with Global Choice.
A model has the form
\[
 \mathcal M=(M,\mathcal X),
 \qquad \mathcal X\subseteq\Pow(M)
\]
externally, with sets in the first sort and classes in the second. Class
extensionality identifies a class by its members. Elementary class comprehension
permits set quantifiers and class parameters but no bound class quantifiers.
Class replacement and Global Choice are included. The standard one-sorted
formulation of $\NBG$ with the corresponding choice convention is equivalent to
this presentation. $\KM$ allows class quantifiers in comprehension. These
conventions remove a frequent ambiguity in the name NBG
\cite{HamkinsYao,WilliamsMin}. (Source~13.)

Two distinctions are fundamental. First, $\mathcal X$ is part of the model, not
something determined by $M$ alone. Second, a proper class is not an individual
surreal number of $M$: the latter is a set-sized sign function. The fact that all
surreals together form a proper class does not make that class a container for
all classes as individual members.

Source 14 states the background thus: the usual NBG presentation with Global
Choice is treated as a one-sorted presentation of $\GBC$. None of the principal
obstruction arguments depends on the terminological preference between these
presentations. We use Henkin semantics for the class sort, not the requirement
that all external subclasses be present. A model is written $(M,\mathcal X)$,
where $M$ is the set model and $\mathcal X$ its specified class collection. An
elementary class formula may have set and class parameters but no bound class
variables. Class comprehension for such formulas, together with class
replacement, is sufficient for our elementary transports and diagonal argument.
Full impredicative comprehension belongs to the KM discussion.

\subsection{The finite analogy, stated accurately}

For hereditarily finite sets the Ackermann map is
\[
 a(x)=\sum_{y\in x}2^{a(y)}.
\]
Its inverse reads binary digits recursively. This gives the familiar membership
test on $\N$. The axiomatic equivalence requires an adequate finite set theory,
including the appropriate induction or transitive-containment principle;
replacing Infinity by its negation without checking the remaining formulation is
not enough to justify every version of the claim \cite{KayeWong}. In ProveIt the
deductive PA/HF bi-interpretation is formalized in \path{Logic/Interpretability/PAHF}
(its Ackermann membership core is \path{AckermannHFCore.lean}); see
\path{Logic/README.md}.

Our transfinite codes will use well-founded graphs rather than a transfinite
binary expansion of a single ordinal. Different labelings of the same transitive
closure are identified. This is why quotient interpretations replace the unique
natural-number code available in the finite Ackermann picture.

\section{Surreal structure visible inside the omnific integers}
\label{hset:sf:sec:bits}

\subsection{The numerical facts used}

We use the sign-sequence construction of $\No$, with the lexicographic comparison
in which a missing sign lies between $-$ and $+$. Conway addition and
multiplication are the usual recursively defined operations. The resulting ordered
field is real closed. Every surreal has a unique Conway normal form
\[
 x=\sum_{i<\lambda}r_i\omega^{a_i},
 \qquad r_i\in\R\setminus\{0\},\quad a_i>a_j\ (i<j),
\]
with set-sized, reverse well-ordered support. The $\omega$-power here is Conway's
monomial map, not ordinary real exponentiation.

The omnific integers form the subring
\[
 \Oz=\Bigl\{\,n+\sum_{i<\lambda}r_i\omega^{a_i}:n\in\Z,\ a_i>0\,\Bigr\}.
\]
Equivalently, $x\in\Oz$ precisely when $x$ is the simplest surreal strictly
between $x-1$ and $x+1$. These are classical descriptions
\cite{Conway,Gonshor,EhrlichKaplanII}.

Two sign-theoretic facts will be crucial:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item\label{hset:sf:item:initial} $\Oz$ is initial in the surreal sign tree: every
prefix of an omnific sign sequence is omnific.
\item\label{hset:sf:item:signcrit} A surreal is omnific if and only if its sign
sequence has no two adjacent positions carrying opposite signs. Thus signs may
change at limit positions, but not between $\gamma$ and $\gamma+1$.
\end{enumerate}
These facts are recorded in Ehrlich--Kaplan, arXiv version 1, Section 2.2,
Lemma 2.1 and the proof of Lemma 2.3; the latter refers to Gonshor, Theorem 8.1,
p.~111 \cite{EhrlichKaplan,Gonshor}. The version matters because later versions
reorganize that section. They are imported, not re-proved, in source 13 and in
this merge (\cref{hset:sf:sub:imported}); every statement below about the native
omnific structure depends on them.

Every ordinal is an omnific integer. The restrictions of surreal addition and
multiplication to ordinals are natural, or Hessenberg, addition and
multiplication. They are commutative and obey ordered-ring identities. They must
not be confused with ordinary ordinal addition and multiplication.

\subsection{Birthdays and ordinals are first-order definable}

For the next lemmas, $\Dd$ is either $\No$ or $\Oz$, with all quantifiers ranging
over $\Dd$. Define
\begin{align}
 \EqB(x,y)&\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \neg(x\precb y)\land\neg(y\precb x),\label{hset:sf:eq:sameb}\\
 \Ord(a)&\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \forall y\,\bigl(\EqB(y,a)\longrightarrow y\le a\bigr).
 \label{hset:sf:eq:ord}
\end{align}

\begin{lemma}[Ordinal recognition; sources 13, 14]\label{hset:sf:lem:ord}
In either canonical structure, $\Ord(a)$ holds exactly for the embedded
ordinals. The birthday $\bd(x)$ is the unique $a$ such that
$\Ord(a)\land\EqB(x,a)$. Hence on $\No$ and on $\Oz$ the languages $\Lb$ and
$\Ls$ are definitionally equivalent.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Among all sign sequences of length $\alpha$, the sequence consisting of $\alpha$
plus signs is greatest. It is the ordinal $\alpha$, and belongs to both domains.
Every other length-$\alpha$ sequence is smaller at its first minus sign. Thus the
greatest member of the birthday fiber is exactly $\alpha$. The last sentence is
the observation that $x\precb y$ is $\bd(x)<\bd(y)$.
\end{proof}

This is \cref{hset:lem:birthday-language} for $\Dd=\No$; the case $\Oz$ uses only
that the all-plus sequence of each length is an ordinal, hence omnific. Source 14
proves it as a remark (``Birthday function versus birthday comparison''): the
primitive $\bd$ is definitionally interchangeable, on the canonical structure,
with the relation $x\precb y\iff\bd(x)<\bd(y)$ in the CHY announcement; in the
reverse direction, with $x\equiv_b y$ for $\neg(x\precb y)\land\neg(y\precb x)$,
$\bd(x)$ is the unique greatest element, in numerical order, of the fibre
$\{y:y\equiv_b x\}$, since among all signs of a fixed ordinal length the all-plus
sequence is greatest; ``thus the change from comparison to a birthday function is
not an additional coding assumption.'' Source 14 also notes that $\bd(x)=x$
defines the nonnegative ordinal surreals.

In subsequent formulas $\bd$ is an abbreviation for this definable function, not
an additional primitive. In particular, ordinal variables can be specified by
$\Ord$, and ordinary ordinal comparison is just the restriction of $<$. The
ordinal $\omega$ is definable as the least positive limit ordinal. For example, a
nonzero ordinal $a$ is limit when
\[
 \forall u\bigl(\Ord(u)\land u<a\longrightarrow u+1<a\bigr).
\]
Here $u+1$ is the ordinary ordinal successor, which agrees with its natural sum
with $1$.

\subsection{Recovering the entire sign sequence}

Define
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:prefix}
 \Prefp(u,z)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \neg(z\precb u)\ \land\
 \forall v\bigl(v\precb u\longrightarrow(v<u\longleftrightarrow v<z)\bigr).
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Prefix test; sources 13, 14]\label{hset:sf:lem:prefix}
For $u,z\in\Dd$, $\Prefp(u,z)$ holds if and only if the sign sequence of $u$ is
a prefix, possibly the whole sequence, of that of $z$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The first conjunct says $\bd(u)\le\bd(z)$. If $u$ is a prefix, comparison with
any shorter sign sequence $v$ is decided before the end of $u$, so $v<u$ and
$v<z$ agree.

Conversely, suppose the lengths are ordered as above but the sequences are not in
the prefix relation. Let $\gamma<\bd(u)$ be their first difference, and let $v$ be
their common prefix of length $\gamma$. The next sign of one is $+$, and that of
the other is $-$. Consequently $v$ lies strictly between $u$ and $z$,
contradicting the second conjunct. For $\Dd=\Oz$, initiality guarantees that this
witness $v$ is still in the quantified domain.
\end{proof}

\eqref{hset:sf:eq:prefix} is source 02's prefix formula of
\cref{hset:rem:prefixes}, and the lemma is \cref{hset:lem:prefix} with that
formula; the case $\Dd=\Oz$ is new and uses initiality,
\ref{hset:sf:item:initial} above. Source 14 states the same lemma in $\B$ with
$\bd$ (its formula is \eqref{hset:sf:eq:prefix} with $\bd(p)\le\bd(x)$ and
$\bd(z)<\bd(p)$), and its proof adds: if there is no disagreement, the birthday
inequality says precisely that $p$ is a prefix, which also handles the empty
prefix.

For ordinal $a$, put
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:plus}
 \Plusp(z,a)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \Ord(a)\land a\precb z\land
 \exists u\bigl(\EqB(u,a)\land\Prefp(u,z)\land u<z\bigr).
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Sign test; sources 13, 14]\label{hset:sf:lem:sign}
If $a<\bd(z)$ is ordinal, then $\Plusp(z,a)$ holds exactly when the $a$-th sign
of $z$ is $+$. It is false for ordinals outside the domain of that sign sequence.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Initiality supplies the unique prefix $u=z\restr a$. Its comparison with $z$ is
determined by the next sign: $u<z$ exactly when that sign is $+$.
\end{proof}

Source 14's bit predicate, for ordinal $\alpha<\bd(x)$,
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:bit}
 \Bit(x,\alpha)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists p\,[\Prefp(p,x)\land\bd(p)=\alpha\land p<x],
\end{equation}
declared false for nonordinal $\alpha$ or $\alpha\not<\bd(x)$, is the same
predicate: the unique prefix of length $\alpha$ lies below $x$ exactly when its
next sign is plus. It is also this report's $\Bit$ of \eqref{hset:eq:bit}.

Notice the quantifier scope. The test reads signs of every original omnific
integer. It does not depend on the integer having any specially chosen code
format. This fact is what will recover the whole numerical carrier in the second
interpretation.

\begin{corollary}[Full bounded subset coding; source 14]\label{hset:sf:cor:subsets}
For every ordinal $\theta$, the surreal numbers of birthday exactly $\theta$ are
in definable bijection with $\Pow(\theta)$, via
\[
 x\longmapsto\{\alpha<\theta:\Bit(x,\alpha)\}.
\]
In particular, universal quantification over subsets of a fixed ordinal can be
replaced by first-order quantification over numbers of that birthday.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The characteristic signs of any subset of $\theta$ form a set-sized function of
domain $\theta$, hence a surreal. Equality of all signs is equality of the
corresponding surreal. No choice principle is needed for this lemma.
\end{proof}

This is \eqref{hset:eq:fiber-subsets} for the full class. In $\Oz$ the numbers of
one birthday do not code every subset (an omnific sign sequence has no adjacent
opposite signs); \cref{hset:sf:thm:bitstorage} replaces the corollary there.

\section{Ordinal bit storage in a single omnific integer}
\label{hset:sf:sec:blocks}

An arbitrary sign sequence need not be omnific: $+-$ represents $1/2$.
Nevertheless, arbitrary bits can be stored in omnific integers by separating their
transitions with limit positions.

\subsection{The block construction}

All products and sums in the next display are \emph{native surreal operations on
ordinal elements}. Define
\[
 f(\beta)=\omega\cdot\beta,\qquad L_\alpha=f(\alpha)+\omega,\qquad
 W_\beta=[f(\beta),f(\beta)+\omega).
\]
The intervals refer to the usual order of ordinals.

\begin{lemma}[Properties of the blocks]\label{hset:sf:lem:blocks}
The function $f$ is strictly increasing. Each $W_\beta$ has ordinary ordinal order
type $\omega$, and
\[
 f(\beta)+\omega=f(\beta+1).
\]
The intervals $W_\beta$ are pairwise disjoint. For $\beta>0$, $f(\beta)$ is a
nonzero limit ordinal.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Strict increase follows from the ordered-field law for multiplication by the
positive number $\omega$. Distributivity gives the displayed identity, since
native $\beta+1$ is the ordinal successor.

In Cantor normal form, natural multiplication by $\omega$ shifts each exponent
$\xi$ to $1\nsum\xi$. These exponents are positive. Thus $f(\beta)$ has no finite
term, and, when nonzero, is a limit ordinal. Because there is no term with
exponent below $1$, natural addition of $\omega$ agrees in this case with ordinary
right-addition of $\omega$. Hence the interval has order type $\omega$.

If $\beta<\gamma$, then $\beta+1\le\gamma$, so the right endpoint of $W_\beta$ is
at most the left endpoint of $W_\gamma$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Omnific bit storage]\label{hset:sf:thm:bitstorage}
For every ordinal $\alpha$ and every set $A\subseteq\alpha$, there is an omnific
integer $z_{\alpha,A}$ such that
\[
 \bd(z_{\alpha,A})=L_\alpha,\qquad
 \beta\in A\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \Plusp(z_{\alpha,A},\,\omega\cdot\beta)\quad(\beta<\alpha).
\]
The relation $z=z_{\alpha,A}$, when $A$ is given by its bits, has a uniform
first-order specification in $\Lb$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Define a sign sequence with domain $L_\alpha$ by
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:blockword}
 z_{\alpha,A}(\gamma)=+\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists\beta\in A\,\bigl(f(\beta)\le\gamma<f(\beta)+\omega\bigr).
\end{equation}
All other positions receive $-$. This is a set-sized function formed by
Separation. No choice is involved.

It has no adjacent opposite signs. A change into a positive block can occur only
at its left endpoint, a limit ordinal unless the endpoint is $0$. A change out of
such a block can occur only at its right endpoint, again a limit ordinal. No
successor position can therefore be the first position after a sign change. The
omnific sign criterion implies $z_{\alpha,A}\in\Oz$. The bit read at $f(\beta)$ is
precisely the membership bit of $\beta$ in $A$, by disjointness of the blocks.

For first-order specification, require the indicated birthday and, for every
ordinal $\gamma<L_\alpha$, require
\[
 \Plusp(z,\gamma)\ \longleftrightarrow\
 \exists\beta<\alpha\,\bigl(\Plusp(z,f(\beta))\land f(\beta)\le\gamma<f(\beta)+\omega\bigr).
\]
All ordinal quantifiers are guarded by $\Ord$. This states that each selected
block is positive and everything else is negative. Prescribing the selected bits
then determines the word uniquely.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Limit gaps are part of the construction]\label{hset:sf:rem:gaps}
Natural multiplication is not generally continuous in an ordinal argument. For
example,
\[
 \sup_{\beta<\omega^\omega}(\omega\cdot\beta)=\omega^\omega,
 \qquad \omega\cdot\omega^\omega=\omega^{\omega+1},
\]
where the products in parentheses and on the right are natural. The gap between
these two ordinals is substantial. The word \eqref{hset:sf:eq:blockword} fills such gaps
with minus signs. Treating the blocks as automatically contiguous at all limits
would be a mistake. The proof requires only disjointness and limit endpoints,
which have been established.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}\label{hset:sf:ex:blocks}
For $A=\{0,2\}\subseteq3$, the code has length equal to the native product
$4\omega$, or ordinary ordinal $\omega\cdot_{\mathrm{ord}}4$, and four successive
blocks of length $\omega$, with signs
\[
 \underbrace{+\,\cdots\,+}_{\omega}\quad
 \underbrace{-\,\cdots\,-}_{\omega}\quad
 \underbrace{+\,\cdots\,+}_{\omega}\quad
 \underbrace{-\,\cdots\,-}_{\omega}.
\]
The last block is the fixed negative terminal block. This notation describes a
sign function, not an assertion that the numerical value is a simple alternating
sum of four copies of $\omega$.
\end{example}

We henceforth use the native formula
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:read}
 \Readp(z,\beta)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \Ord(\beta)\land\Plusp(z,\omega\cdot\beta).
\end{equation}
For every bounded ordinal set, a suitable $z$ gives its characteristic predicate.
Arbitrary $z$ are also permitted: restricting the read predicate to any fixed
ordinal always gives an ordinary set. One need not require graph codes to use the
normalized block format.

For $\Dd=\No$, all these statements remain true because the constructed words
belong to the omnific subclass. Thus the same interpretation formulas can be used
in both numerical domains. (In $\No$ alone \cref{hset:sf:cor:subsets} would allow
the plain bit predicate; the block format is what makes one set of formulas serve
both domains.)

\subsection{A small pairing lemma avoids extra ordinal machinery}

\begin{lemma}[Native ordinal pairing]\label{hset:sf:lem:pairing}
For a positive ordinal $\kappa$, the function
\[
 \pi_\kappa(i,j)=\kappa\cdot i+j
\]
is injective on $\kappa\times\kappa$, with range below the native product
$\kappa^2$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $i<i'$, ordinal discreteness gives $i+1\le i'$. Hence
\[
 \kappa i+j<\kappa(i+1)\le\kappa i'\le\kappa i'+j'\qquad(j,j'<\kappa).
\]
For equal first coordinates, addition cancels. Finally,
$\kappa i+j<\kappa(i+1)\le\kappa^2$. The argument uses only ordered-ring laws and
the fact that native addition by $1$ is ordinal successor.
\end{proof}

This is \cref{hset:lem:pairing} ($\pi_\kappa=p_\kappa$; there the bound is
$\Lambda(\kappa)=\kappa^2+\kappa$).

Consequently, graphs of relations and functions between bounded ordinal domains
can themselves be represented by a single numerical bit code. The large base is a
coding device; it is not the graph's vertex set. Padding a well-founded
extensional graph with extra isolated vertices would generally destroy
extensionality.

\begin{lemma}[Ordinal pairing; source 14]\label{hset:sf:lem:pair}
The polynomial
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:pair}
 \varpi(\alpha,\beta)=(\alpha+\beta)^2+\alpha
\end{equation}
is injective on $\Ord^2$. For $\alpha,\beta<\delta$, its values are below the
ordinal
\[
 \Lambda_{14}(\delta)=(\delta+\delta)^2+\delta+1.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Natural sums and products of ordinals are ordinals. Write $s=\alpha+\beta$. Then
$0\le\alpha\le s$, so
\[
 s^2\le\varpi(\alpha,\beta)\le s^2+s.
\]
If the sum for another pair is $t>s$, ordinal discreteness gives $t\ge s+1$ in
surreal order. Therefore
\[
 t^2\ge(s+1)^2=s^2+2s+1>s^2+s.
\]
Thus the intervals associated with distinct sums are disjoint. Equal pairing
values force equal sums, then equal first coordinates by cancellation, and finally
equal second coordinates. The asserted bound follows by monotonicity in the
ordered field. The proof uses neither ordinary ordinal division nor a global
well-order of all sets.
\end{proof}

The polynomial is source 06's $\varpi$, and the injectivity argument is that of
\cref{hset:lem:pairing06}; source 14's contribution is the explicit bound
$\Lambda_{14}$. Source 14 uses it in $\B$, without blocks: a relation
$E\subseteq\delta^2$ is encoded by a single number $e$ of birthday
$\Lambda_{14}(\delta)$, with
\[
 iEj\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\Bit(e,\varpi(i,j)).
\]
At unused positions put minus signs. The length is fixed even for an empty
relation. A rectangular relation $R\subseteq\delta\times\eta$ can similarly be
put in a sign sequence of birthday $\Lambda_{14}(\delta+\eta)$. This supplies
function graphs and isomorphism witnesses without introducing set variables.
(Source~14.)

\subsection{Canonical finite-tuple encodings}\label{hset:sf:sub:tuple}

\begin{lemma}[Native tuple codec]\label{hset:sf:lem:tuple}
For each fixed positive integer $m$, there is a parameter-free definable
injection $\Dd^m\to\Dd$ with a definable inverse on its image.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For $(x_0,\ldots,x_{m-1})$, let $\alpha_i=\bd(x_i)$ and choose the uniquely
specified ordinal
\[
 \kappa=\max\bigl(2m+1,\alpha_0+1,\ldots,\alpha_{m-1}+1\bigr).
\]
In a $\kappa$-by-$\kappa$ array, use row $i<m$ for a single marker in column
$\alpha_i$, and row $m+i$ for the plus-sign positions of $x_i$. Set every other
entry to zero. Flatten the array with $\pi_\kappa$, set every index below
$\kappa^2$ outside the image of $\pi_\kappa$ to zero, then apply the normalized
block construction with $\alpha=\kappa^2$.

All these requirements have fixed first-order descriptions for fixed $m$, by the
preceding lemmas. The output word is unique. Its birthday determines $\kappa$,
because $\kappa\mapsto f(\kappa^2)+\omega$ is strictly increasing on positive
ordinals. The marker rows recover the lengths, and the other rows recover every
sign. The inputs are therefore unique.
\end{proof}

This lemma will let the class-theoretic construction use classes of single
numbers even when the set interpretation is conveniently written using triples.
Source 14's packing of tuples (\cref{hset:sf:lem:pack}) reaches the same end by
a set-theoretic sign construction translated through the interpretation.

\section{Recovering the set universe from numerical graphs}
\label{hset:sf:sec:graphs}

We now work in either of the canonical $\Lb$-structures on $\Dd=\No$ or
$\Dd=\Oz$. Every quantifier in the definitions below ranges over numbers. Bounds
such as $i<\nu$ mean that $i$ is also required to satisfy $\Ord(i)$.

\subsection{Valid codes}

A candidate code is a triple
\[
 c=(\nu_c,r_c,z_c),
\]
where $\nu_c$ is a nonzero ordinal and $r_c<\nu_c$ is an ordinal. Put
$\kappa_c=\nu_c+1$, and define
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:edge}
 R_c(i,j)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ i,j<\nu_c\ \land\ \Readp(z_c,\kappa_c i+j).
\end{equation}
The orientation is \emph{member to containing set}: $iR_cj$ will mean that the
collapse of $i$ belongs to the collapse of $j$.

The formula $\Valid(c)$ adds the following conditions.

\paragraph{Well-foundedness.}
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:wf}
 \forall w\Bigl[\bigl(\exists i<\nu_c\,\Readp(w,i)\bigr)\longrightarrow
 \exists i<\nu_c\bigl(\Readp(w,i)\land
 \forall j<\nu_c\,(\Readp(w,j)\to\neg R_c(j,i))\bigr)\Bigr].
\end{equation}
Every nonempty subset of $\nu_c$ is tested, because every such subset has a
numerical code by \cref{hset:sf:thm:bitstorage}.

\paragraph{Extensionality.}
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:extgraph}
 \forall i,j<\nu_c\Bigl[\bigl(\forall k<\nu_c\,(R_c(k,i)\leftrightarrow R_c(k,j))\bigr)
 \longrightarrow i=j\Bigr].
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Accessibility from the root.}
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:access}
\begin{split}
 \forall w\Bigl[&\bigl(\Readp(w,r_c)\land
 \forall i,j<\nu_c\,((\Readp(w,j)\land R_c(i,j))\to\Readp(w,i))\bigr)\\
 &\hspace{23mm}\longrightarrow\forall i<\nu_c\,\Readp(w,i)\Bigr].
\end{split}
\end{equation}
This says that the only predecessor-closed subset containing the root is the
whole graph. It is equivalent to every vertex being reachable from the root by a
finite sequence of downward membership edges. The displayed version avoids
introducing a separate finite-path codec.

These are finite first-order formulas after the previously defined abbreviations
are expanded. Their subset quantification is numerical quantification over $w$,
not a second-order quantifier.

Source 14's code is a triple $c=(\delta,r,e)$ with $\delta>0$ an ordinal, root
$r<\delta$, and $e$ coding $E_c\subseteq\delta^2$ by $\varpi$; its three
conditions are the same: every nonempty subset $A\subseteq\delta$, quantified by
its birthday-$\delta$ sign code $t$, has an $E_c$-minimal element,
\[
 \forall t\,\bigl[\bd(t)=\delta\land\exists i<\delta\,\Bit(t,i)\longrightarrow
 \exists i<\delta\,(\Bit(t,i)\land\forall j<\delta\,[\Bit(t,j)\to\neg(jE_ci)])\bigr];
\]
distinct vertices have distinct predecessor sets; and every downward-closed subset
of $\delta$ which contains $r$ is all of $\delta$ (``root generation''), downward
closure being $\forall i,j<\delta\,[(j\in A\land iE_cj)\to i\in A]$. Its
$\Code(c)$ is the conjunction of these, including minus padding at unused
positions; root generation is preferred to paths because it avoids prematurely
introducing a code for natural-number sequences. These are the conditions of
\cref{hset:sec:codes}.

\subsection{Equality and membership of represented sets}

Let $c,d$ be valid codes. To code a candidate map between their vertices, choose
the definable ordinal
\[
 \nu_{cd}=\max(\nu_c,\nu_d)+1
\]
and read the relation
\[
 Y_h(i,j)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ i<\nu_c\land j<\nu_d\land\Readp(h,\nu_{cd}i+j).
\]
A finite formula says that $Y_h$ is the graph of a total injective map from
$\nu_c$ to $\nu_d$. Additional formulas express surjectivity, root preservation,
and preservation and reflection of edges. Define $\Eqc(c,d)$ by the existence of
such a bijection satisfying
\[
 Y_h(r_c,r_d)
\]
and, for all pairs of matched vertices,
\[
 Y_h(i,j)\land Y_h(i',j')\ \longrightarrow\ \bigl(R_c(i,i')\longleftrightarrow R_d(j,j')\bigr).
\]
Thus $\Eqc$ is rooted graph isomorphism.

For membership, it is useful to permit a predecessor-closed embedding instead of
an isomorphism onto the whole target. Require the total injective map to preserve
and reflect edges:
\[
 Y_h(i,j)\land Y_h(i',j')\ \longrightarrow\ \bigl(R_c(i,i')\longleftrightarrow R_d(j,j')\bigr).
\]
In addition, require it to satisfy
\[
 Y_h(i,j)\land R_d(k,j)\ \longrightarrow\ \exists i'<\nu_c\,Y_h(i',k).
\]
Its image is then closed under all predecessors in the target graph. Define
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:mem}
 \Memc(c,d)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \exists h\,\exists j<\nu_d\,
 \left[\begin{array}{l}
 Y_h\text{ is such a predecessor-closed embedding},\\
 Y_h(r_c,j)\ \land\ R_d(j,r_d)
 \end{array}\right].
\end{equation}
The verbal phrase stands for the finite conjunction just specified. It can be
expanded without any additional quantifier sort.

\paragraph{Source 14's membership.} For a vertex $j$ of $d$, its downward hull is
\[
 \operatorname{Hull}_d(j,i)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \text{every downward-closed
 subset containing $j$ contains $i$},
\]
subsets again being numerically quantified; $c\in_* d$ means that for some
$jE_dr_d$ there is a root-preserving isomorphism from $c$ onto the induced graph
on the hull of $j$ in $d$. Surjectivity here means onto that hull, not onto the
entire domain of $d$; a rectangular code expresses this condition without
relabelling the hull by a new ordinal. Source 14's $c\sim_*d$ is $\Eqc(c,d)$. (Observation of this merge:) An
edge-preserving and reflecting embedding with predecessor-closed image containing
$j$ and sending the root to $j$ has image exactly the hull of $j$, by
accessibility of the source graph; so the two membership formulas are equivalent
on valid codes.

\subsection{Collapse and its range}

\begin{theorem}[The graph interpretation; sources 13, 14]\label{hset:sf:thm:graph}
In $\ZFC$, the formulas $\Valid,\Eqc,\Memc$ define an interpretation $\I_{\Dd}$
satisfying
\[
 \I_{\Dd}(\Dd)=\bigl(\{c:\Valid(c)\}/\Eqc,\Memc\bigr)\cong(V,\in).
\]
The isomorphism is the root value under Mostowski collapse and is parameter-free
definable in set theory.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For a valid $c$, the decoded relation is a set relation on an ordinal. The subset
coverage in \cref{hset:sf:thm:bitstorage} makes
\eqref{hset:sf:eq:wf} and \eqref{hset:sf:eq:extgraph} exactly well-foundedness
and extensionality, so Mostowski collapse gives a unique isomorphism
\[
 F_c:(\nu_c,R_c)\longrightarrow(T_c,\in)
\]
onto a transitive set $T_c$. Let $a_c=F_c(r_c)$. Accessibility gives
\[
 T_c=\TC(\{a_c\}).
\]
Indeed, closing the root under finitely many predecessor steps corresponds to its
transitive closure, and \eqref{hset:sf:eq:access} makes this the whole domain.
This closure itself need not be finite.

If $c,d$ are rooted-isomorphic, uniqueness of collapse gives $a_c=a_d$.
Conversely, equal root values give $T_c=T_d=\TC(\{a_c\})$, so $F_d^{-1}F_c$ is a
rooted isomorphism. Its graph is a subset of a bounded ordinal rectangle and
therefore has a numerical code. Consequently
\[
 \Eqc(c,d)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad a_c=a_d.
\]

A predecessor-closed embedding in \eqref{hset:sf:eq:mem} identifies the source
collapse with the transitive closure of its image root. One can see this directly
by well-founded induction: closure of the image under predecessors and
preservation and reflection of edges give
\[
 F_c(i)=F_d(Y_h(i))
\]
for every source vertex. If its root maps to $jR_dr_d$, then $a_c=F_d(j)\in a_d$.
Conversely, if $a_c\in a_d$, let $j$ be the unique vertex of $d$ whose value is
$a_c$. The map obtained from the two collapses embeds $c$ onto the predecessor
closure of $j$, and again has a numerical graph code. Thus
\[
 \Memc(c,d)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad a_c\in a_d.
\]

Finally, given any set $a$, apply Choice to the set $\TC(\{a\})$, choose a
bijection from an ordinal $\nu$ to this transitive closure, and transport
membership and the distinguished element $a$. The resulting graph is
well-founded, extensional, and accessible. Pairing and bit storage produce its
numerical code. This establishes surjectivity.

The relation defining $F_c$ is an ordinary set-theoretic formula asserting that a
set function is the collapse of the specified graph. Its root value therefore
defines the asserted isomorphism.
\end{proof}

For $\Dd=\No$ this is the forward half of \cref{hset:thm:chy}; for $\Dd=\Oz$ it is
new in the collection.

\begin{lemma}[Collapse and range; source 14]\label{hset:sf:lem:collapse}
Every valid code has a unique Mostowski collapse. Its root collapses to a set,
denoted $a_c$. Every set occurs as $a_c$ for some valid code. Moreover, the full
collapsed vertex set is $\TC(\{a_c\})$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof (source 14)]
The relation is a well-founded extensional relation on the set $\delta$.
Well-founded recursion defines
\[
 F(i)=\{F(j):jE_ci\}.
\]
Extensionality, by well-founded induction, makes $F$ injective. The range is
transitive, and root generation says that it is exactly the transitive closure of
the singleton containing $F(r)$. Uniqueness follows from the same recursion.

Conversely, for a set $a$, Choice supplies an ordinal $\delta$ and a bijection
between $\delta$ and the set $\TC(\{a\})$. Pull back membership, choose the vertex
representing $a$ as root, and use \cref{hset:sf:lem:pair} to encode the relation.
This code is well-founded, extensional, and root-generated. The enumeration is
chosen for this one set only. A global definable choice of enumerations is not
used.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Interpretation of the set universe; source 14]\label{hset:sf:thm:setinterpret}
The formulas above give a fixed finite-dimensional interpretation $\I$ satisfying
\[
 \I(\B)=(\Code/{\Eqc},\in_*)\cong(V,\in).
\]
More precisely, in the background set theory the definable relation
$\operatorname{col}(c)=a$, asserting that the root collapse of $c$ is $a$, is the
graph of an isomorphism on equivalence classes.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Second proof (source 14)]
By uniqueness of collapse and root generation,
$c\mathrel{\Eqc}d\iff a_c=a_d$. If an isomorphism exists, the collapses agree. In
the reverse direction, equal root collapses give the same transitive closure of a
singleton, and composing the inverse collapse bijections gives the required
root-preserving isomorphism.

The collapse of the hull of $j$ is $\TC(\{F_d(j)\})$. Consequently the membership
definition says exactly $c\in_*d\iff a_c\in a_d$. Existence of a code for every set
is \cref{hset:sf:lem:collapse}. The two displayed equivalences prove the
equivalence-relation and congruence conditions, as well as the asserted
isomorphism. Every condition is a fixed first-order formula, not a scheme
quantifying over formulas.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[The set $2=\{\varnothing,\{\varnothing\}\}$; sources 13, 14]
\label{hset:sf:ex:two}
Use vertices $0,1,2$, root $2$, and edges
\[
 R=\{(0,1),(0,2),(1,2)\}.
\]
With base $\kappa=4$, the positive relation positions are $\{1,2,6\}\subseteq16$.
Any omnific code $z$ satisfying $\Readp(z,j)\leftrightarrow j\in\{1,2,6\}$ on
$j<16$ gives a valid triple $(3,2,z)$. Relabeling the three vertices changes the
raw triple but not its equivalence class. The empty set has a one-vertex graph
with no edges. These two examples already explain why native numerical equality
and interpreted set equality have different jobs. (Source~13.)

In source 14's coding the same graph has pairing positions $\varpi(0,1)=1$,
$\varpi(0,2)=4$, $\varpi(1,2)=10$. Put plus signs there and minus signs at the other
positions in a sequence of length $\Lambda_{14}(3)=40$. The long padding is
intentionally not an efficient finite code; its benefit is a uniform polynomial
bound at every ordinal. (Source~14.)
\end{example}

\paragraph{Why this is not merely a global relabelling (source 14).} The domain,
code equivalence, and membership formulas are read directly from arithmetic,
order, and birthday. Neither a global map from $V$ to ordinals nor a primitive
membership relation on numbers was added. The code quotient avoids the hidden
global choice that a unique canonical number for every set would require.
Conversely, the construction does not claim an intrinsic interpretation from the
pure real-closed field: birthday carries indispensable information.

\subsection{The exact use of Choice}\label{hset:sf:sub:choice}

\begin{proposition}[Range over $\ZF$]\label{hset:sf:prop:hwo}
Over $\ZF$, the same ordinal-graph collapse has range exactly
\[
 \HWO=\{a:\TC(\{a\})\text{ is well-orderable}\}.
\]
Its surjectivity onto $V$ is equivalent to the Axiom of Choice.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
A collapse with ordinal domain makes its transitive range well-orderable, so its
root value belongs to $\HWO$. Conversely, a well-order of $\TC(\{a\})$ gives an
ordinal labeling and hence a code. If the collapse is onto $V$, every set belongs
to $\HWO$; in particular every set is a subset of a well-orderable transitive
closure and is itself well-orderable. This is equivalent to Choice. The converse
was proved in \cref{hset:sf:thm:graph}.
\end{proof}

This is the choice-sensitive boundary of this particular interpretation, already
identified in this report's \cref{hset:thm:choice,hset:prop:internal-choice}
(source 06's), which source 13 credits. It is not an impossibility theorem for
every conceivable numerical interpretation of $\ZF$. Nor must $\HWO$ be a model of
$\ZF$: if $\Pow(\omega)$ is not well-orderable, every individual real belongs to
$\HWO$ but the full collection $\Pow(\omega)$ does not, and the internal power-set
axiom at $\omega$ fails. (For $\Dd=\Oz$ the block codes are built without Choice,
\cref{hset:sf:thm:bitstorage}, so the omnific range is $\HWO$ too.)

Global Choice is absent from the positive proof. Each set merely has some graph
code. A definable global choice of \emph{one} such code for each set would be
additional structure: a selected graph would let one choose the member with least
vertex label from any nonempty represented set. The quotient interpretation does
not make this selection.

\section{Recovering every original number}
\label{hset:sf:sec:roundtrip}

The previous theorem establishes mutual interpretation only after we also recall
that set theory defines $\Dd$ and its arithmetic. We now verify the extra
condition needed for bi-interpretability.

\subsection{The canonical set-theoretic interpretation}

Fix the standard parameter-free set-theoretic definitions:
\begin{itemize}
\item $\operatorname{Is}_{\No}(s)$: $s$ is a sign function with ordinal domain;
\item $\operatorname{Is}_{\Oz}(s)$: $\operatorname{Is}_{\No}(s)$, with the omnific
sign criterion;
\item the Conway arithmetic relations on these sign functions;
\item lexicographic numerical order and comparison of domain ordinals.
\end{itemize}
Write $\J_{\Dd}$ for this interpretation of the numerical language in set
theory. Thus $\J_{\Dd}(M)$ is the canonical $\Lb$-structure on $\Dd^M$.

Inside the numerical interpretation of sets we may apply these same fixed
formulas, translating them through $\I_{\Dd}$. For example,
$\operatorname{Is}_{\Dd}^{\I_{\Dd}}(c)$ says that the set represented by $c$ is
internally one of the relevant canonical sign functions. There is no
quantification over all definitions or all truths. Only fixed first-order
formulas are being translated.

\paragraph{The set-to-number interpretation, as source 14 describes it.} Let $\J$
be the usual definable construction of surreal numbers inside set theory. Its
domain is the set-coded functions from an ordinal into $\{0,1\}$, with zero
representing minus and one representing plus. Comparison is lexicographic with an
absent sign lying between minus and plus. Birthday is the domain ordinal,
represented again as an all-plus sign sequence. The ring operations are the
definable Conway operations. For completeness, their definability here does not
require a global satisfaction predicate or an elementary class recursion
principle. Canonical options of a number are its shorter prefixes on the
appropriate side. Addition is determined recursively by
\[
 x+y=\{x^L+y,\ x+y^L\mid x^R+y,\ x+y^R\}.
\]
Multiplication uses the usual lower expressions $x^Ly+xy^L-x^Ly^L$,
$x^Ry+xy^R-x^Ry^R$, and upper expressions $x^Ly+xy^R-x^Ly^R$,
$x^Ry+xy^L-x^Ry^L$. For any fixed inputs the required recursion has set-sized
input data, so ordinary set recursion gives definable operation graphs. The
least-birthday separator of a set cut is itself set-definable. These are the
standard arithmetic constructions \cite{Conway,Gonshor}.

\subsection{Linking native ordinals to represented ordinals}

Define $\OG(\alpha,e)$ as follows: $\alpha$ is a native ordinal, $e$ is a valid
set code, and $e$ is equivalent to a graph with domain $\alpha+1$, root $\alpha$,
and edges
\[
 iRj\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad i<j<\alpha+1.
\]
Existence of a code for this graph follows from bit storage. The quantifier over
that code avoids selecting a representative.

\begin{lemma}\label{hset:sf:lem:og}
For a native ordinal $\alpha$ and a valid code $e$, under the collapse
isomorphism,
\[
 \OG(\alpha,e)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad a_e=\alpha.
\]
In particular, every code representing the ordinal $\alpha$ satisfies
$\OG(\alpha,e)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The displayed graph is the membership graph of the transitive set $\alpha+1$, with
root $\alpha$. Its collapse is the identity. Equivalence of valid graph codes is
equality of root values.
\end{proof}

\subsection{An explicit formula for the second isomorphism}

In the language of set theory, let $\operatorname{Dom}(s,\lambda)$ assert that
the function $s$ has domain $\lambda$, and let $\operatorname{Val}_+(s,\beta)$
assert that its value at $\beta$ is the chosen set representative of $+$. For
definiteness, use $0$ for $-$ and $1$ for $+$.

Define $\Cmp_{\Dd}(x,c)$ to say:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $c$ is valid and $\operatorname{Is}_{\Dd}^{\I_{\Dd}}(c)$;
\item with $\lambda=\bd(x)$, there is a code $e$ such that $\OG(\lambda,e)$ and
$\operatorname{Dom}^{\I_{\Dd}}(c,e)$;
\item for every native ordinal $\beta<\lambda$ and every code $e$ with
$\OG(\beta,e)$,
\[
 \operatorname{Val}_+^{\I_{\Dd}}(c,e)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\Plusp(x,\beta).
\]
\end{enumerate}
These clauses define a single first-order numerical formula. Code variables stand
for fixed triples and can be expanded into three ordinary variables each. The
quantifier binding $\lambda$ has scope over both the domain and sign clauses:
formally their conjunction is placed inside
$\exists\lambda\,(\Ord(\lambda)\land\EqB(x,\lambda)\land\cdots)$.

\begin{theorem}[Full numerical round trip; sources 13, 14]\label{hset:sf:thm:roundtrip}
For $x\in\Dd^M$ and valid $c$, in every $M\models\ZFC$, evaluated internally,
\[
 \Cmp_{\Dd}(x,c)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad a_c=x
\]
as equality of canonical sign-function sets. Consequently $\Cmp_{\Dd}$ defines an
isomorphism
\[
 \J_{\Dd}(M)\ \cong\ \J_{\Dd}(\I_{\Dd}(\J_{\Dd}(M)))
\]
on the entire original numerical domain.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{hset:sf:lem:og}, native ordinal $\beta$ and every code $e$ used for it
have the same value $\beta$ after collapse. The domain clause therefore says that
$a_c$ has domain $\bd(x)$, and the last clause says that its signs agree with $x$
at every position of that domain. Sign-function extensionality gives $a_c=x$. The
converse follows from the same observations.

Existence of an image follows from \cref{hset:sf:thm:graph} applied to the set
$x$. The image is unique modulo $\Eqc$; distinct original numbers have distinct
images. Every reconstructed canonical number has an original preimage because
collapse is onto and preserves the fixed definition of $\operatorname{Is}_{\Dd}$.

Finally, the canonical set-theoretic definitions of $+$, $-$, $\cdot$, $<$,
$\precb$, $0$, and $1$ are invariant under the collapse isomorphism. Thus the
comparison just proved preserves every numerical symbol. Equivalently,
$\Cmp_{\Dd}$ is the inverse of the map induced by $\J_{\Dd}$ on the collapse
isomorphism.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Structure-level bi-interpretation]\label{hset:sf:cor:structure}
The canonical structures
\[
 (\No,0,1,+,-,\cdot,<,\precb)\quad\text{and}\quad(\Oz,0,1,+,-,\cdot,<,\precb)
\]
are each parameter-free bi-interpretable, formula by formula in $\ZFC$, with
$(V,\in)$.
\end{corollary}

The surreal instance gives a proof in the setting of the previously announced CHY
result (\cref{hset:thm:chy}). The omnific instance follows from the additional
block-storage lemma and initiality. No use of a monomial-map predicate,
fractional birthday predicate, membership primitive, or auxiliary set sort is made
in its native language. (Source~13.)

\paragraph{Source 14's comparison.} For an ordinal surreal $\alpha$, let
$c^{\mathrm{ord}}_\alpha$ denote the code with vertex domain $\alpha+1$, root
$\alpha$, and relation $iEj\iff i<j$. Its root collapse is the set ordinal
$\alpha$. This is a definable code: its relation is padded using
$\Lambda_{14}(\alpha+1)$. Writing $c^{\mathrm{ord}}_\alpha$ is an abbreviation
for the formula defining that triple, not an additional primitive function.
Define $\rho(x,c)$ to mean that $c$ is a set code which, in the interpreted set
universe $\I(\B)$, is a function with domain $c^{\mathrm{ord}}_{\bd(x)}$, values
in the interpreted set $\{0,1\}$, and satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:rho}
 c(c^{\mathrm{ord}}_\alpha)=1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\Bit(x,\alpha)
 \qquad(\alpha<\bd(x)).
\end{equation}
For the values zero and one in this formula use the interpreted von Neumann
integers, not the original ring elements. The distinction avoids a type error.
Evaluation, functionhood, and domain are ordinary fixed set-theoretic formulas
translated by $\I$. Consequently \eqref{hset:sf:eq:rho}, together with these
conditions, is a single first-order $\Ls$-formula in $x$ and the triple $c$. It
names a specific code for each ordinal where $\Cmp_{\Dd}$ quantifies over all
codes satisfying $\OG$; the two formulas define the same relation on valid codes.

\begin{proposition}[Definable numerical recovery; source 14]\label{hset:sf:prop:recovery}
$\ZFC$ proves that $\rho$ induces an isomorphism
\[
 \B\cong\J(\I(\B)).
\]
Together with $\operatorname{col}$, it witnesses a structural bi-interpretation
between the canonical birthday-enriched surreal structure and the set universe.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}[Second proof of the round trip for $\No$ (source 14)]
Under the collapse isomorphism from \cref{hset:sf:thm:setinterpret},
$c^{\mathrm{ord}}_\alpha$ becomes the actual ordinal $\alpha$. Thus a code
satisfying $\rho(x,c)$ collapses to exactly the sign function defining $x$. This
proves existence and uniqueness modulo $\Eqc$, and also injectivity. Every sign
function in the recovered set universe is the sign function of a surreal in the
original canonical domain, giving surjectivity.

Order and birthday are determined by the same signs on both sides. The
set-theoretic definitions of the Conway operations are the same formulas in the
two set universes related by collapse. An isomorphism preserves every fixed
definable construction; hence it preserves these operation graphs. Equivalently,
one can induct on the relevant set-sized recursion tables. The resulting
bijection therefore preserves $0,1,+,-,\cdot,<,\bd$, not just the underlying order.
\end{proof}

\section{Explicit formal axiomatic theories}
\label{hset:sf:sec:axioms}

The structure-level result is not yet a displayed axiom system. This section gives
one, using fixed first-order formulas and an effective translation of the $\ZFC$
axiom schemes. Its correctness does not depend on the final form of any
unpublished intrinsic surreal-arithmetic axiomatization.

\subsection{A general principle}

\begin{theorem}[Axioms for inverse interpretations; sources 13, 14]\label{hset:sf:thm:axiomprinciple}
Let $S$ be a recursively axiomatized theory in a finite language. Let $K$ define,
in $S$, a structure in a finite language $L$. Suppose that:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item fixed $L$-formulas $I$ interpret the language of $S$;
\item $S$ proves admissibility of $I$ in its $K$-structure and proves a fixed
definable isomorphism $I(K(M))\cong M$;
\item a fixed $L$-formula $C$, evaluated in the $K$-structure, is proved in $S$ to
give an isomorphism
\[
 K(M)\cong K(I(K(M))).
\]
\end{enumerate}
There is a recursively axiomatized $L$-theory $T$ bi-interpretable with $S$. It
consists of
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:axioms}
 \boxed{\;T=\operatorname{Adm}(I)\ \cup\ \{\sigma^I:\sigma\text{ is an axiom of }S\}\ \cup\
 \{\operatorname{Iso}(C)\}.\;}
\end{equation}
The first and last expressions denote finite lists of first-order sentences.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By its first two groups of axioms, $T$ admits $I$ as an interpretation of $S$.
Conversely, $S$ proves the $K$-translations of the admissibility sentences by
hypothesis. It proves $(\sigma^I)^K$ because $I(K(M))$ is definably isomorphic to
its own universe. It proves the translated isomorphism assertions by the last
hypothesis. Thus $K$ interprets $T$ in $S$. The source-side comparison and the
explicitly asserted comparison $C$ are the two definable isomorphisms required.

All translations are finite syntactic substitutions and relativizations. The
languages are finite, so admissibility and preservation of their symbols require
only finitely many sentences. Enumerating the axioms of $S$ and translating them
enumerates the axioms of $T$.
\end{proof}

In this proof $K$ is a construction, not the letter $K$ of a field; the instances
below take $K=\J_{\Dd}$.

\begin{lemma}[Round-trip axiom construction; source 14]\label{hset:sf:lem:rt}
Let $U$ be a recursively axiomatized theory. Suppose $\J$ is a fixed definable
