construction of structures in a finite language $L$ inside $U$. Suppose fixed
formulas $\I$, $\eta$, and $\rho$ satisfy, provably in $U$:
\[
 \I(\J(M))\models U,\qquad \eta:M\cong \I(\J(M)),\qquad
 \rho:\J(M)\cong \J(\I(\J(M))).
\]
Then there is a recursively axiomatized $L$-theory $T$ bi-interpretable with $U$,
whose canonical models are the $\J(M)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Second proof (source 14)]
Let $\operatorname{Val}(\I)$ be the finite set of interpretation-validity
sentences for the domain, quotient equivalence, and congruence formulas. Let
$\operatorname{Iso}(\rho)$ be the finite collection saying that $\rho$ is an
isomorphism from the original $L$-structure onto the structure obtained by the
composite $\J\circ\I$. After unwinding the fixed interpretations, these are
$L$-sentences. They say that $\rho$ is total, single-valued modulo interpreted
equality, injective, surjective, and preserves each primitive symbol of $L$. Take
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:rtaxioms}
 T=\operatorname{Val}(\I)\ \cup\ \{\sigma^\I:\sigma\in\operatorname{Ax}(U)\}\ \cup\ \operatorname{Iso}(\rho).
\end{equation}
This is a recursively enumerable axiom set in a finite language. It proves that
$\I$ interprets $U$. Conversely the hypotheses prove that $\J$ interprets $T$ in
$U$. The two definable isomorphisms are respectively $\eta$, already proved in $U$,
and $\rho$, asserted by the coherence axioms in $T$. Thus the two theories are
bi-interpretable.

There is no assertion of a global truth predicate in \eqref{hset:sf:eq:rtaxioms}.
The middle term is an external, effectively specified axiom scheme; only
individual axioms are translated.
\end{proof}

\Cref{hset:sf:thm:axiomprinciple,hset:sf:lem:rt} are the same recipe, proved
independently: $\operatorname{Adm}(I)=\operatorname{Val}(\I)$ and $C=\rho$.

The isomorphism assertions are \emph{axioms}, not consequences asserted without
proof from a small ordered-ring fragment. The source-side proof that they hold in
the intended number structure is the substantive requirement. Here it was
discharged by \cref{hset:sf:thm:roundtrip}. (Source~13.) Source 14 adds that it
is easy to confuse a statement about the intended structure with a first-order
theory having the required equivalence in \emph{all} models; the recipe closes
that gap.

\subsection{The two numerical theories}

\begin{definition}\label{hset:sf:def:nativetheories}
For $\Dd=\No,\Oz$, define $\Tth_{\Dd}$ by \eqref{hset:sf:eq:axioms}, using
$S=\ZFC$, the numerical language $\Lb$, the graph interpretation $\I_{\Dd}$, the
canonical number interpretation $\J_{\Dd}$, and the signwise formula
$\Cmp_{\Dd}$. Write $\Tth_{\No}$ and $\Tth_{\Oz}$ for these theories.
\end{definition}

Here are the three axiom groups in explicit form.

\paragraph{Group A: quotient admissibility.} Assert:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\exists c\,\Valid(c)$;
\item $\Eqc$ is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive on valid codes;
\item if $\Eqc(c,c')$ and $\Eqc(d,d')$, then $\Memc(c,d)\leftrightarrow\Memc(c',d')$.
\end{enumerate}
Code variables are fixed triples. These are finite first-order sentences in the
numerical language.

\paragraph{Group B: the translated set axioms.} Take the usual axioms of $\ZFC$,
including every Separation and Replacement instance. Replace equality by $\Eqc$,
membership by $\Memc$, and each set quantifier by a quantifier over valid triples.
For example,
\[
 \exists x\,\varphi(x)\quad\mapsto\quad
 \exists \nu_x\,\exists r_x\,\exists z_x\,\bigl(\Valid(\nu_x,r_x,z_x)\land\varphi^{\I}\bigr).
\]
Universal quantifiers are relativized correspondingly.

\paragraph{Group C: the numerical comparison is an isomorphism.} Let
$\operatorname{Is}_{\Dd}^{\I}(c)$ assert that $c$ represents a canonical
$\Dd$-number in the interpreted universe. Assert the universal closures of
\begin{align*}
 &\Cmp_{\Dd}(x,c)\ \longrightarrow\ \Valid(c)\land \operatorname{Is}_{\Dd}^{\I}(c),\\
 &\forall x\,\exists c\,\Cmp_{\Dd}(x,c),\\
 &\bigl(\Cmp_{\Dd}(x,c)\land\Eqc(c,d)\bigr)\ \longrightarrow\ \Cmp_{\Dd}(x,d),\\
 &\bigl(\Cmp_{\Dd}(x,c)\land \Cmp_{\Dd}(x,d)\bigr)\ \longrightarrow\ \Eqc(c,d),\\
 &\bigl(\Cmp_{\Dd}(x,c)\land \Cmp_{\Dd}(y,d)\land\Eqc(c,d)\bigr)\ \longrightarrow\ x=y,\\
 &\Valid(c)\land \operatorname{Is}_{\Dd}^{\I}(c)\ \longrightarrow\ \exists x\,\Cmp_{\Dd}(x,c).
\end{align*}
Valid-code guards are understood for every occurrence of $\Eqc$. The equality
$x=y$ in the injectivity axiom is \emph{raw numerical equality}, not code
equivalence.

For preservation of addition, assert
\[
\begin{split}
 \Cmp_{\Dd}(x,c)\land \Cmp_{\Dd}(y,d)\land \Cmp_{\Dd}(z,e)\ \longrightarrow\
 \bigl(x+y=z\ \longleftrightarrow\ \operatorname{Add}_{\Dd}^{\I}(c,d,e)\bigr).
\end{split}
\]
Include the corresponding sentences for multiplication and negation, and for
constants $0,1$. Finally, assert
\[
\begin{split}
 \Cmp_{\Dd}(x,c)\land \Cmp_{\Dd}(y,d)\ \longrightarrow\
 &(x<y\longleftrightarrow\operatorname{Less}_{\Dd}^{\I}(c,d)),\\
 \Cmp_{\Dd}(x,c)\land \Cmp_{\Dd}(y,d)\ \longrightarrow\
 &(x\precb y\longleftrightarrow\operatorname{Earlier}_{\Dd}^{\I}(c,d)).
\end{split}
\]
All predicates on the right are translations of fixed set-theoretic definitions of
the canonical numerical structure.

This describes an actual recursive axiom list. It does not say to include all
truths of the intended numbers, and it does not introduce the displayed
abbreviations as new primitive symbols.

\begin{theorem}[Numerical foundations for $\ZFC$; sources 13, 14]\label{hset:sf:thm:theories}
The theories $\Tth_{\No}$ and $\Tth_{\Oz}$ are parameter-free bi-interpretable
with $\ZFC$:
\[
 \Tth_{\No}\bi\ZFC\bi\Tth_{\Oz}.
\]
Every $M\models\ZFC$ yields the canonical model $\J_{\Dd}(M)$ of $\Tth_{\Dd}$.
Every $A\models\Tth_{\Dd}$ is definably isomorphic to the full canonical
$\Dd$-structure of the set universe $\I_{\Dd}(A)$ which it interprets.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{hset:sf:thm:axiomprinciple} using
\cref{hset:sf:thm:graph,hset:sf:thm:roundtrip}. The last sentence is exactly the
Group C comparison.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[An explicit surreal foundational theory; source 14]
\label{hset:sf:def:SArt}
$\SArt$ is the theory \eqref{hset:sf:eq:rtaxioms} obtained by taking $U=\ZFC$, the
code formulas of \cref{hset:sf:sec:graphs} in source 14's form, the construction
$\J$ above, and the formula $\rho$ in \eqref{hset:sf:eq:rho}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Surreal-only set foundations; source 14]\label{hset:sf:thm:surrealtheory}
The theory $\SArt$ is parameter-free bi-interpretable with $\ZFC$. Every model
$N\models\SArt$ is definably isomorphic to $\J(\I(N))$, where $\I(N)\models\ZFC$.
In particular, it contains no unintended extra numerical component invisible to
the interpreted set universe.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof (source 14)]
\Cref{hset:sf:thm:setinterpret,hset:sf:prop:recovery} verify the hypotheses of
\cref{hset:sf:lem:rt}. The last statement is precisely the surjectivity and
injectivity clause for $\rho$.
\end{proof}

$\SArt$ and $\Tth_{\No}$ are the same recipe applied to two presentations of the
same interpretation, in the definitionally equivalent languages $\Ls$ and $\Lb$
(\cref{hset:sf:lem:ord}); both are bi-interpretable with $\ZFC$, hence with each
other. This definition is intentionally not a claim that the candidate intrinsic
SA axiom list in the CHY slides \cite{CHYCUNY} has been fully verified here. It is
a different, mechanically specifiable presentation. It sacrifices axiomatic
elegance to make the theory-level round trip explicit. Its primitive vocabulary
remains numerical; sets occur only as interpreted codes. (Source~14.)

\subsection{The meaning of the axioms}

There is no hidden set sort in $\Tth_{\Oz}$. A quantified triple $(\nu,r,z)$
consists of three omnific integers, and the assertion that it represents a set is
a numerical formula. For example, the singleton operation on represented sets is
specified by
\[
 \operatorname{Singleton}(c,a)\ \Longleftrightarrow\
 \forall d\bigl(\Valid(d)\to(\Memc(d,c)\leftrightarrow\Eqc(d,a))\bigr),
\]
with validity of $a,c$ included. Pairing uses $\Eqc(d,a)\lor\Eqc(d,b)$; union uses
$\exists e\,(\Valid(e)\land\Memc(e,a)\land\Memc(d,e))$. For valid $p,a$, the
power-set condition is
\[
 \forall c\Bigl[\Valid(c)\to\Bigl(\Memc(c,p)\leftrightarrow
 \forall d\,(\Valid(d)\to(\Memc(d,c)\to\Memc(d,a)))\Bigr)\Bigr].
\]
These operations are on code equivalence classes. Native numerical addition
remains ordinary omnific addition; it is not being redefined as set union.

Source 14 displays several translated axioms the same way, variables
$a,b,c,u,v,w$ abbreviating valid triples:
\begin{align*}
 \text{Extensionality:}\quad&\forall a,b\,[\forall c(\Memc(c,a)\leftrightarrow \Memc(c,b))\to \Eqc(a,b)],\\
 \text{Pairing:}\quad&\forall a,b\,\exists c\,\forall u\,[\Memc(u,c)\leftrightarrow(\Eqc(u,a)\lor \Eqc(u,b))],\\
 \text{Union:}\quad&\forall a\,\exists b\,\forall u\,[\Memc(u,b)\leftrightarrow\exists v(\Memc(v,a)\land \Memc(u,v))],\\
 \text{Power Set:}\quad&\forall a\,\exists b\,\forall u\,[\Memc(u,b)\leftrightarrow\forall v(\Memc(v,u)\to \Memc(v,a))].
\end{align*}
Separation for a set-language formula $\varphi$ becomes
$\forall a,\bar p\,\exists b\,\forall u\,[\Memc(u,b)\leftrightarrow(\Memc(u,a)\land\varphi^\I(u,\bar p))]$.
Replacement becomes the statement that the image of an interpreted set under any
translated definable functional relation is represented by a code. Infinity
asserts the existence of a code for an inductive set; Foundation, Empty Set, and
Choice translate in the same fixed syntactic way. All quantifiers ultimately range
over surreal individuals.

Group B supplies the set existence assertions. Group C ensures that the numerical
carrier and its operations agree with the number system of that universe. It
rules out spurious extra copies of numbers or numerical structure that disappears
upon decoding.

An intrinsic theory based on short arithmetic, generation, and birthday-bounding
principles could be more illuminating and easier to use. The theory here instead
settles the precise \emph{existence} question with transparent logical
guarantees. Reducing these axiom groups is a further problem, not a premise of the
theorem.

\subsection{Provability and independence transfer}

\begin{corollary}\label{hset:sf:cor:provability}
For every set-theoretic sentence $\sigma$ and numerical sentence $\varphi$,
\[
 \ZFC\vdash\sigma\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \Tth_{\Dd}\vdash\sigma^{\I_{\Dd}},
 \qquad
 \Tth_{\Dd}\vdash\varphi\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ZFC\vdash\varphi^{\J_{\Dd}}.
\]
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The forward implications are interpretation of proofs. Translate back for the
reverse implications and use the corresponding definable isomorphism to identify
the double translation with the original sentence.
\end{proof}

Thus questions about CH, large cardinals, or other set-theoretic assertions have
numerical translations with exactly the same provability status. This is
representational equivalence; it does not decide any statement independent of
$\ZFC$. Consistency of any one of the three theories is equivalent to consistency
of the others.

Every additional set-theoretic axiom $\sigma$, such as a large-cardinal assertion,
can be added as $\sigma^\I$. The same round trips then give
\[
 \ZFC+\sigma\quad\text{bi-interpretable with}\quad\SArt+\sigma^\I.
\]
This is a consequence of the established interpretation, not evidence for $\sigma$
or a new consistency proof. (Source~14; the same holds for $\Tth_{\Dd}$, and
\cref{hset:sf:lem:persist} states the general fact.)

\paragraph{Finite axiomatizability.} Assuming consistency, these numerical
theories are incomplete and undecidable. They are not finitely axiomatizable
either. Finite axiomatizability is preserved by a bi-interpretation between finite
languages: choose a finite source fragment proving the translated finite axiom
list and the finite assertion that the double interpretation is an isomorphism,
then translate proofs back to show that this fragment proves the entire source
theory. But reflection proves consistency of each finite fragment of $\ZFC$; a
finite axiomatization of all of $\ZFC$ would therefore contradict the second
incompleteness theorem. (Source~13; source 14's \cref{hset:sf:lem:finite} proves
the preservation statement in full.)

\subsection{Why this is more than an external relabeling}

An external bijection between two domains permits transport of arbitrary
relations, but supplies no native way to read them. Our formulas use the actual
numerical arithmetic, order, and birthday relation. The graph relation is defined
by signs at specified numerical positions. The second comparison matches the full
original sign function, not an externally chosen enumeration.

Nevertheless, the axiom system is deliberately built from the interpretation. It
is reasonable to want a shorter arithmetic presentation. The distinction is
between a proved formal existence theorem and a desirable simplification of its
axioms. In particular, nothing here proves that an arbitrary ordered ring with an
unrelated well-founded relation must recover set theory.

\begin{lemma}[Persistence under translated extensions; added in this merge]
\label{hset:sf:lem:persist}
Let $S$ and $T$ be bi-interpretable by $\I$ (interpreting $S$ in $T$) and $\J$
(interpreting $T$ in $S$), with the definable isomorphisms provable in $S$ and $T$
respectively. For every set $\Sigma$ of sentences of the language of $S$, the
theories $S+\Sigma$ and $T+\{\sigma^\I:\sigma\in\Sigma\}$ are bi-interpretable by
the same $\I$, $\J$ and isomorphisms. If $T$ has a computably enumerable
axiom set, $\Sigma$ is computably enumerable, and the translation
$\sigma\mapsto\sigma^\I$ is computable, then the second theory is computably
axiomatizable.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
$\I$ interprets $S+\Sigma$ in $T+\Sigma^\I$ by construction. For $\J$ it remains to
see that $S+\Sigma$ proves $(\sigma^\I)^\J$ for $\sigma\in\Sigma$: this sentence
says that $\sigma$ holds in $\I(\J(M))$, which $S$ proves equivalent to $\sigma$ by
the definable isomorphism $M\cong\I(\J(M))$. The isomorphisms are unchanged, and
their properties remain provable in the larger theories. Under the effective
hypotheses, dovetail an enumeration of the axioms of $T$ with the computable
translations of an enumeration of $\Sigma$. This enumerates an axiom set of
$T+\Sigma^\I$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Effective hypotheses in the persistence lemma]
\label{hset:sf:rem:persistence-effectivity}
The merge of 4 October 2026 ended the lemma with ``if $\Sigma$ is recursive,
so is the second theory.'' Without an effective axiomatization of the
starting theory this is false: take $S=T=\operatorname{Th}(\mathbb N)$,
identity interpretations, and $\Sigma=\varnothing$. The target remains true
arithmetic, which is not computably axiomatizable: otherwise it would be a
consistent, complete, computably axiomatized extension of arithmetic,
contrary to incompleteness. The corrected clause also states effectivity
of translation; fixed-formula interpretations between the finite languages
used here satisfy that condition. The bi-interpretation assertion itself
needs neither of these computability assumptions.
\end{remark}

This is classical and is used below for the class variants
(\cref{hset:sf:cor:variants}); source 14 gives the same argument in the proof of
\cref{hset:sf:thm:classlift}.

\section{Consequences for definability and the choice of language}
\label{hset:sf:sec:language}

\subsection{Every ambient set definition has a native numerical translation}

\begin{corollary}[Definability transfer]\label{hset:sf:cor:definability}
Let $\Dd=\No$ or $\Oz$. For each first-order set-theoretic formula
$\psi(s_1,\ldots,s_n)$, there is a parameter-free formula
$\psi_{\Dd}(x_1,\ldots,x_n)$ in $\Lb$ such that $\ZFC$ proves
\[
 \Dd(x_1)\land\cdots\land \Dd(x_n)\ \longrightarrow\
 \bigl(\psi(x_1,\ldots,x_n)\leftrightarrow\psi_{\Dd}(x_1,\ldots,x_n)\bigr).
\]
Here the arguments on the set-theoretic side are the canonical sign-function sets
representing the numbers.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Use the formula
\[
 \exists c_1\cdots\exists c_n\Bigl(\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\Cmp_{\Dd}(x_i,c_i)
 \ \land\ \psi^{\I_{\Dd}}(c_1,\ldots,c_n)\Bigr).
\]
The collapse isomorphism and \cref{hset:sf:thm:roundtrip} give exactly the claimed
equivalence.
\end{proof}

Thus the native birthday language defines much more than a convenient membership
coding. It can express every set-theoretically definable relation on its numerical
domain. For example, in $\No$ it defines the usual monomial map and surreal
exponential by translating their fixed set-theoretic definitions. In $\Oz$ it
defines their graphs where both arguments lie in $\Oz$, and it defines ordinary
ordinal addition and multiplication as relations on the native ordinals.
Additional numerical parameters are carried through the same comparison.

This is a separate translation for each formula. It is not a single formula
evaluating the truth of every formula code. Consequently it supplies no truth
predicate and does not evade Tarski's theorem. Class-quantified formulas of a
stronger class theory are also outside the assertion unless the class lift is
used.

\begin{proposition}[Canonical bi-interpretations of reducts and expansions; added in this merge]
\label{hset:sf:prop:reducts}
Let $\mathcal A=(\Dd;R_1,\ldots,R_k)$, with $\Dd=\No$ or $\Oz$, be a structure each
of whose finitely many primitives $R_i$ is given by a fixed parameter-free
set-theoretic formula on the canonical sign functions, and let $\bar p$ be a finite
tuple from $\Dd$. The following are equivalent, provably in $\ZFC$ formula by
formula:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item\label{hset:sf:item:canon} there are an interpretation $\I'$ of set theory in
$\mathcal A$ and a relation $F(x,c)$, both first-order in $\mathcal A$ with
parameters $\bar p$, and a set-theoretically definable isomorphism
$G:\I'(\mathcal A)\cong(V,\in)$, such that $F(x,c)\iff G(c)=x$ (each number goes
to the codes of its own sign sequence);
\item\label{hset:sf:item:def} the symbols $0,1,+,-,\cdot,<,\precb$ are definable in
$\mathcal A$ with parameters $\bar p$.
\end{enumerate}
If they hold, $\mathcal A$ is bi-interpretable with $(V,\in)$, with the canonical
set-to-number interpretation, and definitionally equivalent, over $\bar p$, to
$(\Dd,\Lb)$. Moreover \ref{hset:sf:item:canon} then holds for every interpretation
$\I'$ that is $\mathcal A$-definable over $\bar p$ and has a set-theoretically
definable isomorphism onto $(V,\in)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
\ref{hset:sf:item:canon}$\Rightarrow$\ref{hset:sf:item:def}. Let $\psi(\bar s)$ be
a set-theoretic formula defining one of the symbols on canonical sign functions.
Then $\psi(\bar x)$ holds iff
$\exists\bar c\,\bigl(\bigwedge_iF(x_i,c_i)\land\psi^{\I'}(\bar c)\bigr)$, because
$\psi^{\I'}(\bar c)$ holds iff $\psi(G\bar c)$ does, and $G c_i=x_i$. This is an
$\mathcal A$-formula over $\bar p$.

\ref{hset:sf:item:def}$\Rightarrow$\ref{hset:sf:item:canon}. Substitute the
definitions of the symbols of $\Lb$ into $\Valid$, $\Eqc$, $\Memc$ and
$\Cmp_{\Dd}$; \cref{hset:sf:thm:graph,hset:sf:thm:roundtrip} give
\ref{hset:sf:item:canon} with $\I'=\I_{\Dd}$ and $F=\Cmp_{\Dd}$. Each $R_i$ is
$\Lb$-definable by \cref{hset:sf:cor:definability}, so the two structures are
definitionally equivalent over $\bar p$; the comparison $\Cmp_{\Dd}$ preserves every
$R_i$, being the identity on numbers after collapse, so the canonical
set-to-number interpretation of $\mathcal A$'s language, composed with $\I_{\Dd}$,
gives a bi-interpretation. For another $\I'$ with
$G':\I'(\mathcal A)\cong V$, the relation ``the $\I_{\Dd}$-code $c$ and the
$\I'$-code $d$ represent the same set'' is $\mathcal A$-definable over $\bar p$:
inside the set universe $\I_{\Dd}(\mathcal A)$ the structure $\mathcal A$ is
reconstructed as $\J_{\Dd}$ of it, transported along $\Cmp_{\Dd}$, and $\ZFC$
proves that $V\cong\I'(\mathcal A)$ by the definable $G'$; composing gives $F$ for
$\I'$.
\end{proof}

Three cases matter here. (1) For a reduct of $\Lb$ containing $\precb$, a
canonical bi-interpretation exists exactly when the reduct defines the missing ring
symbols; source 06's \cref{hset:cor:reduct} is a positive instance at uncountable
cardinal cutoffs, for $(\No_{<\kappa};<,\bd,G_\varpi)$. (2) For dsn's
$\mathfrak S_\Omega=(\No;+,\cdot,<,\Oz,\Omega)$ (\cref{hset:sf:rem:dsn}), which
names the ring operations, the specific form of
\lbl{dsn:op:q:biinterpretation} is equivalent to the definability of $\precb$ in
$\mathfrak S_\Omega$; whether $\precb$ is so definable is not decided here. (3) For
the pure ordered ring of $\Oz$, \ref{hset:sf:item:def} fails even with parameters
(\cref{hset:sf:cor:nondefbirthday}); \cref{hset:sf:prop:purenogo} below excludes more,
namely any comparison, canonical or not. The proposition concerns comparisons
sending each number to codes of itself; it says nothing about one-way
interpretations, the subject of the ``Minimal signature'' question of
\cref{hset:sec:questions}.

\subsection{Recovering fractions and their birthdays}

The classical fraction-field fact provides a useful comparison with the direct
construction.

\begin{proposition}[Fractions; sources 13, 14]\label{hset:sf:prop:fractions}
Every surreal is a fraction of two omnific integers, with positive denominator.
Thus the natural map $\Frac(\Oz)\to\No$ is an ordered-field isomorphism.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $x=\sum_i r_i\omega^{a_i}$, choose a positive ordinal $\beta$ larger than every
$-a_i$. The support is a set, so such an ordinal exists. Put $b=\omega^\beta$. Then
$b\in\Oz$, $b>0$, and
\[
 bx=\sum_i r_i\omega^{\beta+a_i}\in\Oz,
\]
since every exponent is positive. Hence $x=(bx)/b$. The case $x=0$ is immediate.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Second proof (source 14)]
For $x\ne0$, write its normal form as $x=\sum_{\gamma\in S}r_\gamma\omega^\gamma$.
The support $S$ is a set. There is a positive ordinal surreal $\delta$ larger than
every $-\gamma$ for $\gamma\in S$. For example, first bound the birthdays of those
numbers, then choose a larger ordinal; a sufficiently long all-plus sequence
exceeds every sequence in that birthday bound. Let $q=\omega^\delta$ and $p=qx$. All
exponents $\delta+\gamma$ of $p$ are positive. Hence $p,q\in\Oz$, and $x=p/q$. The
case $x=0$ uses $0/1$. Injectivity of the natural fraction map follows from the
integral-domain property of $\Oz$.
\end{proof}

This fact and stronger simultaneous denominator-clearing results already appear in
the collection, including Lean formalizations (\lbl{odg:thm:fractions};
\cref{hset:sf:sub:machine}). It must not be confused with recovery of the birthday
relation. Taking a fraction field from the \emph{pure} ring adds no birthday
information. (Source~13.) Source 14 adds: this standard fact is also noted by
Je\v{r}\'abek \cite{JerabekOz}; the proof emphasizes its \emph{whole-support} bound.
Shifting merely the leading exponent would not suffice when the support extends
arbitrarily far down within a set-sized range. The assertion also does not follow
for an arbitrary integer part of a real-closed field: the fraction field of $\Z$
is $\Q$, not $\R$. (The Hilbert's-tenth intake adds that the shift requires an
ordinal bound for every exponent of a set-sized support and is not a bounded
integer operation.)

One could instead enrich $\Oz$ with the four-variable predicate
\[
 \FB(a,b,c,d)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad b,d>0\ \land\ \bd(a/b)<\bd(c/d).
\]
It is invariant under the usual fraction equivalence $ad=bc$, with the appropriate
pairs of numerators and denominators. The fraction interpretation would then
directly recover the birthday-equipped surreal field.

The native result is stronger in its choice of primitives.
\Cref{hset:sf:cor:definability} gives:

\begin{corollary}\label{hset:sf:cor:fractionbirthday}
The relation $\FB$ is already parameter-free definable in
$(\Oz,0,1,+,-,\cdot,<,\precb)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Canonical surreal division and comparison of the domains of its sign functions
have fixed set-theoretic definitions. Apply \cref{hset:sf:cor:definability} to
their composite on four omnific arguments.
\end{proof}

The resulting formula can be large. Finding a short direct arithmetic description
remains a useful simplification problem (\cref{hset:sf:q:shortFB}); the existence
of a definition is settled here. (Source~13.)

\begin{proposition}[Quotient birthday from unary birthday; added in this merge]
\label{hset:sf:prop:unary}
Source 14's quotient birthday $h(a,b)$, with $h(a,0)=0$, is parameter-free
definable in $(\Oz;0,1,+,-,\cdot,<,\bd\restr\Oz)$. Consequently the languages
$\Lz$ and $\Lb$ are definitionally equivalent on $\Oz$, and \cref{hset:sf:q:unary}
has a positive answer.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
On $\Oz$, $\bd\restr\Oz$ and $\precb\restr\Oz$ are interdefinable
(\cref{hset:sf:lem:ord}), so \cref{hset:sf:cor:fractionbirthday} applies. For an
ordinal $\gamma$, $\gamma/1=\gamma$ and $\bd(\gamma)=\gamma$. Hence for $b>0$,
\[
 h(a,b)=\gamma\iff\Ord(\gamma)\land\neg\FB(a,b,\gamma,1)\land\neg\FB(\gamma,1,a,b),
\]
since the two negated conjuncts say $\bd(a/b)=\gamma$; for $b<0$ put
$h(a,b)=h(-a,-b)$, because $a/b=(-a)/(-b)$; and $h(a,0)=0$. Conversely
$x\precb y\iff h(x,1)<h(y,1)$ defines $\precb$ on $\Oz$ from $h$.
\end{proof}

The definition inherits the length of \cref{hset:sf:cor:fractionbirthday} and its
dependence on the imported omnific sign facts (through
\cref{hset:sf:thm:roundtrip} for $\Oz$). Source 14's warning that neither the
fraction construction alone nor knowledge of the two input birthdays settles the
question stays correct: the definition goes through the whole interpretation.

\subsection{A second omnific route: quotient birthday (source 14)}
\label{hset:sf:sub:qb}

Source 14 reaches the omnific integers through fractions instead of native
blocks. An omnific integer is a surreal with normal form
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:omnific}
 z=\sum_{\gamma>0}r_\gamma\omega^\gamma+n,\qquad n\in\Z,
\end{equation}
where the nonzero support is a set and is reverse well-ordered, and the
coefficients $r_\gamma$ are real. Zero support and negative coefficients are
allowed. This is the usual omnific ring $\Oz$.

Define a binary operation on $\Oz$ by
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:h}
 h(a,b)=\begin{cases}\bd(a/b),&b\ne0,\\0,&b=0.\end{cases}
\end{equation}
The output really is an omnific integer: birthdays are ordinal surreals, and
ordinal surreals are omnific integers. Put
\[
 \mathcal O_{\mathrm{qb}}=(\Oz;0,1,+,-,\cdot,<,h).
\]
The subscript records the extra quotient-birthday information. We do \emph{not}
assert that $h$ is definable in the pure ordered ring. (The letter $\mathcal O$ is
not the collection's valuation ring.)

Use pairs $(a,b)$ with $b>0$ as fraction codes, and define
\begin{align*}
 (a,b)\sim_F(c,d)&\iff ad=cb,\\
 (a,b)<_F(c,d)&\iff ad<cb,\\
 (a,b)+_F(c,d)&=(ad+cb,bd),\\
 (a,b)\cdot_F(c,d)&=(ac,bd),\\
 \bd_F([(a,b)])&=[(h(a,b),1)].
\end{align*}
For the actual structure, equal fractions have equal quotient birthdays, so the
last operation is well-defined. These fixed formulas interpret $\B$ in
$\mathcal O_{\mathrm{qb}}$.

For the reverse interpretation, $\Oz$ is definable in $\B$. One method is to
translate the fixed set-theoretic normal-form definition \eqref{hset:sf:eq:omnific}
through the already established set interpretation and $\rho$. This avoids any
assumption that an ordered-field formula alone recognizes the normal form. The
operation $h$ is then definable using field division and $\bd$. Call the resulting
$\Ls$-formula $\operatorname{Omnific}(x)$.

\begin{theorem}[Omnific quotient-birthday bi-interpretation; source 14]\label{hset:sf:thm:omnificstruct}
The structures $\mathcal O_{\mathrm{qb}}$, $\B$, and $(V,\in)$ are parameter-free
bi-interpretable by the constructions above.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\Cref{hset:sf:prop:fractions} identifies the interpreted fraction field with all
of $\No$, including birthday by definition of $h$. Starting with $\B$, the
number-to-fraction round trip is defined by the relation $a=bx$, with $a,b\in\Oz$
and $b>0$. It is single-valued modulo fraction equivalence and onto, and respects
every primitive.

Starting with $\mathcal O_{\mathrm{qb}}$, the ring-to-fractions map
$z\mapsto[(z,1)]$ identifies exactly the original omnific integers with the
omnific predicate in the recovered field. The formula for $h$ is preserved because
it uses the same quotient and birthday. This gives both definable round trips.
Composition with \cref{hset:sf:prop:recovery} gives the set-theoretic assertion.
\end{proof}

With \cref{hset:sf:prop:unary}, $\mathcal O_{\mathrm{qb}}$ is a definitional
expansion of $(\Oz,\Lb)$, so this theorem and the omnific case of
\cref{hset:sf:cor:structure} are two routes to one structural statement: source
13's by native sign blocks, without fractions, source 14's by fractions, with $h$.
Source 14's route does not use the omnific sign criterion; it uses the normal-form
definition \eqref{hset:sf:eq:omnific} and \cref{hset:sf:prop:fractions}.

\paragraph{An axiom system on the omnific sort (source 14).} Source 14 presents
this as ``an explicit alternative to simply applying \cref{hset:sf:lem:rt}
again''. Define $\OAqb$ in $\Lz$ by these axiom groups:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{O\arabic*.}]
\item The ordered-integral-domain axioms, with $0<1$.
\item $h(a,0)=0$, and fraction invariance:
\[
 bd\ne0\land ad=cb\quad\longrightarrow\quad h(a,b)=h(c,d).
\]
\item Every axiom of $\SArt$, translated to the positive-denominator fraction
interpretation above.
\item In that interpreted surreal structure, for every fraction $u$,
\[
 \operatorname{Omnific}(u)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\exists z\ (u=[(z,1)]).
\]
Here $\operatorname{Omnific}$ is the fixed formula just constructed.
\end{enumerate}
These are first-order axiom groups, and O3 is an effective scheme. The equivalence
in O4 is crucial: merely requiring a real-closed fraction field would allow many
other subrings and would not ensure recovery of the original omnific sort.

\begin{theorem}[An effective omnific foundational theory; source 14]\label{hset:sf:thm:omnifictheory}
$\OAqb$ is recursively axiomatized and parameter-free bi-interpretable with
$\SArt$, hence with $\ZFC$. Its canonical interpretation has only omnific-integer
individuals and has the intended $h$ operation.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof (source 14)]
O1 and O2 make the fraction construction and its birthday operation well-defined.
O3 makes it a model of $\SArt$. O4 says that the reverse omnific interpretation
recovers exactly the original domain. The map $z\mapsto[(z,1)]$ preserves the
ordered ring operations.

For $b\ne0$, fraction invariance identifies any representation of
$[(a,1)]/[(b,1)]$ with $(a,b)$. Thus its interpreted birthday is $[(h(a,b),1)]$; at
zero denominator O2 gives the stated convention. So the map preserves $h$ as well.
The converse direction follows inside $\SArt$ by transfer of
\cref{hset:sf:prop:fractions,hset:sf:thm:omnificstruct} through its numerical round
trip. All formulas are fixed and all axiom translations effective.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[O1--O4 is an instance of the recipe; added in this merge]
\label{hset:sf:rem:oaqb}
O1--O4 is \cref{hset:sf:thm:axiomprinciple} applied with $S=\SArt$, the
fraction interpretation as $I$, and the comparison $z\mapsto[(z,1)]$ as $C$: O1 and
O2 are the admissibility sentences, O3 the translated axioms, and O4 the one
clause of $\operatorname{Iso}(C)$ (surjectivity) that is not provable from O1--O3;
injectivity and preservation of $+$, $\cdot$, $<$ and $h$ follow from O1--O2 as the
proof shows. So it is not an alternative to the recipe but a presentation of it in
which most isomorphism clauses are derived; its transparency is that of O4. By
\cref{hset:sf:prop:unary} the same recipe in $\Lb$ is $\Tth_{\Oz}$ up to a
definitional translation of $h$.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[An example and a warning; source 14, re-scoped]\label{hset:sf:rem:warning}
For ordinary integers, $h(1,2)=2$, since $1/2$ has sign sequence $+-$. Hence
quotient birthday is not the birthday of the numerator or denominator alone.
Giving an axiom system with $h$ does not settle whether the weaker language
consisting of ordered-ring arithmetic and the unary restricted birthday
$z\mapsto\bd(z)$ already suffices. That precise reduction remains a research
question here. (Source~14.) [Merge: it is settled positively by source 13's
\cref{hset:sf:cor:fractionbirthday}, through \cref{hset:sf:prop:unary}; see
\cref{hset:sf:q:unary}.]
\end{remark}

\subsection{Why the pure ordered surreal field is insufficient}

\begin{proposition}[Classical decidability obstruction; sources 13, 14]\label{hset:sf:prop:rcf}
The pure ordered field $(\No,0,1,+,-,\cdot,<)$ does not first-order interpret
Robinson arithmetic $Q$, even with a fixed finite tuple of parameters. In
particular it cannot interpret $\ZFC$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Its elementary theory is the complete decidable theory $\RCF$. Suppose that fixed
formulas, with parameters $p$, interpret $Q$. Admissibility and the finitely many
axioms of $Q$ give a single ordered-field formula $\theta(p)$ true in this field.

In the language with constant symbols $c$, the consistent theory
$\RCF+\theta(c)$ is decidable: deciding whether it proves $\varphi(c)$ amounts to
deciding
\[
 \RCF\vdash\forall c\bigl(\theta(c)\to\varphi(c)\bigr).
\]
The proposed interpretation would therefore give a decidable consistent extension
of $Q$, contradicting its essential undecidability. Since $\ZFC$ interprets $Q$,
it is excluded as well.
\end{proof}

This is an instance of the general interpretation/undecidability principle
discussed by Friedman \cite{FriedmanTarski}. The extra birthday relation is
carrying logically substantial structure. (Source~13.)

\begin{proof}[Second proof, parameter-free (source 14)]
The surreal field is real closed, and the complete theory of real-closed ordered
fields is decidable by quantifier elimination. If it interpreted Robinson
arithmetic, take the inverse image of its complete decidable theory under the
effective interpretation translation. This would be a consistent decidable complete
extension of Robinson arithmetic, contradicting essential undecidability. Since
$\ZFC$ interprets that arithmetic, it cannot be interpreted either.

If birthday were ordered-field definable, substituting its definition in our
set-code formulas would supply precisely the forbidden interpretation.
Equivalently, $\bd(x)=x$ would define the ordinals, whereas one-variable sets in a
real-closed field are finite unions of points and intervals. The ordinal subclass
has neither form.
\end{proof}

Source 14 states it as: the ordered-field theory of the surreal numbers does not
interpret $\ZFC$ or Robinson arithmetic; in particular, no ordered-field formula
defines a birthday expansion sufficient for the constructions above. The standard
real-closed-field background is also highlighted in the CHY announcement. The
argument is \emph{not} a no-go theorem for the pure omnific ring: it is not a
field, and it already has arithmetic strength. Nor does it show that every
enrichment weaker than birthday must fail. Those are separate model-theoretic
questions. (Source~14.) This report's \cref{hset:prop:nondef} (sources 05, 07) proves
the nondefinability of birthday, also in every o-minimal expansion of the field, at
epsilon-number cutoffs and formula by formula on $\No$.

\begin{remark}[The pure omnific ring has arithmetic strength]\label{hset:sf:rem:pureoz}
Source 14's last remark is confirmed in the collection: the pure ring $\Oz$
parameter-free defines $\Z$ and $\N$ with their arithmetic
(\lbl{odg:def:cor:arithmetic}), so it interprets $Q$ and its theory is
undecidable; it interprets the field $\No$ with $\R$ and the order
(\lbl{odg:def:thm:realrecovery}), the order of $\Oz$ is definable in its pure unital
ring language, and $(\Oz;+,-,\cdot,0,1)$ is bi-interpretable with $(\No,\Oz)$
(\lbl{dsn:op:prop:native-omnific}). None of these interprets set theory.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The pure omnific ring loses ordinal and birthday information}

No parallel decidability assertion about the pure omnific ring is assumed. A
different argument proves a narrower but strong obstruction. The repository
contains more extensive results about omnific-preserving surreal automorphisms;
the following proof isolates a consequence relevant here
\cite{ProveItSF13,KKS}.

\begin{proposition}[Automorphisms fixing prescribed parameters]\label{hset:sf:prop:automorphism}
For every set $P\subseteq\No$, there is a nonidentity parameter-definable
ordered-field automorphism $\Phi$ of $\No$ which fixes $P$, preserves $\Oz$, and
sends an ordinal to a nonordinal.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Collect the exponents occurring in the Conway normal forms of members of $P$, and
then collect the supports of the normal forms of those exponents. These are sets.
Choose a positive ordinal $\delta$ greater than every member of the second
collection.

For any surreal $a$, let $\operatorname{co}_\delta(a)\in\R$ be the coefficient of
$\omega^\delta$ in its normal form, or zero when that monomial is absent. Define
\[
 \vartheta(a)=a+\tfrac12\operatorname{co}_\delta(a).
\]
Coefficient extraction is additive. Since $\delta>0$, adding a real does not
change $\operatorname{co}_\delta(a)$, so
$\vartheta^{-1}(a)=a-\tfrac12\operatorname{co}_\delta(a)$. This is an additive
automorphism.

It preserves order. When $\operatorname{co}_\delta(a)\ne0$, the leading monomial
of $a$ is at least $\omega^\delta$, hence is infinite; changing only a real term
does not change its sign. When the coefficient is zero, $a$ is fixed. Apply this to
differences to obtain strict order preservation.

Lift $\vartheta$ to normal forms:
\[
 \Phi\Bigl(\sum_i r_i\omega^{a_i}\Bigr)=\sum_i r_i\omega^{\vartheta(a_i)}.
\]
An additive order automorphism of the exponent group preserves reverse
well-ordered supports and the sums of exponents appearing in multiplication. The
displayed map is therefore an ordered-field automorphism, with inverse given by the
lift of $\vartheta^{-1}$. This standard Hahn-series lifting argument is described
in \cite{KKS}.

Every exponent occurring in $P$ has zero $\omega^\delta$-coefficient by the choice
of $\delta$. Consequently $\Phi$ fixes $P$ pointwise. The map $\vartheta$ fixes $0$
and preserves positive exponents; $\Phi$ fixes real coefficients. It therefore
preserves the normal-form criterion for $\Oz$.

Finally, the ordinal $o_\delta=\omega^{\omega^\delta}$ is sent to
\[
 \Phi(o_\delta)=\omega^{\,\omega^\delta+1/2}.
\]
The exponent lies strictly between the ordinal $\omega^\delta$ and its successor,
so is not ordinal. An ordinal's Conway normal form is its Cantor normal form and
has only ordinal exponents. Uniqueness of normal form therefore shows that
$\Phi(o_\delta)$ is not ordinal.
\end{proof}

The conclusion follows from \lbl{opa:thm:parameters} of
\repo{surreal/omnific-preserving-automorphisms}, whose automorphism also fixes
$\R\cup\{\omega\}$, is strong and real-linear, and sends the ordinal
$\omega^{\omega^\beta}$ to a nonmonomial (\cref{hset:sf:rem:dsn}); the shear is a
short self-contained route, with no priority claim.

\begin{corollary}\label{hset:sf:cor:nondefbirthday}
The embedded ordinals and birthday precedence are not definable in the pure
ordered omnific ring, even with finitely many numerical parameters. The analogous
nondefinability holds in the pure ordered surreal field.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Apply the proposition with the parameter tuple included in $P$. Any definable
predicate is preserved by every automorphism fixing its parameters. The ordinal
class is not preserved. If birthday precedence were definable,
\eqref{hset:sf:eq:ord} would define the ordinals, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[No bi-interpretation of the pure omnific ring with $V$ on the canonical numbers; source 13, made a proposition in this merge]
\label{hset:sf:prop:purenogo}
There are no interpretation $\I'$ of set theory in the pure ordered ring
$\mathcal R=(\Oz;0,1,+,-,\cdot,<)$ and relation $F$, both first-order in
$\mathcal R$ with a finite tuple $\bar p$ of parameters, together with a
set-theoretically definable (with parameters) isomorphism
$G:(V,\in)\cong\I'(\mathcal R)$, such that $F$ is an isomorphism of $\mathcal R$
onto the canonical ordered ring $\J(\I'(\mathcal R))$ of the interpreted universe.
In other words, the pure omnific ring is not bi-interpretable, even with
parameters, with the actual well-founded universe when the set-to-number direction
is the canonical construction. The same holds for the pure ordered field $\No$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $\I'$, $F$, $\bar p$ and $G$ existed. Apply
\cref{hset:sf:prop:automorphism} with $P$ the set of entries of $\bar p$; it gives a nonidentity ordered-ring
automorphism $\Phi$ of $\mathcal R$ fixing $\bar p$, definable in set theory with a
parameter.

Because the formulas of $\I'$ use only the parameters $\bar p$, the map
$\Phi_{\I'}:[\bar c]\mapsto[\Phi\bar c]$ is a well-defined automorphism of
$\I'(\mathcal R)$, definable in set theory with parameters. Then
$\pi=G^{-1}\circ\Phi_{\I'}\circ G$ is a definable automorphism of $(V,\in)$. By
$\in$-induction $\pi$ is the identity: if $\pi(y)=y$ for every $y\in x$, then
$z\in\pi(x)\iff\pi^{-1}(z)\in x$, and $\pi^{-1}(z)\in x$ gives
$z=\pi(\pi^{-1}(z))=\pi^{-1}(z)\in x$, while $z\in x$ gives $z=\pi(z)\in\pi(x)$. So
$\Phi_{\I'}$ is the identity of $\I'(\mathcal R)$, and therefore so is the map it
induces on $\J(\I'(\mathcal R))$, which is defined inside $\I'(\mathcal R)$ by fixed
set-theoretic formulas.

The graph of $F$ is defined by a ring formula over $\bar p$, so it is preserved by
$\Phi$ acting on numbers and $\Phi_{\I'}$ acting on codes:
$F(\Phi x)=\Phi_{\I'}(F(x))=F(x)$ for every $x$. Since $F$ is injective, $\Phi$ is
the identity, a contradiction. The field $\No$ is treated in the same way.
\end{proof}

Source 13 gave this argument as a paragraph after
\cref{hset:sf:cor:nondefbirthday}: ``The same construction rules out a
bi-interpretation between the pure omnific ring and the actual well-founded set
universe whose set-to-number direction is the canonical construction. Indeed the
displayed automorphism is set-theoretically definable with a parameter. A proposed
interpretation would transport it to a definable automorphism of $(V,\in)$.
Foundation and induction on rank force every such automorphism to be the identity.
Definability of the second comparison then forces the original numerical
automorphism to be the identity as well, a contradiction. Finite interpretation
parameters cause no problem because they can be included in $P$.'' The argument is
complete; the proof above writes out its three steps. It uses only that
$\mathcal R$ has, over every finite tuple, a set-definable automorphism that is not
the identity; it does not use \cref{hset:sf:cor:nondefbirthday}. For comparisons
that send each number to codes of itself, \cref{hset:sf:prop:reducts} with
\cref{hset:sf:cor:nondefbirthday} gives the same conclusion by definability.

This does \emph{not} prove that the pure omnific ring cannot interpret some model
of set theory. Nor does it exclude every noncanonical theory-level interpretation
one could formulate. The negative assertion is deliberately about definability and
the canonical inverse direction. (Source~13.) The remaining question is
\cref{hset:sf:q:pureoz}.

By contrast, the actual birthday-equipped structures are rigid: an automorphism
preserves the definable ordinals, fixes this well-ordered class pointwise, and then
fixes every birthday and every sign by \eqref{hset:sf:eq:plus}. This last argument
concerns the actual well-founded universe; it does not assert that all nonstandard
models of $\Tth_{\Dd}$ have no automorphisms. (Source~13.)

\section{The obstruction at the class boundary}
\label{hset:sf:sec:classobstruction}

The preceding construction recovers $(V,\in)$. It does not recover an arbitrary
class part $\mathcal X$ of a model $(M,\mathcal X)\models\GBC$. This distinction
cannot be removed by saying that $\No$ itself is a proper class. (Source~13.)

\subsection{Conservativity is not interpretability}
\label{hset:sf:sub:reflection}

The obstruction below is stronger than failure of bi-interpretability. Its proof
uses ordinary reflection and incompleteness, not an assumption that proper classes
have an external cardinality. (Source~14.)

\begin{lemma}[Reflection produces models of finitely axiomatized targets; source 14]
\label{hset:sf:lem:reflect}
If a finitely axiomatized first-order theory $U$ has a uniform finite-dimensional
interpretation in $\ZFC$, then $\ZFC$ proves that $U$ has a set-sized model. The
statement also holds for finitely many sorts, definable quotient domains, and
interpretations with a provably nonempty domain of finitely many admissible set
parameters.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Fix the interpretation formulas. Let $\tau$ be the conjunction of the translations
of a finite axiom basis of $U$ and the finite interpretation-validity sentences. By
hypothesis $\ZFC\vdash\tau$. The L\'evy reflection theorem \cite{Levy}, applied to
this finite collection of formulas and its subformulas, proves the existence of a
rank segment $V_\alpha$ satisfying $\tau$.

The interpreted domain is now a definable subset of a finite power of the
\emph{set} $V_\alpha$. Separation forms it as a set, and the interpreted relations
are sets as well. Its equivalence classes form a set quotient. The congruence and
axiom clauses of $\tau$ show that the resulting structure is a model of the finite
theory $U$. Multiple sorts are handled one at a time.

With parameters, first take witnesses from the provably nonempty parameter domain
and reflect above a rank containing them. Equivalently include the assertion that
admissible parameters exist in the finite reflected conjunction. The same set
construction follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[No interpretation of NBG in $\ZFC$; sources 13, 14]\label{hset:sf:thm:noint}
Assume $\Con(\ZFC)$. Then the usual NBG theory, equivalently $\GBC$, is not
uniformly first-order interpretable in $\ZFC$, even allowing fixed finite tuple
domains, quotients, and admissible set parameters. Consequently no theory
bi-interpretable with $\ZFC$, including $\Tth_{\No}$ or $\Tth_{\Oz}$, interprets
$\GBC$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof (source 14)]
NBG has a finite first-order axiom basis. Its usual set-sort restriction interprets
every axiom of $\ZFC$; the corresponding syntactic proof translation is effective
and its correctness is formalizable in ordinary arithmetic. Finite axiomatizability
of GBC/NBG is the standard background fact recorded, for example, in
\cite[Corollary 3.11 and its proof]{WilliamsDiss}.

If $\ZFC$ interpreted NBG, \cref{hset:sf:lem:reflect} would make $\ZFC$ prove the
existence of a set model of NBG and therefore prove $\Con(\NBG)$, by soundness for
set-sized structures. The standard proof translation from $\ZFC$ into the set sort
of NBG gives, already formalizably in arithmetic,
\[
 \Con(\NBG)\longrightarrow\Con(\ZFC).
\]
Thus $\ZFC$ would prove its own consistency, contradicting G\"odel's second
incompleteness theorem \cite{Godel} under the assumed consistency. The last
sentence of the theorem follows because interpretations compose.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Second proof (source 13)]
Use a finite axiomatization of $\GBC$. If an interpretation existed, a finite
fragment $\Gamma$ of $\ZFC$ would prove its admissibility and the translations of
those finitely many axioms. Reflection in $\ZFC$ gives a set model of $\Gamma$.
Applying the interpretation produces a set model of $\GBC$, whose set part is a
model of $\ZFC$. Thus $\ZFC$ would prove $\Con(\ZFC)$, contrary to the second
incompleteness theorem. Interpretations compose, giving the consequence.
\end{proof}

Source 13 states the theorem as ``If $\ZFC$ is consistent, it does not interpret
$\GBC$'', with the consequence for $\Tth_{\No}$, $\Tth_{\Oz}$, and attributes the
argument to Hamkins and Yao \cite[Section 4]{HamkinsYao}. That attribution was
checked for this merge against arXiv version 2 (5 December 2022) of
\cite{HamkinsYao}: Section~4, ``Class theory'', states that GBC and ZFC, if
consistent, ``are not mutually interpretable'', because GBC is finitely
axiomatizable, an interpretation of it in ZFC would use only finitely many axioms
of ZFC, ZFC proves the consistency of its finite fragments, and so the
interpretation would violate the second incompleteness theorem. It is compatible
with the usual conservativity and equiconsistency results. Conservativity concerns
sentences in the set language; a class-theoretic interpretation would have to
supply the entire class domain. (Source~13.)

One can equivalently say that a set model of NBG has a set-sized set-sort reduct
satisfying all $\ZFC$ axioms. The syntactic version of the proof is useful because
it makes clear that the assertion about \emph{all} axiom instances is not an illicit
appeal to a truth predicate for $V$. The translation of formal proofs is a
finitary arithmetical operation. (Source~14.)

\begin{corollary}[The canonical numerical no-go theorem; source 14]\label{hset:sf:cor:numericalnoint}
Assuming $\Con(\ZFC)$, if a numerical theory $T$ is uniformly interpretable in
$\ZFC$, then $T$ does not interpret NBG. In particular, neither $\SArt$ nor
$\OAqb$ is bi-interpretable with NBG.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
An interpretation of NBG in $T$ could be composed with the interpretation of $T$ in
$\ZFC$, contradicting \cref{hset:sf:thm:noint}.
\end{proof}

This includes any proposed numerical axiom system for which a fixed ZFC definition
of the native numerical structure is proved to satisfy each axiom. If one removes
that provability hypothesis, the corollary no longer applies automatically. A
complete theory or an expansion by arbitrary predicates may carry additional
information which $\ZFC$ cannot verify. One must analyze such a proposal
separately rather than cite this corollary without its hypothesis. (Source~14.)

\paragraph{A second diagnostic: finite axiomatizability (source 14).}

\begin{lemma}[Finite axiomatizability transfers; source 14]\label{hset:sf:lem:finite}
For theories in finite languages, ordinary bi-interpretability preserves finite
axiomatizability.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $T$ and $U$ are bi-interpretable and $U$ has a finite basis. Inside $T$,
take the finitely many statements which ensure that its interpretation of $U$ is
valid and satisfies that basis, and that the specified round-trip relation is an
isomorphism. A finite subset $T_0\subseteq T$ proves all these statements.

If $M\models T_0$, then the interpreted model $\I(M)$ satisfies $U$. Interpreting
back gives $\J(\I(M))\models T$. The round-trip isomorphism, already secured by
$T_0$, makes $M\models T$. Hence $T_0$ axiomatizes $T$. The reverse implication is
symmetric.
\end{proof}

Assuming consistency, $\ZFC$ is not finitely axiomatizable: a hypothetical finite
basis would reflect to a set model, making $\ZFC$ prove its own consistency.
Therefore $\SArt$ and $\OAqb$ (and $\Tth_{\No}$, $\Tth_{\Oz}$) are not finitely
axiomatizable either. NBG is finitely axiomatizable. This gives a second proof that
the displayed numerical theories are not bi-interpretable with NBG. Unlike
\cref{hset:sf:thm:noint}, this diagnostic alone does not rule out a one-way
interpretation.

The reflection reasoning is classical. Its application here should not be
misrepresented as a new inconsistency result or as a newly discovered separation of
ZFC and NBG. It explains exactly why an otherwise successful surreal encoding
cannot be promoted to the requested class theory merely by changing its name.
(Source~14.)

\paragraph{Why conservativity does not contradict the obstruction (source 14).}
\label{hset:sf:sub:conservativity}
GBC/NBG is conservative over $\ZFC$ for set-language sentences. This means that
adding class quantification does not produce new theorems \emph{in that restricted
language}. It does not provide a uniform interpretation of every class object by
set objects, and does not require the two theories to have matching finite
axiomatizability. For a fixed set model, a suitable collection of definable classes
may supply a class-theoretic expansion in appropriate circumstances. That external
model construction is not the same as a first-order internal interpretation using a
uniform truth-evaluation formula. In particular, Global Choice and the exact
allowed class collection must not be silently inserted. The reflection proof
pinpoints the difference without appealing to an informal ``there are more
classes'' slogan. The foundations report states conservativity in the same way
(\lbl{found:rem:conservativity}).

\subsection{A stronger obstruction when the individuals are original sets}

The reflection argument concerns a target theory already bi-interpretable with
$\ZFC$. The next result allows arbitrary numerical relations, including ones
depending on the original class part. Its only structural restriction is that the
numerical individuals remain coded by finitely many original sets.

We use two established model-theoretic facts. A countable $\GBC$-realization
$\mathcal X$ of a countable $M$ has a proper countable extension with the same
sets, obtained by adding a class Cohen generic. Also, the union of an increasing
chain of $\GBC$-realizations of the same $M$ is a $\GBC$-realization. See
Williams, Theorem 1.61(5), the proof of Theorem 1.65, and the class-forcing
discussion preceding them \cite{WilliamsDiss}; the related realization framework is
developed in \cite{WilliamsMin}. (Both are imported, \cref{hset:sf:sub:imported}.)

\begin{lemma}[Different external sizes of the two sorts]\label{hset:sf:lem:twosizes}
Assuming $\Con(\GBC)$, there is a model $(M,\mathcal X)\models\GBC$ with $M$
countable and $\card{\mathcal X}=\aleph_1$, as measured externally.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Start with a countable model $(M,\mathcal X_0)$. By recursion on the external
ordinals $\alpha<\omega_1$, choose countable $\GBC$-realizations of the same $M$
with
\[
 \mathcal X_\alpha\subsetneq\mathcal X_{\alpha+1}.
\]
At a successor use a proper countable class extension. At a nonzero limit put
$\mathcal X_\lambda=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}\mathcal X_\alpha$. This is countable
because $\lambda$ is countable, and is a realization by the union fact.

Now put $\mathcal X=\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_1}\mathcal X_\alpha$. This union again
satisfies $\GBC$. For clarity, elementary comprehension at the union uses only
finitely many class parameters; all occur in some earlier stage, where the required
class already exists. A class function in the union likewise occurs in one stage,
which verifies class replacement for it. A global choice class from the initial
stage remains available. These observations explain why the union argument applies
here.

There are at most $\aleph_1$ classes in this union. The strict successor steps
supply $\aleph_1$ distinct classes, so equality holds.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[No uniform class recovery from set-coded individuals]
\label{hset:sf:thm:canonicalclassnogo}
Assume $\Con(\GBC)$. There is no uniform first-order bi-interpretation between
$\GBC$ and a one-sorted theory $U$ whose forward interpretation, in every
$(M,\mathcal X)\models\GBC$, has domain a definable quotient of a subclass of
$M^n$, for some fixed finite $n$.

In particular, no such bi-interpretation has forward domain the canonical $\No^M$
or $\Oz^M$, or fixed finite tuples or quotients of these, even if its relations
depend on $\mathcal X$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Apply the proposed interpretations to the model of \cref{hset:sf:lem:twosizes}.
The forward domain is countable, because it is a quotient of a subset of $M^n$. The
interpreted $U$-structure is therefore countable.

Every sort of any ordinary first-order interpretation in a countable structure is
again a quotient of a subset of a finite Cartesian power, hence countable. Thus the
inverse interpretation produces a $\GBC$-model with countable class sort. A
bi-interpretation would require that model to be isomorphic to the original
$(M,\mathcal X)$. Its class sort cannot be isomorphic to the uncountable
$\mathcal X$, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

The conclusion tolerates very liberal definitions: the forward domain and relations
may use class parameters, bound class quantifiers, and arbitrary finite tuple
coding. None of those devices enlarges a countable domain. The proof requires no
inaccessible cardinal or transitive model. Its consistency assumption is the
ordinary consistency assumption for the class theory.

The theorem is uniform over all models, as a theory bi-interpretation must be. It
does not claim that two particular countable structures cannot be externally
relabeled by a bijection. Nor does it prohibit a one-sorted presentation in which
some individuals represent genuine class objects and therefore are not canonical
set-sized surreal numbers of the original universe. (Source~13; source 14 has no
counterpart of this theorem.)

\subsection{What diagonalization does and does not show}
\label{hset:sf:sec:diagonal}

There is a useful direct diagonal argument, but its comprehension hypothesis must
be stated. The preceding argument compares theories; this one works inside a given
class universe and clarifies the intended semantics of ``only numerical objects''
(source 14).

\begin{theorem}[No uniform set-code enumeration of classes; sources 13, 14]\label{hset:sf:thm:diagonal}
Work in $\GB$. Let $Q$ be a class of sets, and let $E(x,c)$ be an elementary
formula, possibly with fixed class parameters. There cannot be a coding scheme
satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:classcodes}
 \forall A\ \exists c\in Q\ \forall x\,[x\in A\leftrightarrow E(x,c)],
\end{equation}
where $A$ ranges over all classes of the model and $x$ ranges over its sets. Codes
need not be unique.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Elementary comprehension forms the diagonal class
\[
 \Delta=\{c\in Q:\neg E(c,c)\}.
\]
By \eqref{hset:sf:eq:classcodes}, some $d\in Q$ codes $\Delta$. Substituting $x=d$
gives
\[
 d\in \Delta\iff E(d,d),\qquad d\in \Delta\iff\neg E(d,d),
\]
a contradiction. Injectivity or unique codes were never used.
\end{proof}

Source 14 stated the theorem in $\GBc$; its proof, and source 13's, use only
elementary comprehension, so it is printed over $\GB$. Source 13's form
(``No class-valued universal decoder''): in $\GB$, let $Q$ be a class of sets and
let $E\subseteq Q\times V$ be an available class relation; its sections
$E_c=\{x:E(c,x)\}$ cannot include every class. Its proof forms
$\Delta=\{c\in Q:\neg E(c,c)\}$; if $\Delta=E_d$ for some $d\in Q$, then
$d\in\Delta\leftrightarrow d\notin\Delta$. The two forms are the same theorem, an
available class relation being one given by an elementary formula with a class
parameter.

\begin{corollary}[Ordinary surreal and omnific codes are insufficient; source 14]
\label{hset:sf:cor:diagonal}
No elementary evaluation scheme assigns a surreal number, an omnific integer, a
fixed finite tuple of them, or an arbitrary set-sized package of them to
\emph{every class of sets} in the same $\GB$ universe.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Each proposed code is a set, so apply \cref{hset:sf:thm:diagonal} to the class of
all valid packages. Coding equality by an equivalence relation does not help, since
the proof permits multiple codes.
\end{proof}

For subclasses of $\No$ rather than of $V$, a version with codes in $\No$ has the
identical proof: the diagonal lies within $\No$. Fixed finite numerical tuples can
also be packed into a single surreal, as shown in \cref{hset:sf:lem:pack}. The
essential issue is the presence of a \emph{single elementary evaluator} whose
sections exhaust the available classes. The theorem does not assert that an
externally countable model cannot have its classes enumerated externally; it
asserts that the required internally available evaluation is impossible.
(Source~14.)

This immediately excludes a universal decoder whose evaluation relation is an
elementary formula with class parameters. It does not by itself exclude every
arbitrary formula with bound class quantifiers: predicative comprehension need not
turn that formula into an available class relation. Suppressing this distinction
would overstate the argument. The model-cardinality theorem above avoids this
restriction. (Source~13.)

In $\KM$, full comprehension removes that loophole.

\begin{proposition}[Impredicative coding obstruction]\label{hset:sf:prop:KMdiagonal}
In $\KM$, no formula $\Phi(X,c)$, even with bound class quantifiers and parameters,
can assign every class $X$ some set code $c$, with each code assigned to at most
one class.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Full comprehension forms
\[
 E(c,x)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\exists X\,\bigl(\Phi(X,c)\land x\in X\bigr).
\]
It then forms $\Delta=\{c:\neg E(c,c)\}$. Choose a code $d$ for $\Delta$.
Uniqueness of the class assigned to $d$ gives $E(d,x)\leftrightarrow x\in \Delta$,
and hence the diagonal contradiction.
\end{proof}

Source 14 makes the same point: the same diagonal works in KM for evaluators whose
definitions involve class quantifiers, because KM has the corresponding
comprehension; in GB, the elementary hypothesis is indispensable: one must not form
a class by a formula for which the base theory lacks comprehension.

\paragraph{Definable classes do not evade the diagonal.} Coding formulas and their
finite parameters is not a universal solution either. Such codes naturally describe
definable classes, whereas general $\GBC$-models have additional classes. A single
evaluator definable in the original set language and correct for all formulas of
that language would yield a truth definition forbidden by Tarski. An additional
truth class can exist in stronger class theories, but it does not enumerate all
classes. Particular formulas can be translated, as in
\cref{hset:sf:cor:definability}; a uniform truth evaluator is a different object.
(Source~13.) Source 14: for each fixed formula $\varphi(x,p)$, the class it defines
can be specified by that formula and its parameter. This is a nonuniform
metalinguistic observation. To turn all such descriptions into the objects of a
numerical first-order theory, one needs a uniform predicate which evaluates
arbitrary formula codes. For the whole set universe, that is exactly where Tarski's
undefinability phenomenon enters. A global truth predicate may be added as extra
class structure in a stronger theory, but then it was not obtained merely by
decoding an ordinary number in the original elementary language. Restricting the
complexity of formulas can give partial satisfaction relations and fragmentary
coding; it does not give the forbidden unrestricted \eqref{hset:sf:eq:classcodes}.

\subsection{The precise conclusion for the original question}

For $\ZFC$, numbers with birthdays suffice. For full $\NBG/\GBC$, the usual numbers
of the same set universe do not suffice as the entire individual domain of a
uniform bi-interpretation. Allowing richer numerical operations or a predicate for
Global Choice does not bypass \cref{hset:sf:thm:canonicalclassnogo}.

One can retain full class theory by allowing classes of numbers, as the next section
shows. Alternatively one can change what counts as a number, for example by
admitting certain class-length sign objects. Such objects are not ordinary Conway
surreals: a surreal has an ordinal, set-sized sign domain. Any proposed
generalized-number programme must specify this changed ontology and establish its
own arithmetic. (Source~13; source 14's \cref{hset:sf:prop:branches} and
\cref{hset:sf:q:signobjects} make the same point.)

\section{A faithful theory of numbers and their classes}
\label{hset:sf:sec:classlift}

There is a positive class-theoretic analogue, provided that classes are retained as
objects of a second sort. The number sort can consist entirely of surreals or
entirely of omnific integers. (Source~13.) Source 14 puts it as: the correct
class-level analogue of replacing sets by numerical codes is to replace classes of
sets by \emph{classes of numerical codes}; the important point is that this can be
done reversibly, with both round trips and without a hidden canonical
representative choice.

\subsection{From classes of sets to classes of numbers}

Fix $\Dd=\No$ or $\Oz$, and let $(M,\mathcal X)\models\GBC$. Put
\[
 N=\Dd^M,\qquad \mathcal Y=\{A\in\mathcal X:A\subseteq N\}.
\]
Equip $N$ with its canonical numerical structure and use ordinary class membership
between $N$ and $\mathcal Y$. The expression defining $\mathcal Y$ specifies an
interpreted class sort. It does not assert that the collection of all these classes
is itself a class object.

Use \cref{hset:sf:lem:tuple} to replace each valid triple by a single number. Let
$\UC\subseteq N$ be the resulting definable class of unary set codes, with
equivalence relation $\Eqc$ and represented membership inherited from $\I_{\Dd}$.
Write
\[
 \operatorname{col}:\UC/{\Eqc}\longrightarrow M
\]
for the collapse isomorphism.

A numerical class $B\in\mathcal Y$ represents a class of sets precisely when it is
contained in $\UC$ and is $\Eqc$-saturated:
\[
\begin{split}
 \Sat(B)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ &\forall n\,(n\in_N B\to n\in \UC)\\
 {}\land{}&\forall c,d\in \UC\,\bigl(\Eqc(c,d)\to(c\in_N B\leftrightarrow d\in_N B)\bigr).
\end{split}
\]
Both containment and saturation are part of this first-order condition. The
recovered set sort is $\UC/{\Eqc}$; the recovered class sort consists of the $B$
satisfying $\Sat$. Set--class membership is simply $c\in B$, well-defined on the
quotient.

\begin{lemma}[Definable numerical packing; source 14]\label{hset:sf:lem:pack}
In $\B$ there is a parameter-free definable injection $P:\No^2\to\No$ with a
definable image and definable inverse coordinates on its image. Hence the same is
true for every fixed finite power of $\No$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Here is an explicit set-theoretic sign construction. Replace each sign of the first
input by a two-sign block: plus becomes $++$, minus becomes $+-$. Append a delimiter
block $--$, and then append the similarly encoded second input, with no second
delimiter. Blocks are indexed in ordinal order. For inputs of lengths
$\alpha,\beta$, the result has ordinary ordinal length
\[
 2\cdot_{\mathrm{ord}}\alpha+_{\mathrm{ord}}2+_{\mathrm{ord}}2\cdot_{\mathrm{ord}}\beta.
\]
Every data block starts with plus, and precisely the delimiter block starts with
minus. Decomposing an ordinal into consecutive blocks of length two is unique,
including at limit stages. The delimiter therefore recovers the first input length
and all its signs, and the remaining blocks recover the second input. The
empty-input cases are included.

This is a fixed set-definable construction, as are the validity test for its image
and its two decoders. Translate those formulas through $\I$ and use $\rho$ to
identify interpreted sign functions with original surreal numbers.
\Cref{hset:sf:prop:recovery} makes the resulting functions $\Ls$-definable.
Iterating the construction packs a fixed finite tuple. The ordinal operations in
this proof are explicitly \emph{ordinary} ordinal operations inside the
interpreted set theory; they are not mistaken for the native ring operations.
\end{proof}

Source 14 fixes a packing of triples and lets its class $D$, here $\UC$ (for
$\Dd=\No$), be the packed valid set codes, with $\Eqc$ and $\Memc$ transported to
them; its $\Sat(B)$ is the displayed condition: $B\subseteq\UC$ and
$\forall d,e\in \UC\,[\Eqc(d,e)\to(d\in B\leftrightarrow e\in B)]$. These are
\emph{saturated} classes of codes, meaning that they contain all representatives of
every set they represent. The two packings differ in mechanism: source 13's native
codec (\cref{hset:sf:lem:tuple}) is a block word with marker rows, source 14's a
delimiter construction translated through the interpretation; either works, since
only definability of the injection and of its inverse is used.

\begin{lemma}[Classes are recovered exactly; sources 13, 14]\label{hset:sf:lem:classinverse}
The following maps are definable and inverse:
\[
 X\longmapsto B_X=\{c\in \UC:\operatorname{col}(c)\in X\},
\]
\[
 B\longmapsto X_B=\{x\in M:\exists c\in \UC\,(\operatorname{col}(c)=x\land c\in B)\}.
\]
They establish an isomorphism between the original class sort and the saturated
numerical classes, compatible with the collapse map on sets.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The graph of collapse is an elementary set-theoretic definition. Both displayed
classes therefore exist by elementary comprehension with the given class parameter.
The class $B_X$ is saturated because equivalent codes have the same collapse.

Surjectivity of collapse gives $X_{B_X}=X$. For saturated $B$, if $c\in B_{X_B}$,
some $d\in B$ has the same collapse as $c$; then $\Eqc(c,d)$, and saturation gives
$c\in B$. The converse is immediate, so $B_{X_B}=B$. Membership preservation follows
directly from the definitions.
\end{proof}

No representative is selected from each equivalence class. This is why the
construction requires no additional global choice of graph codes. The source's
Global Choice class is transported as one of its ordinary class objects.
(Source~13.)

Source 14 states the lemma as its \emph{exact class correspondence}, over $\GBc$:
for $(M,\mathcal X)\models\GBc$ with $N=\B^M$ its internally constructed numerical
structure and $\mathcal Y=\{B\in\mathcal X:B\subseteq\No^M\}$ the available
subclasses of the numerical domain, it puts
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:sharp}
 A^\#=\{d\in \UC^M:\exists a\,(\operatorname{col}(d)=a\land a\in A)\}
\end{equation}
for $A\in\mathcal X$, a member of $\mathcal Y$ by elementary comprehension and
saturated, and, for saturated $B\in\mathcal Y$,
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:flat}
 B^\flat=\{a\in M:\exists d\in B\,\operatorname{col}(d)=a\}.
\end{equation}
These definitions are relative to $M$; for nonstandard models they use its
internally defined collapse relation. So $A^\#=B_A$ and $B^\flat=X_B$.

\begin{theorem}[Exact class correspondence; source 14]\label{hset:sf:thm:classcorr}
The maps $A\mapsto A^\#$ and $B\mapsto B^\flat$ are mutually inverse between
$\mathcal X$ and the saturated members of $\mathcal Y$. They preserve membership in
the recovered set sort. In particular,
\[
 (A^\#)^\flat=A,\qquad(B^\flat)^\#=B.
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Second proof (source 14)]
Every set $a$ has at least one valid code, and all of its valid codes are
equivalent. Therefore a set belongs to $(A^\#)^\flat$ exactly when it belongs to
$A$.

If a valid code $d$ belongs to $(B^\flat)^\#$, then some $e\in B$ has the same root
collapse. Hence $\Eqc(d,e)$, and saturation gives $d\in B$. The converse is
immediate. The membership assertion is the equivalence
\[
 a\in A\iff d\in A^\#\qquad\text{whenever }\operatorname{col}(d)=a.
\]
All classes used are obtained by elementary formulas with existing class
parameters. No class choice or global selection of a single code has been used.
\end{proof}

\subsection{An explicit two-sorted axiom system}

The language has the numerical symbols $\Lb$, a class sort, and a relation
$n\in_N A$ between numbers and classes. It has no primitive set-membership relation
between numbers.

\begin{definition}\label{hset:sf:def:classaxioms}
The theory $\Tth^{\mathrm{cl}}_{\Dd}$ consists of:
\begin{enumerate}
\item the axioms of $\Tth_{\Dd}$ on the number sort;
\item class extensionality:
\[
 \forall A\,\forall B\,\bigl[\forall n\,(n\in_N A\leftrightarrow n\in_N B)\to A=B\bigr];
\]
\item elementary numerical comprehension, namely
\[
 \exists A\,\forall n\,(n\in_N A\leftrightarrow\varphi(n,\bar p,\bar B))
\]
for each formula $\varphi$ with number quantifiers, number parameters, and class
parameters, but no bound class quantifiers;
\item every axiom of $\GBC$, translated by taking $\UC/{\Eqc}$ as the set sort and
the saturated numerical classes as the class sort.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

In the final group, equality of represented sets is $\Eqc$; membership of
represented sets is the graph-decoding relation; and membership in a represented
class is $\in_N$. Class quantifiers are relativized to $\Sat$. In particular, this
group includes the translations of class replacement and Global Choice. Elementary
comprehension alone must not be substituted for those requirements.

Every clause is a first-order sentence or a recursive first-order scheme in the
two-sorted language. The unary codec, its image, and the comparison formulas are
native definitions, whose required properties follow from $\Tth_{\Dd}$.

\subsection{Recovering the entire numerical class sort}

One must still check a second class round trip. The saturated subclasses of $\UC$
recover $\mathcal X$, but the numerical structure originally had \emph{all}
available subclasses $B\subseteq N$, not only saturated ones. (Source~14.)

\begin{theorem}[The class lift; sources 13, 14]\label{hset:sf:thm:classlift}
For $\Dd=\No$ and $\Dd=\Oz$,
\[
 \Tth^{\mathrm{cl}}_{\Dd}\bi\GBC.
\]
The forward interpretation uses the canonical numbers of the set universe together
with its available classes of those numbers.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
From a $\GBC$-model, construct $(N,\mathcal Y)$ as above. The number sort satisfies
$\Tth_{\Dd}$. Class extensionality and numerical comprehension are inherited from
the original model by translating the numerical symbols to their elementary set
definitions. By \cref{hset:sf:lem:classinverse}, the saturated-code interpretation
is isomorphic to the whole original $\GBC$-model, so every translated class axiom
holds. This proves one interpretation and its source-side comparison.

Conversely, from a model $(N,\mathcal Y)$ of $\Tth^{\mathrm{cl}}_{\Dd}$, take its
interpreted $\GBC$-model. The numerical comparison $\Cmp_{\Dd}$ gives the required
isomorphism between $N$ and that model's canonical number structure. It remains to
recover \emph{every} original numerical class.

For $A\in\mathcal Y$, use elementary numerical comprehension to form
\[
 B_A=\{c\in \UC:\exists n\,(n\in_N A\land \Cmp_{\Dd}(n,c))\}.
\]
Here $\Cmp_{\Dd}$ has been rewritten using the unary codec. The class $B_A$ is
saturated and contains only codes for reconstructed numbers. It is therefore a class
of numbers in the reconstructed class theory. Conversely, from any such $B$, form
\[
 A_B=\{n\in N:\exists c\in \UC\,(\Cmp_{\Dd}(n,c)\land c\in_N B)\}.
\]
The totality, surjectivity, injectivity, and equivalence-invariance of
$\Cmp_{\Dd}$ show that these maps are inverse. Class extensionality turns equality
of memberships into equality of classes. Together with $\Cmp_{\Dd}$, they preserve
numerical operations and number--class membership. This supplies the second full
definable isomorphism.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Source 14's second round trip, and its form of the theorem.} For an
arbitrary $B\subseteq N$, source 14 uses the numerical isomorphism $\rho$ to form its
image among the surreals of $\I(N)$; its corresponding class of interpreted sets is
represented by
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:Bhat}
 \widehat B=\{d\in \UC:\exists x\in B\,\rho(x,d)\},
\end{equation}
with $\rho(x,d)$ read after unpacking $d$. This is a saturated class in
$\mathcal Y$, and in the recovered set universe it is a class of surreal sign
functions. Pulling it back along $\rho$ returns exactly $B$:
\[
 x\in B\iff\exists d\in\widehat B\,\rho(x,d).
\]
Existence follows from the totality of $\rho$; uniqueness of the original $x$
follows from its injectivity. Thus arbitrary numerical classes, not just sets and
saturated code classes, are recovered. ($\widehat B$ is source 13's $B_A$ with
$A=B$.)

\begin{theorem}[Class-level bi-interpretation; source 14]\label{hset:sf:thm:classbi}
There are fixed first-order two-sorted interpretations giving
\[
 (M,\mathcal X)\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad(\B^M,\mathcal Y)
\]
with definable inverse round trips for both the individual and class sorts. The
construction is valid over $\GBc$; it carries Global Choice, full KM comprehension,
and additional specified class principles to their exact translated counterparts.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Second proof (source 14)]
Interpret numerical individuals in $(M,\mathcal X)$ by $\J$, and numerical classes
by those class objects contained in the numerical domain. In the opposite direction
use $\UC/{\Eqc}$ for sets, $\Memc$ for set membership, and saturated numerical
classes for classes of sets. The first round trip is $\operatorname{col}$ on
individuals and \eqref{hset:sf:eq:sharp}--\eqref{hset:sf:eq:flat} on classes.

The second is $\rho$ on individuals and \eqref{hset:sf:eq:Bhat} on arbitrary
numerical classes. The preceding proofs establish totality, equality, surjectivity,
and preservation of the membership relations. Class extensionality turns agreement
of members into equality of class objects. Every formula used has only individual
quantifiers apart from its free class parameters, so $\GBc$ comprehension suffices
for all required class constructions.

For an additional axiom or scheme, translate it through these fixed
interpretations. The two definable isomorphisms identify its round-trip translation
with the original. This proves exact preservation rather than an informal analogy.
\end{proof}

A first-order axiomatization of the numerical class theory is obtained by the same
recipe as \cref{hset:sf:lem:rt}, now with two sorts: include interpretation
validity, the translated class-theory axioms, and the finite individual-and-class
round-trip coherence conditions. Call the GBC version $\mathsf{SAC}_{\GBC}$. It is
bi-interpretable with NBG under our convention, and it has a finite axiom basis
because NBG does (\cref{hset:sf:lem:finite}). There is no conflict with
\cref{hset:sf:sub:reflection}: it is not a one-sorted theory uniformly interpreted
in $\ZFC$; it includes a class sort. (Source~14.) [Merge: $\mathsf{SAC}_{\GBC}$ and
$\Tth^{\mathrm{cl}}_{\No}$ are bi-interpretable, both being bi-interpretable with
$\GBC$; $\Tth^{\mathrm{cl}}_{\No}$ as displayed (with the full scheme of $\Tth_{\No}$
on the number sort) is equivalent to a finite theory by \cref{hset:sf:lem:finite}.]

The omnific version is obtained in the same way. Finite tuples of omnific integers
are themselves definably packable into one omnific integer after the established
bi-interpretation: first pack in the surreal sort, then apply a fixed definable
injection $\No\to\Oz$. One example is
\[
 x\longmapsto\omega^{\,1+x/\sqrt{1+x^2}},
\]
whose exponent lies strictly between $0$ and $2$. The image consists of
positive-exponent monomials, and injectivity follows from strict monotonicity. The
omega-map and inverse on its image are available in the recovered set structure; no
assertion of pure-ring definability is being made. Alternatively one may simply
retain finite tuples in the interpretation, which avoids this optional packing step.
(Source~14; source 13's native codec \cref{hset:sf:lem:tuple} works in $\Oz$
directly.)

\paragraph{The class collection is genuine additional information (source 14).}
The transformation does not replace $\mathcal X$ by all external subclasses. It
transports the particular classes the model actually has. Distinct class
realizations of the same $M$ cannot collapse to the same $\mathcal Y$:
\cref{hset:sf:thm:classcorr} would recover the same $\mathcal X$ from it. More
precisely, for two $\GBc$ realizations over one fixed $M$,
\begin{equation}\label{hset:sf:eq:inclusion}
 \mathcal X_1\subseteq\mathcal X_2\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathcal Y_1\subseteq\mathcal Y_2.
\end{equation}
The forward direction is restriction. For the reverse direction, apply
